# Sample Manuscript Page: On the Virtues of Rosemary

It is well known among the ancient doctors that the herb <term>Rosemary</term> <note>A woody, perennial herb (Salvia rosmarinus) used since antiquity for both culinary and medicinal purposes.</note> possesses a warming quality that aids the memory. <margin>This is also confirmed by Galen</margin> When the leaves are boiled in water, they produce a decoction that strengthens the head and clears the mind.

### The Preparation of the Oil

One must take the fresh sprigs and place them in a <term>Cucurbit</term> <note>A gourd-shaped chemical vessel used in distillation.</note> for the space of three days. As the Latin masters wrote, *the power is in the oil, not the dross* <note>original: "potentia in oleo est, non in recrementis"</note>. 

| Ingredient | Quantity | Method |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Rosemary sprigs | Two handfuls | Bruised |
| Spring water | One quart | Filtered |
| <unclear>White Wine</unclear> | Half a pint | <gloss>Use only the best Rhine wine</gloss> |

One must be careful to keep the fire at a <insert>gentle</insert> heat, lest the spirit of the plant be lost to the air. If the mixture should <unclear>boil over</unclear>, the virtue is diminished. 

->**Concerning the Preservation of Sight**<-

If the eyes are clouded, take the smoke of the burning wood and let it pass over the face. This will <gloss>clear the humors</gloss> and restore the light to the pupils. <margin>Take care not to singe the eyebrows.</margin>

# Concerning the Nature of the Elements
## A Treatise on Alchemical Principles

In the pursuit of the Great Work, one must first understand that all matter is composed of the four primary roots. As the ancients said, the soul of the world is a living fire <note>original: "anima mundi est ignis vivens"</note> that permeates all things. 

->The Correspondence of Metals<-

| Metal | Planetary Ruler | Quality |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Gold | The Sun | Hot and Dry |
| Silver | The Moon | Cold and Moist |
| Iron | Mars | Dry and Harsh |

We observe that the **Spirit** <insert>or the volatile essence</insert> is often trapped within the *prima materia* <note>original: "prima materia" — the first matter or the primitive formless base of all things</note>. To release it, one must apply a gentle heat, similar to that of a brooding hen.

<margin>Note the importance of the <term>Athanor</term> <note>A furnace used in alchemy designed to maintain a uniform and constant heat over long periods</note>.</margin>

If the vessel is not sealed with the <term>Hermetic seal</term> <note>An airtight seal made by fusing glass or using a specialized clay; the origin of the phrase "hermetically sealed"</note>, the vital breath will escape into the air <gloss>this refers to the evaporation of the spirit during distillation</gloss>. 

### On the Extraction of Salt

One must be careful when handling the <unclear>corrosive</unclear> waters of the second stage. As it is written in the Greek texts, "One nature rejoices in another" <note>original: "Η φύσις τῇ φύσει τέρπεται" (Hē physis tē physei terpetai). This is a famous alchemical aphorism attributed to Pseudo-Democritus</note>.

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental alchemical relationship between the four elements, planetary influences on metals, and the necessity of maintaining proper heat and seals during the distillation of the "spirit."</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, four elements, planetary metals, Athanor, Hermetic seal, distillation, anima mundi</keywords>

## FROM
# THE PRINTER.

**Messrs. des Marets, the brothers**, <note>Henry and Samuel des Marets, influential theologians and pastors who opposed Labadie.</note> Pastors in the **Walloon Churches** <note>French-speaking Protestant churches in the Netherlands, primarily composed of refugees from the Southern Netherlands and France.</note> of **Delft** and **The Hague**, were urgently requested by their friends six weeks ago to justify the proceedings of the **Synods** <note>Formal assemblies of church leaders or "classes" convened to decide on matters of doctrine and discipline.</note> against **Mr. de Labadie**, to lift the veil from his **mystery of iniquity**, <note>original: "mystere d'iniquité" — a biblical allusion to 2 Thessalonians 2:7, suggesting a hidden or spiritual evil.</note> to uncover his hypocrisy, and above all to refute the **Manifesto** of his schism, which bears the title *The Christian Declaration*, etc., printed in The Hague long before the last Walloon Synod held in **Dordrecht** had pronounced his **deposition**. <note>A formal removal of a minister from their religious office.</note> Although this hateful subject should have excused them from such an unpleasant task—allowing them to apply their time and their studies to works more worthy of their vigilance and their care—nonetheless, misfortune having willed that a good number of people of

# CURIOUS HISTORY
## of the Life, Conduct, and
## true sentiments of
# Mr. JEAN DE LABADIE.

What a learned Father of the Early Church <note>Likely a reference to St. Jerome or another patristic writer who commented on the nature of various nations.</note> once said of France—*that it was in a position to supply the world with men of eloquence and courage*—could be said in some way of our United Provinces <note>The Dutch Republic (the Netherlands).</note>: that they have always had the honor of seeing illustrious men shine within their borders, who in eloquence and strength never yielded to those of ancient Rome, and much less to those of modern France. Without it being necessary to mark famous names on paper—which are so well engraved on marble that time will have difficulty erasing them—we will content ourselves with painting the portrait of a man of reputation and great notoriety <note>The original French "grand bruit" literally means "great noise," suggesting someone who was the subject of much gossip or public debate.</note> who, although a native of France, nevertheless had the kindness to come to us to be naturalized in as many ways as he thought possible. This is Mr. Jean de Labadie, so well known in France and in these Provinces that it seems almost useless to provide a vivid por- <note>The catchword "ture," on the original page completes the French word "peinture," meaning portrait or painting.</note>

<summary>The text concludes a warning against false prophets and begins a biography of Jean de Labadie, a French-born religious figure who became a prominent and controversial naturalized citizen of the Netherlands.</summary>

We will be easily forgiven, as we hope, if we say nothing of his air, his stature, his appearance, or his physiognomy—all these external things being so subject to deceiving and lying that it is not possible to draw an assured conclusion of integrity from them. For it is well known that there are heads that can be dismantled <note>A 17th-century idiom suggesting people who can change their expression or persona at will.</note>, faces that wear makeup, appearances that are faked, and people so symbolic in this world that at the very moment they show the simplicity of doves in their eyes, they carry the prudence of serpents in the depths of their hearts. <note>A reference to Matthew 10:16: "Behold, I send you forth as sheep in the midst of wolves: be ye therefore wise as serpents, and harmless as doves."</note> It is enough to say that our Mr. de Labadie has a very engaging air, attractive speech, honeyed discourse, and a manner so suited to winning the favor of the weaker sex <note>A common, though patronizing, historical reference to women.</note>, that just as he knows that the devil, to seduce man and make him fall into rebellion, attacked the woman, whom he easily engaged in his party; so too he has the skill to make advantageous use of these same weapons, by addressing himself to women whom he cajoles as tenderly as possible for a good end, that is to say

This is a skill which few or none of our Doctors <note>Learned theologians or scholars of the church.</note> have thought of, except perhaps some wicked Heretics who have done nothing but trouble the Church and who are not worth the honor of being named; but it is a skill which he uses so admirably that it has been noted that he pronounces, with an air much more sweet and touching, the term *My Sister* than that of *My Brother*.

This does not prevent him from winning the affections of the stronger sex with almost as much ease, and from occasionally so well-preparing their inclinations to his advantage that some would declare themselves as frankly his martyr as that of JESUS CHRIST; because most of these good people whom he engages in his party make no distinction between the Master and the Minister, and they judge that their interests being merged, the zeal they show for the one can conveniently be extended to the other. If one were curious to know who taught this great man this fine method, it would be easy to satisfy them: it is because he had the good fortune to be instructed at the feet of the Gamaliels <note>A reference to Gamaliel, a celebrated doctor of Jewish Law and teacher of St. Paul. Here, it refers to influential teachers.</note> of the Order of Loyola <note>The Jesuits, founded by Ignatius of Loyola. The author implies Labadie's persuasive tactics were learned during his time as a Jesuit.</note>, who although they support doctrines that are not received in our Churches and which Mr. de Labadie has abandoned, nevertheless have a very particular skill for infil-

to insinuate themselves into minds and to engage them in their methods; all the evil these good Fathers do is constantly done among them **In the name of the Lord** <note>original: "In nomine Domini"</note>, and a Prince Colonna, Vice-Roy of Sicily, was right to say once to the Jesuit Alfonso, **"Forgive me, my Fathers of Jesus, you others have your minds in Heaven, your hands in the world, and your soul to all the devils,"** <note>original: "Perdonate mi Padri di Jesu, V'altri havete la mente nel cielo, gli mani nel mondo, l'anima al tutti diavoli"</note>, that is to say, *Forgive me my Fathers of Jesus, you others have your spirit in Heaven, your hand in the world, and your soul to all the devils*. Nevertheless, it is a great stain on the life of Mr. de Labadie, and one which has given a very bad tint to all his actions: that in his tender youth at the age of seventeen, he entered the pernicious School of these abominable Masters; for the short time he was there, since it was only for some fifteen years, he sucked in the poison so well that ever since, in all his conduct—which one must say with regret—he has never appeared perfectly "de-Jesuitized."

We would engage in too extensive a discourse if we were to follow the thread of this part of his life, which he spent in the communion of those of Rome <note>Meaning the Roman Catholic Church.</note>, to describe its strange Romance and untangle all its intrigues; we prefer to reserve this detail for a more favorable occasion, and as there is no one who should know Mr. de Labadie better than himself,

# Curious History
<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Curious History</header>
<meta>catchword: consolation</meta>

...if he had been satisfied in Geneva with the Church and the Public—or rather, if the Public and the Church had received from him the satisfaction that was to be expected—he surely would not have left. For reason and conscience should have obligated him not to abandon such a large and significant flock to follow the calling of a small <term>Walloon Church<note>French-speaking Protestant churches in the Dutch Republic, primarily serving refugees from the Spanish Netherlands and France.</note></term> in Middelburg, had he not felt, on one hand, that his usual extravagances, his unbearable pride, and his bold ventures against both his colleagues and his sovereign Magistrate had made him an object of loathing. On the other hand, he hoped that his schismatic design would succeed with greater results in these Provinces, fueled by the foolish imagination he had regarding the incompetence of the Ministers serving the Walloon Churches of this country, compared to his own high and pastoral capacity.

Thus, as soon as he set foot in the country, it was immediately seen that his first steps were irregular, making it clearly known that his entire goal and all his designs tended only toward tearing things apart. Instead of going straight to <term>Zeeland<note>A western province of the Netherlands where Middelburg is located.</note></term> to the Church that had called him and which awaited him impatiently, he took the path of a schoolboy playing truant—though he believed he was taking the path of the great Masters and notable Inspectors. He wished to see the principal cities of the country in the two Provinces of Holland and Utrecht, and to give this fine conso-

<column-break/>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>of Jean de Labadie.</header>
<sig>A 7</sig>
<meta>catchword: as</meta>

-lation to the people: letting himself be heard in the most famous Churches of our language. But what do we think was his design in these sermons, delivered with such eagerness? It was to work principally toward these three things:

First, to discredit the Pastors in the eyes of their flocks, whom he accused of negligence and of failing to put their hands to the Reformation, saying plainly from the pulpit that *if he were in their place, he would act in quite another way*, just as Absalom said at his father’s Court: *O, that I were made judge in the land!* <note>original: "O que ne m'établit-on pour Juge au Pays"</note>, 2 Sam. 15:4.

Secondly, he worked to make the wealthiest and most significant members of the Churches hateful to the simple commoners, inveighing with very heated rhetoric against the luxury of their furniture, their ornaments, and their clothes. This "good man" knew well that nothing more greatly flatters the ears of the common people, and that there is nothing the less well-off hear more willingly, than censures against those whom they cannot equal in such matters.

Thirdly, his goal was to preach the <term>Fifth Monarchy<note>A radical 17th-century belief that the four biblical world empires (Babylon, Persia, Greece, Rome) were ending, to be replaced by the thousand-year reign of Christ.</note></term> of the <term>Chiliasts<note>Millenarians; those believing in a literal 1,000-year reign of Christ on Earth.</note></term>, as a subject very well suited to raising the standard of rebellion, making himself a party leader, and soon marching at the head of a troop of mutineers to rebuild the ruins of the <term>Kingdom of Münster<note>A reference to the 1534-1535 Anabaptist rebellion in Münster, Germany, which ended in a violent siege and became a historical warning against religious radicalism.</note></term> and rouse the people against their legitimate Magistrates. What is more, as...

# A Curious History
<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>A Curious History</header>
<meta>catchword: appear</meta>

...as if he had come to give the Law to the whole world and receive it from no one, hardly had he entered his Church than he began not only to rebel against his colleagues, but even to mutiny against the entire body of those churches in the country. This he did through his repeated refusal to sign the public <term>Confession<note>A formal statement of religious belief; in this context, likely the Belgic Confession used by Reformed churches.</note></term>, to approve the regulations, and to submit to their resolutions. He sought everywhere for opportunities to quibble, to contradict, and to break away. All the poisoned writings, full of gall, which he has published from time to time against the <term>Synods<note>Regional or national meetings of church leaders and elders.</note></term>, prove this with such clarity and evidence that posterity will be astonished that the outbursts and extravagances of this turbulent man were tolerated for so long without applying the remedy of a vigorous discipline.

Not only did he open the path to schism through the irregularity of his conduct—even before any formal censure had been brought against him—going so far as to hold secret <term>conventicles<note>Illegal or unauthorized religious meetings held outside the established church.</note></term> in various places without the knowledge of the Pastors and <term>Consistories<note>The local governing body of a Reformed church, consisting of the minister and elders.</note></term> established there, a proceeding highly condemned by all church laws, both old and new. But also his nature, if considered carefully, provides the two sure characteristics of a troublemaker and a schismatic, which are *Pride* and *Enthusiasm* <note>In the 17th century, "Enthusiasm" was a derogatory term for someone claiming direct, private divine inspiration over established scripture or church authority.</note>. Never did a man in the world make 

<column-break/>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>of Mr. Jean de Labadie.</header>
<meta>catchword: me</meta>

appear more pride under the guise of a pretended humility; his intolerable arrogance, like that of another <term>Diogenes<note>A Greek philosopher who lived in a barrel and practiced extreme poverty; the author suggests Labadie uses his "poverty" to mock others.</note></term>, was visible through the holes of his cloak. All the seditious pamphlets he has published are nothing but eulogies in his own praise. In them, he treats all others as "little boys" and looks down on them from on high, and he places himself everywhere at such an inestimable value that he would be careful not to trade places with anyone else without expecting a great deal in return. He usually gives himself the title of *Pastor* without specifying the name of the Church where he serves, as if he intended to be considered in the Church in the same rank as the brothers of Kings in France, to whom one gives purely and simply the title of *Monsieur* <note>In the French court, "Monsieur" was the official title for the King's eldest brother, requiring no further name.</note>. Or perhaps, as if his conscience reproached him that he was not performing the function of a true Pastor in the Church, he was ashamed to name a Church when giving himself the title and quality of Pastor.

He has taken it upon himself to publish *Pastoral Letters* for the Reformation of the Churches, as if he were the Bishop or the <term>Metropolitan<note>A high-ranking bishop with authority over other bishops.</note></term> of the entire country, and as if the others whom God has honored with the holy Ministry there were under him as mere village curates. He titled the first sermons he gave at Middelburg *The Apostolic Arrival*, and he has the audacity to compare himself there with the Apostle St. Paul, even claiming to have been cut from the same...

<summary>This page characterizes Jean de Labadie as a deeply arrogant and rebellious figure who defied church authority and local governance immediately upon his arrival in the Netherlands. The author accuses him of "Enthusiasm" and false humility, noting his refusal to affiliate his title with any specific church, as if he held universal authority.</summary>

...measures me, and claims to be of the same stature as him. He wants his example to serve as a rule for all others, and it is a fact that the most arrogant of the Pharisees always spoke of himself and his great holiness with much more modesty and restraint than he does. And although he seems to have chosen the School of humility by formerly joining with <term>Jansenius<note>Cornelius Jansen, a bishop whose teachings on grace and human frailty sparked the Jansenist movement, which emphasized deep piety and was often at odds with the Jesuits.</note></term> on the subject of grace, it is nevertheless certain that he has always harbored, and still harbors in his breast, all the pride of Pelagius, Caelestius, and Julian <note>Pelagius and his followers were ancient heretics who argued that humans could achieve salvation through their own will without divine grace—the theological opposite of Jansenism.</note>. Indeed, Jansenism in his case has functioned much like the God of the Stoic Planetiades, who, according to Plutarch’s report, drove out vices through the front door only to receive them back through the rear. He has even reached that criminal presumption of **Impeccability** <note>original: "Impeccance"; the belief that a person can reach a state where they are no longer capable of sinning.</note> and of an entire perfection within this state of imperfection and infirmity. He undoubtedly claims this for himself as much as he has preached it and pressed it upon others, even to the point of putting a noose around their necks and driving them to despair, as could be shown by certain fatal examples that occurred under his very eyes in the city where he exercised his Pastorate.

Although no man ever spoke or wrote more poorly than he—yes, one who understood the Holy Scriptures less—he nevertheless wants his long and tedious—

<column-break/>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>of Mr. Jean de Labadie.</header>
<meta>catchword: with</meta>

—sermons (in which he relies much more on his lungs and his bile than on his reasoning and his brain) to be taken for proofs of his profound erudition and as highly finished works. And he would be very pleased if one took the "flow of his mouth"—or to put it better, his salivation, caused by his raw **mercury** <note>In 17th-century medicine, "mercury" was used as a treatment, but here it likely refers to a "mercurial" or volatile and unstable temperament.</note>—for a torrent of eloquence. The swelling of his pride is further noted by the fact that although he was but a simple Pastor, and in that capacity had only sheep to govern, he nonetheless set himself up, without a vocation and without any legitimate title, as a Doctor and Professor. He did this to gain disciples in Theology, whom he wished to prepare for the Holy Ministry by infatuating them with his mad fantasies and instilling his prejudices into them. All of these are incontestable proofs of his self-love, his eccentricity, and his pride.

As for ***Enthusiasm*** <note>In this period, "Enthusiasm" was a derogatory term for those claiming direct, private revelations from God that bypassed Church tradition or scripture.</note>, he has been so possessed by it since the very time he was a Jesuit, Jansenist, <term>Illuminé<note>A member of a group claiming special spiritual "illumination" or direct sight of the divine.</note></term>, Carmelite, and <term>Adamite<note>A radical sect that sought to return to the primal innocence of Adam, sometimes practicing religious nudity.</note></term>, without ever letting it go, that one will hardly find among the most visionary **Quakers** anyone who boasts so much of *spirituality*, *presence*, *inspiration*, *irradiation*, and the *Anointing of the Holy Spirit*. What is very remarkable—and we must mention it in passing—is that even during the time he was in the Society of the Jesuits, he boasted of having private conversations...

And indeed, the same spirit that had pushed him into the Jesuit order, and which possessed him so absolutely under that black habit, in the most ardent heat of his **Loyolism** <note>Devotion to the principles of Ignatius of Loyola, the founder of the Jesuits.</note>, and among the terrifying shadows of the *Chamber of Meditations*, was that very same spirit which birthed in him a disgust for it. It drew him out after fifteen years to employ him in "higher and more significant things," which he describes in his declaration in a somewhat monkish jargon and with very inflated terms. He did this with such skill that, while speaking very favorably of his assignments in Paris and Picardy after having "de-Jesuitized" himself and joined the party of the **Jansenists** <note>A Catholic theological movement that emphasized original sin and divine grace, often in conflict with the Jesuits.</note> (because those places are very far from Montauban, where people preferred to believe him rather than go there to see for themselves), he says nothing at all about what happened in Bordeaux, Bazas, Toulouse, and at the Hermitage of Graville. Just so, he has now set himself up here as the *Herald* of the reign of Jesus Christ and of the **Fifth Monarchy** <note>A millenarian belief that a fifth world empire, led by Christ, was about to begin.</note> through the outbursts of the most arrant **Quakerism** <note>Used here as a derogatory label for radical spiritualism that relies on "inner light" rather than established church authority.</note> that was ever found.

& the articles of our Synods speak of them enough.

Indeed, this man who had caused so many irregularities in all the Churches where he served, could only be received into the service of that of Middelburg by underhanded means. He entered through the very door of irregularity, a fatal omen for a man who was destined to disturb the peace of the Churches, that he could not obtain a <term>calling<note>The formal process of inviting and appointing a minister to a specific congregation.</note></term> in this country without overturning the Ecclesiastical orders; this is what Article 18 of the Synod held at Middelburg in the month of May of the year 1666 marks in very express terms, since it explains itself in these words: *the Church of Middelburg having called our very dear Brother Mr. Jean de Labadie, Minister of the Church of Geneva, to serve it as Pastor in the place of our late <note>original: "feu."</note> very honored brother Mr. Jean le Long, and having requested the approval of the Synod, this company, notwithstanding that it has noticed some irregularities that clash with our regulations, nevertheless etc.* As God is a God of order, and as He blesses those callings where, after the invocation of His name, one proceeds with order and observes the regulations, so too where this was not observed, there was reason to fear that His curse would fall upon this poor Church, which in fact saw itself afterwards divided into horrible factions, and which received such deep wounds that time will have difficulty healing them.

Even in this Synod of Middelburg, in Article 25, it was found appropriate—on the occasion of what had passed in this irregular calling of Labadie—to draw up this article to prevent the Churches from striking against a similar reef in the future: *To avoid several irregularities in the callings of Pastors, this assembly seriously recommends the sixth articles of the Synod of Utrecht of the year 1635 and the 26th of the Synod held at The Hague in April of the year 1636.* But the sequel will make known how this newcomer satisfied them; for in the Classis that was to regulate all these things, he maneuvered <note>original: "cabala."</note> so well, and chose his time so precisely, that they did nothing there but approve his calling and nothing more.

This Classis, convened in Amsterdam primarily on his account, did not have the honor of seeing his face, as he did not appear there under the pretext of illness or a migraine. He did, however, deign to present himself at the following Synod in Amsterdam in May 1667, where he first requested, by way of instruction, to be relieved from the article of the Synod of Heusden, claiming that he had not been legitimately summoned at that time. But the Synod judged by its 23rd article that the Synod of Heusden had been right to summon him to receive the necessary clarifications. It was in this same Synod of Amsterdam, as appears by Article 25, that he refused to sign the Confession of Faith, testifying that he had not yet read it—even though, having promised the Classis of Vlissingen <note>original: "Flessingue"</note> to sign it, he led them to believe he had already read it, or at least that he should have done so before accepting the <term>vocation<note>A formal call to serve as a minister in a specific church.</note></term> of Middelburg, as the article states. He then feigned being ill, and thus incapable of reading it *with all the mental application* which he said he required to judge certain expressions; yet it is certain that at that very time he was employing all his mental application in conferring with Serarius, Comenius, Galenus, and other meddling spirits. <note>Peter Serrarius, Jan Amos Comenius, and Galenus Abrahamsz were prominent 17th-century intellectuals and religious radicals known for their millenarian or mystical views.</note> This proceeding revealed, first of all, that he was seeking

Whereupon the Synod, through a very particular indulgence, still had the kindness to allow him to present himself at the next Synod to sign the said French Confession and explain it using the Latin version for those expressions that seemed a bit harsh to him, granting him at the same time permission to discuss his scruples with the Synodal Churches. ...simple expressions; his designs were much more vast and more glorious, and he only lacked a favorable occasion to raise the Standard and to declare war on our Churches. To better seek a quarrel and a pretext to break away, he did not appear at the following Synod held in Leiden in September of the year 1667. However, it was there that this spirit of pride—unable to bear that the <term>Classis<note>A regional governing body in the Reformed Church, composed of ministers and elders.</note></term> of Amsterdam and the previous Synod held in the same city had confirmed by article that the Synod of Heusden had valid reasons for summoning him—so much did this alleged affront weigh on his heart—demanded again through a petition of grievance from his Church, which the Deputies read, that both rulings be reformed. 

But this was something that all the justice in the world could not grant him. Thus, the Synod judged by a specific article that everything should remain as it was. It declared that the Church of Middelburg had been wrong to prevent Labadie from attending the Synod, where his presence was so necessary, and that he, Labadie, should be obliged, as well as all other Pastors who shall be received from now on, to sign the Confession of the Walloon Churches. This was the same Confession he had criticized in a private letter, claiming it contained several expressions, applications, and explanations that were improper and ill-suited to a public Confession of Faith, and that it was full of errors and erroneous propositions contrary to the Scriptures and to what

*1. regarding discipline, by teaching a doctrine not accepted among us, by having books printed without the Synod's consent, and by refusing, on his own authority and in the middle of a full assembly, to give the bread and wine of the Holy <term>Supper<note>The Eucharist or Communion service.</note></term> to Mr. Henry du Moulin, his colleague; 2. regarding his rebellion, which he showed by the fact that, despite the express prohibition several times made to him, he did not stop peddling his opinion concerning the glorious reign of Jesus Christ on earth <note>This refers to Labadie's "Chiliasm" or millenarianism—the belief in a literal 1,000-year kingdom of Christ on earth before the final judgment, which was considered heterodox by many Reformed authorities.</note>, and had an entire book printed on it, regardless of the previous Synod’s prohibition, even boasting several times in public that he would sooner have his hand cut off than be forced to do what had been ordered; 3. regarding the atrocious insults and invectives against our Synods and the Pastors who compose them; nevertheless, the assembly grants him this grace, as much in consideration of his gifts (which it admits are acceptable if well employed) as in consideration of his Church, to halt the course of justice and not proceed to his suspension, which it judged*

he claimed he had deserved; however, ordering a <term>Classis<note>A regional governing body in the Reformed Church, composed of ministers and elders from several local churches.</note></term> at Vlissingen for the tenth of the following month, with the intention of dealing with him there, in accordance with the intentions of the Synod of Leiden.

Before the holding of this Classis, the Synod had ordered that some Deputies should arrive ahead of the scheduled date and go to Middelburg to prepare the business and try to bring Labadie back to his duty by every gentle means they could devise. But these Deputies worked in vain, for they spoke to a deaf man and tried fruitlessly to touch a hardened and inflexible heart. They withdrew on the appointed day to Vlissingen, where the Classis was convened; but the one who did not present himself was Labadie. Instead, by a letter addressed not to the Classis but to the Synodal Church, he wrote that his "infirmity" exempted him from traveling to Vlissingen, even though he was not truly sick—indeed, he had shown himself only too vigorous and healthy in his preaching and his solicitations. Consequently, to conduct business, the Classis was forced, whether they liked it or not, to move to Middelburg to meet Labadie, since he would not move to Vlissingen to meet them. Despite all these concessions being made to him, contrary to order and custom, he would never consent to be separated from his <term>Consistory<note>The local governing council of a single congregation, made up of the minister and elders.</note></term> to appear separately before the Classis, and

and one after the other, as the Synod of Leiden had ordered. But as soon as the Moderator began to tell him that he had something to represent to him in private, he rose quickly, saying with a look that marked a signal contempt for the assembly: *That he did not recognize it as a Classis.* Even despite the efforts that the Flemish Consistory of the Church of Middelburg made through its Deputies to engage him to satisfy the intentions of the Classis and the Synod—representing to him that it was <note>original: "Res judicata"</note> *a matter already judged*, from which, according to the rules, there was no appeal—he could not be moved. He said loudly to the delegated Flemish Pastors, *That he would not preach on the matter of the Kingdom except when his text led him to it*; adding, *that if the Synods had such great authority over the Churches, they would be Popes.* Thus, the Classis, having employed all its pains in vain and despairing of bringing this man to his duty, was constrained to suspend him from all the functions of his Ministry until the following Synod, a decision at which he mocked.

At the following Synod, which was that of Vlissingen in April of the year 1668, they began, according to the instructions of several Churches, with the affairs of Labadie. They wished to try a path of reconciliation rather than entering into an examination of the proceedings of the Classis held at Middelburg on the 10th of October

To make this plan succeed, the Synod first sent deputies to Their Lordships the States of the Province of Zeeland, who were then assembled in Middelburg, to assure them of the good intention it had for peace; and the Synod learned through the mouth of their <term>Pensionary<note>A high-ranking legal and administrative official of a Dutch province or city who advised the government.</note></term> that Commissioners had been appointed—one from each city in the Province—to handle this matter with the Deputies of the Synod. Finally, a settlement was drafted based on articles that were agreeable to both parties, and by virtue of these, a reconciliation was made with joy and satisfaction.

The summary of the articles was as follows: that Labadie would sign the revised **Confession of Faith** according to the new edition in two columns; that he would recognize the legitimate authority of the Synods and <term>Classes<note>Regional governing bodies in the Reformed Church.</note></term> over the <term>Consistories<note>The local council of elders and ministers in a single congregation.</note></term> according to the ecclesiastical discipline of the **Synod of Dordrecht** <note>The 1618–1619 national assembly that established the official doctrines of the Dutch Reformed Church.</note>. That his offensive writings would be held as null and void, just as, on the other hand, the resolutions of the Synod against the Consistory of Middelburg and Mr. de Labadie would be, notably his suspension pronounced by the Classis of Middelburg on October 10, 1667. That he would neither preach nor distribute in writing or by word of mouth the doctrine of the glorious Reign of <term>Jesus-Christ</term> on earth <note>This refers to Millenarianism, the belief in a future thousand-year earthly kingdom of Christ, which was considered heterodox by the established church.</note>. For soon after this storm had calmed, another accident occurred, which today serves as a famous pretext for Labadie to color his schism, since it is upon this that he primarily bases his separation from us. This was that his Church—or rather he himself—declared itself an accuser against the book of our very dear Brother, Mr. Wolzogue <note>Ludwig Wolzogen (1633–1690), a Walloon theologian whose rationalist approach to Scripture often sparked controversy.</note>, claiming it contained *several erroneous and scandalous propositions: Pelagian, Semi-Pelagian, Socinian, Arminian, and Papist, profane and blasphemous, against the Holy Scripture.* <note>These terms refer to various theological heresies involving the denial of original sin (Pelagian), the Trinity (Socinian), or strict predestination (Arminian).</note> The Synod appointed examiners for the said book, with orders to deliver their remarks into the hands of the said Mr. Wolzogue, who then offered to respond to them at the next Synod. They likewise appointed examiners for Labadie's book, titled the *Herald of the Great King <term>Jesus</term>*, as well as for that of Mr. Hulsius which opposed it, and all of this was to be presented at the following Synod of Naarden.

It was learned there that Labadie, contrary to the sixth article of the settlement made at Vlissingen and against ecclesiastical discipline (besides his book *The Herald* which he had already published long ago), had published various theological treatises which contained several dangerous and erroneous doctrines. These tended toward <term>Independentism<note>The belief that each local congregation should be autonomous, rejecting the oversight of a central church hierarchy.</note></term> and toward <term>Enthusiasm<note>In this era, "Enthusiasm" was a negative term for those claiming to receive direct, private revelations from the Holy Spirit, often seen as a threat to the authority of the Bible and the Church.</note></term>, according to the opinion of the Synod, which the Synod condemned. It was learned there by the unanimous report of the examiners that his book *The Herald* contained doctrine contrary to the Holy Scriptures, pernicious to religion, and dangerous to the State; as a consequence of this report, this book was also condemned along with the others by the Synod. This was granted to him by a very special indulgence. These examiners, after an exact review of the book, having again unanimously declared it **Orthodox**, seemed to have sufficiently protected it; but the Church of Middelburg, or rather Labadie, subsequently showing that he was not absolutely satisfied with this declaration and desiring that all the deputies of the Synod, both Elders and Pastors, take notice of their remarks—so that, as their deputies said through the mouth of Labadie, the judgment made of it would be a *Synodal judgment*—the Synod, through a very special and unprecedented kindness (to remove any pretext for quibbling from a spirit that asked for nothing better than to divide a body which by the grace of God has always been so perfectly united), gave in to this demand, without setting any precedent for the future. But this order of the Synod did not please a proud and haughty spirit such as that of Labadie, who knows nothing of submission; and as this was the only weak pretext remaining for his <term>schism<note>A formal split or division within a church body.</note></term>, he resolved to withdraw the following day, without doing anything that had been ordered of him, leaving only a letter full of insults against the Synod, written by the hand of his fellow deputy, which was delivered to the Synod after their departure. This departure, so prompt and so hurried, did not prevent the Synodal assembly from forming its judgment of suspension against Labadie, based on several significant and well-verified points; such as obstinacy in refusing to repair the honor of one of his colleagues, whom he had so calumniously defamed; infraction of Ecclesiastical discipline and the articles of the Vlissingen agreement <note>original: "l'accomodement de Fleffingues"</note> for having published books without the approval of the Synod; and infraction of Ecclesiastical discipline and the articles of the agreement for having published books full of doctrines injurious to Ecclesiastical orders, false, danger-

But like a runaway horse that had taken the bit between its teeth <note>original: "pris le frain aux dens," an idiom meaning to act uncontrollably or stubbornly</note>, the further he went, the more he strayed from his proper course, as could be seen at the Classis of Middelburg; for as soon as the Deputies of the Classis arrived there, believing they would meet at the Consistory of the **Choorkerck** <note>The "Choir Church" in Middelburg, part of the larger abbey complex.</note>, they found a great crowd of people in the Temple, and learned that Labadie and his followers had come to seize the Consistory, without the knowledge of those who had granted the place to the Deputies of the Classis; this obliged them to seek another location, into which Labadie and his followers did not fail to intrude themselves again. It went so far that when the Moderator of the Classis ordered him to leave, after having first very gently and humanely asked him to do so, Labadie, on the contrary, instead of obeying, replied in a surly tone and solely with the intent to trouble the assembly, ***I order you.***

...even to the point of feigning not to read our letter; so naturally humble, mild-mannered, and submissive is this man in all his conduct. <note>The author is being deeply ironic here, as the following sentences describe Labadie's supporters as a violent mob.</note> The <term>Classis<note>A regional governing body in Reformed church structure, consisting of ministers and elders from a specific district.</note></term>, seeing that there was no proper way to maintain order with people who—through their insults, murmurs, and threats—were playing the part of "Knights of Terror," was once again forced to abandon the place of their assembly. As the Deputies were leaving the premises, one of the most formidable mutineers of the gang that had besieged them, and through which they had to pass, had the insolence to say to the Moderator as he exited, *"if justice were done to you all, your heads would be cut off"*; for Labadie had so thoroughly turned the brains of these poor <term>Walloons<note>French-speaking Protestants from the Southern Netherlands and border regions who formed their own congregations in the Dutch Republic.</note></term> that they perhaps believed they would have made a sacrifice to God by beating to death half a dozen Ministers of the Classis.

The official minutes <note>original: "Les Verbaux"</note> drawn up in this Classis prove the difficulties the Deputies faced in dealing with these stiff-necked people, who disrupted affairs in every way they could—faking absences, declining summons, and creating every imaginable difficulty to prevent the execution of the sentence from the last <term>Synod of Naerden<note>A general assembly of the Walloon churches held in the town of Naarden.</note></term>. Labadie was then malicious enough to spread a rumor that he was only suspended for having accused Mr. Wolzogue's book of heterodoxy, thus using a pretext as a cause. He was extra-

<page-num>39</page-num>

vagant enough that, rejecting the order of our Synods and our Classes, he requested Commissioners from the Classis of Walcheren to examine Mr. Wolzogue's book, and begged them to take notice of the proceedings of the Synod of Naerden and the Classis of Middelburg against him and his followers. But the Classis of Walcheren was more prudent and better advised; for it declared in its response that it had no right to involve itself in anything, except to oblige Labadie and his <term>Consistory<note>The governing body of an individual local church, made up of the minister and elders.</note></term> to submit to the Synod, the Classis, and the Ecclesiastical order, as is right. Nevertheless, he knew how to lobby the Gentlemen Burgomasters of the city, and even the Noble and Powerful Estates of the Province of Zeeland <note>The sovereign governing body of the province.</note>, so effectively that the Classis could hardly execute the orders of the Synod without it costing much time, and without the need to very often keep the doors of the <term>Temple<note>A term commonly used by French Protestants (Huguenots and Walloons) to refer to their church buildings, distinguishing them from Catholic "churches."</note></term> closed. This saw the faithful deprived of the consolation of hearing the holy word of God preached there, to the great scandal of the whole city; until finally, the judgment of the Estates of Zeeland intervened, which in some way confirmed the decrees of the Synod of Naerden, and allowed Labadie and his followers to represent their grievances at the following Synod of Dordrecht.

suspension from the ministry <note>original: "Ministere decretée"</note> was decreed against Labadie, and it was expected that this man—who calls himself so zealous and, like another Saint Paul, ready to become an anathema for his brothers—would have submitted himself to the following <term>Synod of Dordrecht<note>A general assembly of the Reformed Church held in the city of Dordrecht. While the city is famous for the great Synod of 1618–19, this refers to a later meeting of the Walloon (French-speaking) branch of the church.</note></term>. There, he was expected to give glory to God, ask forgiveness for his errors, his infractions, and his slanders, and thus redeem the peace of all the Churches—especially that of Middelburg, which he has brought to the brink of ruin through his divisions. For this, he will have to ask forgiveness from his God as long as he lives. But *the Ethiopian could not change his skin, nor the leopard rid itself of its spots*, Jer. 13:23 <note>original: "le More ne pouvoit pas changer sa peau, ny le Leopard se defaire de ses taches." This is a biblical metaphor suggesting that Labadie’s nature was fundamentally unchangeable.</note>. For as soon as he arrived at Dordrecht with the faithful guards of his inner circle <note>original: "gardes de sa manche," literally "guards of his sleeve," implying a loyal or personal retinue.</note>, first Yvon, accompanied by Master Everard, presented to the Synod a writing signed by several persons. By this document, they represented that being authorized to bring their grievances to the Synod, the most important of their grievances was that of the approval of the book by Master <term>Wolzogue<note>Louis Wolzogen (1633–1690), a Walloon minister and professor whose theological writings were a major point of contention between Labadie and the church authorities.</note></term>. They stated that the second was to ask if the Synod held to the censures that had been made against those who had denounced the said book. To which the company replied in few words: *That it promised to respond to their demands in its own time.* Shortly thereafter, Masters de Labadie, Meunix, Schorer, and Baute presented themselves. Labadie, acting as the spokesman, said that in accordance with the act of the <term>Estates of Zeeland<note>The provincial government of Zeeland, which often intervened in church disputes to maintain civil order.</note></term>

<meta>catchword: that having</meta>

—jointly to propose all their complaints, and then it would be seen which one would be started first. It was represented to them that their arguments to the contrary were not sufficient: not the first, since it was not a question of establishing or rejecting a point of doctrine, but only of knowing whether the book of Mr. Wolzogue <note>original: "Sieur Wolzogue." This refers to Louis de Wolzogen, a theologian whose Cartesian-influenced ideas were under fire from traditionalists.</note> contains anything contrary to sound doctrine or not; not the second, since the company can satisfy everyone regarding these demands, as it has resolved to do, even if it does not begin precisely with their examination; nor the third, because a short delay will not burden their consciences; nor the fourth, since the other matters are entirely detached from this one; nor the fifth, since the true way to shorten the proceedings is to separate them well after having examined them all; nor the sixth, because that example carried no weight: because if at Naerden <note>A town in the Netherlands; the site of a previous church gathering.</note> they had started with the matter of Mr. Wolzogue, it was only to force Mr. de Labadie to prove his denunciation, since he had deferred it from one Synod to another; in short, having the intention to settle all matters, they should not get stuck on their order.

But it was in the interest of Labadie's schism to hang the progress of all business on this minor circumstance; because having—

<column-break/>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>of Mr. Jean de Labadie.</header>
<meta>catchword: explain</meta>

—having planned <note>original: "minuté," suggesting he had drafted or timed his exit in advance.</note> his withdrawal based on Mr. Wolzogue’s book, it was only right that he should press for this matter to be the first one proposed. It was in his interest that the other grievances did not march in the front ranks, because if he had been deposed by a sentence of the Synod for having violated <term>Ecclesiastical discipline<note>The formal rules governing the conduct of ministers and the management of the Reformed Church.</note></term> in so many ways, and for having published, against orders, books full of erroneous and pernicious doctrines, there would have been no reason to color his Schism and his withdrawal. This would be true even if his Denunciation against Mr. Wolzogue’s book were later judged legitimate, and if that book had been condemned in all the length and breadth of his bold accusation. It was therefore of the utmost importance, both for Labadie's reputation and for the Schism he had in mind, not to budge on this point and to demand above all things that the matter concerning the Orthodoxy of Mr. Wolzogue's book be treated first. This was because it would always yield one of these two advantages: that if the approval of the book were confirmed—of which his conscience, if he has any left, could well assure him, since this book, when read without prejudice, contains nothing but what is Orthodox—he would have a pretext to withdraw and say that he no longer wanted any communion with <term>heterodox<note>Holding opinions that differ from the established or "correct" religious doctrine.</note></term> people and the promoters of Heresy (as, by his grace, he did not fail to explain—)

The author argues this was a strategic move by Labadie to justify his planned departure from the church as a matter of religious principle rather than a disciplinary punishment.</summary>

# A Curious History
<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>A Curious History</header>

—explained at length in his Manifesto (which disguises his Schism <note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674) was a former Jesuit who converted to Protestantism but eventually led a separatist movement known as Labadism, which emphasized internal piety over church structure.</note>, which we shall examine shortly). He argued that if, on the contrary, to remove every excuse from him, the assembly refused to start with the complaint regarding the book <note>This refers to the book by Louis de Wolzogen discussed on the previous page.</note> and instead wanted to begin with the other charges against him, he would have had the opportunity to withdraw and claim that they refused to hear him and denied him justice.

And indeed, Labadie and his followers took this incident as an excuse to break away, and they withdrew from the city, as the story is more fully recorded in Article 4 of the Synod of Dordrecht <note>The Synod was a high-level meeting of church leaders. While the Great Synod of 1618-19 is most famous, this refers to a later regional assembly.</note>. This did not prevent the Assembly from performing its duty in the fear of God and with the justice required by these affairs; this is noted in Article 5. For, reviewing all the facts alleged and proven against the said Labadie—such as erroneous and dangerous doctrines contrary to Holy Scripture that are not accepted in our Churches, stubbornness, slanders, violations of Ecclesiastical Discipline and of the settlement treaty made by the Lords of the States of Zealand, bad faith, the disguising of truth, manifest rebellion against the Assembly, and a flagrant contempt for the Synod—and having then heard the report of the Classis <note>A 'Classis' is a regional governing body in the Reformed tradition, similar to a presbytery.</note> of Zealand held at Middelburg on October 10, 1668, which declared him by one of its acts to be removable from all functions of his Ministry, for several faults

...by a sincere repentance for all his faults he might edify the Church of God, just as he has scandalized it by his behaviors and by his entire conduct.

Following this sentence of deposition <note>Deposition refers to the formal removal of a person from their office as a minister.</note> drawn up against Jean de Labadie <note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674) was a former Jesuit who became a Reformed minister before being expelled for his radical separatist views, later founding the Labadist sect.</note>, the Synod ordered a <term>Classis<gloss>A regional governing body in the Reformed Church, composed of ministers and elders from a specific district.</gloss></term> at Middelburg to execute what had been decreed; but this Classis did not pass without Labadie and his followers raising new storms against it. These were such—both through the horrible scandal that Labadie gave by taking the pulpit of the Temple <note>In French Protestant tradition, the church building was called the "Temple" to distinguish it from Catholic "Churches."</note> by force, and profaning the august Sacrament of the <term>Holy Supper<gloss>Also known as Holy Communion or the Eucharist.</gloss></term>, which was meant to be distributed by another Pastor, as well as by other enormous irregularities—that we must praise God that the Churches are delivered from a man whose soul seems to be sold to trouble and division, and who has excited so many disorders throughout all of Zeeland, and notably in the city of Veere <note>original: "Terveere"</note>, where this schismatic had since entrenched himself. It is nothing but the truth to say that since the tempests of the years [16]18 and [16]19 of this century <note>A reference to the years 1618–1619, the time of the Synod of Dort, which was a period of intense religious and political conflict in the Netherlands.</note>, the vessel of the Reformed Church has not felt any more perilous or more fatal.

What can be said to all these things? And how can those good souls so deeply engaged in the party of Mr. de Labadie justify the proceedings of this man, condemned on so many counts, by so many Classes, by

Is there any reason to suspect that entire Synods, composed of so many Pastors and so many Elders—each of whom knows he is accountable for his actions to the Great Judge—would have, either through envy or some other equally unreasonable motive, wished to wound their own consciences just to have the pleasure of condemning a single Labadie? A man who came from elsewhere, and moreover a man who is not in a position to dazzle <note>original: "ebloüir"</note> a good number of our Pastors by his talents or by his extraordinary erudition? Who among us all would have wanted to plot such a design, if in good conscience he had not judged him condemnable, and if he had not believed there was a need to restrain a turbulent man who made a glory of violating the orders of the Church, of upsetting the Synods, of decrying the Confessions of Faith, and of affecting a proud dominion over the heritage of the Lord. What can be said in defense of this factious man? But what can be produced as a charge against our Synods?

Will it be said that Mr. de Labadie has done much good since he has been in these Provinces, and that he has awakened the zeal of many good souls who previously let themselves be swept away by the torrent of this corrupt century?

# Curious History

to examine the reverse side, this is very capable of dazzling many people and greatly swelling the ranks of our newly "Illuminated" <note>A derogatory term for radical mystics who claimed direct divine inspiration, often used for Labadie’s followers.</note> and our malcontents; but let the public truth teach them that if Monsieur de Labadie has done much good since he has been in these Provinces, it is certain that he has done a thousand times more harm, and that he has not well observed the rule of the Apostle, who, while not permitting on one hand *that one should do evil so that good may come of it* <note>A reference to Romans 3:8, a common theological argument against using unethical means to achieve supposedly holy ends.</note>, also seems on the other hand not to permit that one should do a little good so that much evil might result. For to reform the morals of the age (which appeared to be Labadie's primary aim), he did not need to clash head-on with the Confessions of Faith <note>Specifically the Belgic Confession, the doctrinal standard of the Dutch Reformed Church.</note> signed with the blood of so many glorious Martyrs, nor to overturn the public Discipline of our Churches, nor to throw our Synods into turmoil, nor to recklessly decry the reputation of our Pastors, nor to plot a Schism that was later made to burst forth. A gentle and moderate spirit, who would have learned from the mouth and after the model of his Savior to be humble and mild, would have undoubtedly acted with less violence, less passion, and more success; it is not always enough that our designs be just and reasonable, the means we employ to make them succeed must also be legitimate and approved. Christian prudence is 
<meta>catchword: not</meta>

<column-break/>

# of Lord Jean de Labadie.

not reckless; it takes its time, chooses its measures, and takes advantage of opportunities. And just as medicines taken at the wrong time result in a very perilous operation, so too the Reformations that one wishes to put into use in an inconvenient season—in which one makes it a task to overturn the foundation of the union of the Churches and to break the bonds of their correspondence and their Discipline—are Reformations that serve rather to propagate disorders than to pull up the root of evils. How much easier would it have been for Monsieur de Labadie to employ his insights, which he does not lack, to seek the solid friendship of his colleagues, and, joining interests and causes with them, to work together with one shoulder <note>A 17th-century idiom meaning to work with a common effort or in unison.</note> for the advancement of the glory of their common Master? But vanity holding the upper hand in Labadie’s mind, his own glory would have suffered a loss if he had associated himself with anyone else; it was necessary that he alone, against all Orders, without support, be proclaimed a new John the Baptist, and a third Cato <note>Cato the Younger was a Roman statesman famous for his rigid morality and stubborn resistance to corruption; here it implies Labadie’s perceived self-importance as a moral judge.</note> descended from Heaven to come and correct the abuses of the age, while meanwhile multiplying troubles and divisions in the Church.

Will it be said that Monsieur de Labadie is an extremely zealous man who preaches the sanctification of the faithful through works so much that he often forgets to speak to them of their <term>justification by Faith<gloss>The core Protestant doctrine that salvation is a gift from God through faith alone, rather than through human deeds or "works."</gloss></term>?

But if the zeal of this gentleman is accompanied by neither Charity nor Prudence; if it is a zeal without knowledge and inconsiderate; if it is a zeal that has far more fire than light, and far more impetuosity than reason, can such zeal make him worthy of recommendation? Now, the zeal he has displayed until now is a zeal without charity and without prudence, since it has led him to incessantly declaim against the Leaders of all our Churches, without them ever having deserved his censure, and without him having any right to go and blacken their reputations to their Flocks without their knowledge, even from the very pulpits they occupied. *For who are you to judge another’s servant? To his own master he stands or falls,* <note>original: "Car qui és tu qui juges le serviteur d'autruy, il se tient ferme, où il trebuche à son propre Seigneur." A translation of Romans 14:4.</note> says the Apostle. His zeal has been without knowledge and inconsiderate, since for having pretended not to have sufficiently understood the meaning of a certain book (upon which he has since tried to unjustly base the subject of his entire quarrel and his dispute against the Synod, and even his Schism), his impetuosity and blind ardor carried him so far that he pronounced this Book full of sentiments of Irreligion, Impiety, Popery, Socinianism, Pelagianism, Arminianism, <note>These terms refer to various theological heresies or rival doctrines that the Reformed Church found threatening.</note> and everything else that his bile, stirred up at the time, made him vomit as insults against this Treatise; which nevertheless had the honor and advantage of being declared Orthodox by the Deputies of an entire Synod. His anger was the cause

<meta>catchword: cause</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>55</page-num>
-># of Mr. Jean de Labadie.<-

cause of his blunder; and just as those whose minds are troubled by wine are prone to see, as the saying goes, *a double Sun and a double Thebes* <note>A classical reference to the madness of Pentheus from Euripides' "The Bacchae," often used to describe someone whose judgment is distorted by passion or intoxication.</note>—that is to say, the doubling of things that exist only in their pure and simple state—so too the pride of Monsieur de Labadie, which had made him mount his high horses and disapprove of this book, engaged him so deeply that he lashed out against it with as many atrocious terms as the jealousy he bore toward a young Pastor, <note>Likely a reference to Louis Wolzogen, a rival minister whose work Labadie attacked.</note> whose talents are incomparably more beautiful than his own, was capable of inventing. These passions made him see things in the book that could only be drawn from it by forced and very uncharitable conclusions. Furthermore, his zeal was nothing but pure impetuosity when, at Middelburg, he took the public pulpit in the Church by storm and seized the Holy Table where the August Sacrament of the Holy Supper was to be distributed. <note>The "Holy Supper" or "Sainte Cène" is the Protestant term for Communion or the Eucharist.</note> Without considering that this Sacrament is the mark of the union and communion of the faithful, he turned it into a character of division between the Members of the same Body, distributing it with a heart troubled by hatred and with sacrilegious and profane hands. What fine disposition could there be in the soul of Mr. de Labadie, and in the conscience of his Communicants, after an action of this nature, in which sedition broke out, force was made visible, and violence made itself known? ...what spirit could they all bring together to a communion that ought to have been so holy, having their hearts filled with such bitterness and such animosity against the rest of their brothers? And then, is this the holy and sacred zeal of our Mr. de Labadie, which has caught the eye and awakened the zeal of so many people who have declared themselves his followers? <note>term: "followers" (sectateurs). In this context, it refers to those who left the established Reformed Church to join Labadie’s separatist movement.</note>

So that if his devouring zeal has since ignited the zeal of many other good souls, we must say that it was by artifice and by a deception worthy of the flexibility of his spirit, rather than by a holy and pious motive; so much so that when we see so many people—otherwise quite reasonable in their conduct and quite pious in their sentiments—yet following the impetuous and unregulated movements of Mr. de Labadie, in the manner of the inferior spheres that allow themselves to be swept away by the speed of the prime mover: <note>term: "prime mover" (Primum Mobile). In the old Ptolemaic system of astronomy, this was the outermost sphere of the universe that moved all the inner planetary spheres.</note> We cannot help but groan within ourselves to see this prodigious reversal of spirit in the heads of a good part of his partisans, and we are astonished to see that some inhabitants of the Earth run after the Beast, <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Revelation, suggesting Labadie’s movement has an almost apocalyptic or deceptive nature.</note> and that the world, which once sighed to see itself suddenly become Arian, <note>Arian: a reference to a 4th-century theological crisis where the monk Arius denied the full divinity of Christ. The author is quoting Saint Jerome, who famously said, "The world groaned and was astonished to find itself Arian."</note> has reason to sigh now to see itself suddenly become Labadist. However, if he had the advantage of awakening a false zeal in some, he had the

<column-break/>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>of Mr. Jean de Labadie.</header>
misfortune of awakening against himself the just anger of others, who cannot tolerate that the most turbulent of all men, the most malicious of all Pastors, the most proud of all Schismatics, should be so bold as to set up altar against altar <note>term: "altar against altar" (dresser autel contre autel). A traditional phrase describing the act of creating a schism or a rival church within the same territory.</note> in a country where the Reformation has such powerful Protectors and is so gloriously authorized. But God will perhaps soon step out of the machine of his Providence to blow upon these pernicious designs, since this truth is often confirmed by notable experiences: *That never does God appear more as God, than when men appear more as Demons.*

Yes, but some will perhaps say, it is wrong to blame the zeal of Mr. de Labadie, since it is certain that he is a holy man of exemplary piety; for has one ever heard of his drunkenness, his debauchery, his incontinence, or his frivolousness?

Even though the white of his hair has perhaps erased the darkest parts of his life: nevertheless, when one reasons in this manner, can one infer that Labadie is indeed of such exemplary holiness and piety, under the pretext that he is neither of an age nor a profession to be either a lecher or a drunkard? If this reasoning were of sound logic, could one not conclude from it that the Devil himself, having neither flesh nor

nor blood, would have the right to aspire to **Canonization**, <note>term: "Canonization." The process by which the Catholic Church declares a deceased person a saint. Here, the author uses it ironically to mock Labadie’s pretensions of holiness within a Protestant context.</note> that is to say, to pass for holy and for just, under the pretense that he has not defiled himself with either lewdness or drunkenness. Impurity and debauchery are vices that, without a doubt, absolutely degrade Pastors, since the Scripture ordains that to be legitimate models for their flock, they must be continent and sober. However, just because one is not a slave to these two sorts of vices—intemperance and impurity—it does not follow that one can establish the holiness and piety of a Pastor of the Church; for he may have **vices of the spirit**, which undoubtedly far surpass those of the body, according to the maxim: *That things which are the best in their essence, are the worst in their corruption.* <note>original: "Que les choses qui sont les meilleures dans leur être, sont les pires dans leur corruption." This is a translation of the Latin legal and philosophical proverb "Corruptio optimi pessima."</note> And indeed, the vices that shine most brightly today in Labadie are those of the spirit, such as, among others: hatred, pride, lying, envy, jealousy, the desire to trouble and disturb consciences, to seduce people away from the communion they once professed, and what is most criminal, to establish **Schism** <note>term: "Schism" (Schisme). A formal division within a church or religious body. Labadie was frequently accused of breaking the unity of the Reformed Church to start his own sect.</note> in Religion. So much so that at present it is true to say that if Labadie does not give in to the sins that make men like **Beasts**—that is, impurity and drunkenness—he gives in to the more subtle and more pernicious sins

<column-break/>

<page-num>59</page-num>
->### of Sieur Jean de Labadie.<-
<sig>C 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: (sans</meta>

which elevate men to the condition of **Demons**.

We must, however, say on this chapter that the history of his life teaches us that formerly, in his less-withered youth, he had a greater leaning toward the sins of the Brutes than toward those of the Demons. That is to say, if at present he makes his spirit sin much more than his body, it is certain that in the past he had more of an inclination to follow the outbursts of the desires of his flesh than those of his turbulent and Schismatic zeal. And so that it does not appear that we are speaking obscurely or at random, it is only right that the reader be informed of a meeting—a bit too carnal—that the good Mr. de Labadie had with a very pretty and very well-to-do young person, the daughter of **Sieur Drevon**, residing near the Clock in **Orange**. <note>Orange was an independent principality in southern France at the time, a significant stronghold for Protestants (Huguenots).</note> It happened that, having felt the heat of his zeal awaken at the sight of this girl, who had inspired in his heart I know not what tenderness for her (which shows, in passing, that a **Jesuit** <note>Labadie was originally a member of the Society of Jesus (Jesuits) before converting to Protestantism. The author uses "naked Jesuit" to mean a man stripped of his religious pretenses.</note> completely naked is a man like any other), he believed that his reputation for being very Prudish, very Devout, very Pious, and very capable of inspiring Devotion in Souls, would be a convenient cloak under which he could make his approaches to commit this girl to his "Devotion." For this purpose, he resolved to write her a letter, which in its superscription bore these words: *To the Chosen Soul.* <note>original: "l'Ame Eleüe." This term mimics the mystical language Labadie often used to flatter his female followers by suggesting they were part of God's spiritual elite.</note>

Shall we be told that we must believe that the Piety of **Monsieur de Labadie** is not counterfeit, since God has crowned him with such beautiful talents, that he is endowed with an eloquence so natural, so vivid, so strong, that while other Pastors are constrained in ten thousand ways to fill their hour in the Pulpit, **Monsieur de Labadie** on the contrary is a man so abundant in beautiful conceptions, so rich in words, so fluid in his discourse, that he can hold his Listeners for four and five hours straight without wearying their patience, nor wearying himself?

Never were Father Veron <note>François Véron (1575–1649), a Jesuit known for his aggressive and theatrical style of debating Protestants.</note>, formerly of his Order, or the little Father André, an Augustinian monk in Paris <note>André Boulanger (1578–1657), known as "le petit Père André," a preacher famous for his humorous and often grotesque sermons.</note>, so extravagant in the pulpit as Labadie is even more than they; for he alone has made his listeners laugh far more than those two great Charlatans together have entertained their assembly. One day, having to explain this text from Psalm 51: *My mother conceived me in sin and I was warmed in iniquity* <note>original: "Ma Mere ma conceu en peché & j'ay été échauffé en iniquité." <header>of Monsieur Jean de Labadie.</header>

this remark in the form of an introduction, which was found quite crude to the chaste ears of his listeners, and which he uttered roughly in these terms: "This child," he said, "was conceived in sin, and those two" (pointing to the Groom and the Bride) "are going to warm themselves in iniquity." Another time, speaking of the immodesty of the girls of Geneva and the too-free access they gave to foreigners, he addressed to them from the middle of the pulpit this fine exhortation in these words or similar: "They flirt with you," he said, "they give you gifts of ribbons, lace, fans; they give you muffs, and then honor imperceptibly slips away through the hole of the muff." Are these terms suitable for a Minister who wishes to be believed so Saintly, so Reformed, and so Pious? Are these the words that Saint Paul, in Colossians 4, wants to be seasoned with salt and grace to give edification to those who hear us? Yet another time, and it was on a day of the Lord's Supper, it is said he spoke to the whole assembly in these terms: "Vain men and vain women, I see you today in a decent enough state; but what! You will soon take up your beauty patches again, your ribbons, your trailing gowns, your masks; in truth, I would rather see you all naked." Was this not a remnant of the spirit of the Carmelite of Graville <note>A reference to a scandalous religious figure or sect associated with "Adamite" tendencies—preaching a return to the innocence of nakedness.</note> who preached nakedness to girls to better lead them back to the state of original Innocence? Are these the strokes of Eloquence that people admire in this Mountebank?

If being Eloquent means having the talent to speak a lot and for a long time in a row, Ha! how the Charlatans at the fairs and the fishwives <note>The "harangères" (fishwives) of Paris were famously loud, foul-mouthed, and articulate in their own way, often used as a standard for rowdy, unrefined speech.</note> at the Fish Markets deserve beautiful Crowns. Indeed, if he had less presumption of being Eloquent, and if he did not strive for Praise through a heap of troublesome words, there would be reason to hope that he might become so; But he employs such ridiculous discourses, and such disfigured figures of speech, to acquire this quality, that those who have often exhausted their patience listening to him were constrained to say of him upon leaving his Sermon what Theocritus said of a certain Anaximenes, *that he had a river of words, where there was not a drop of good sense.* <note>This quote is attributed to the Greek poet Theocritus regarding Anaximenes of Lampsacus, a rhetorician known for his verbosity.</note> We must not pass over in silence an example of the great ease this Eloquent Preacher possesses in explaining a text in the Pulpit on the spot, and without any preparation of the mind, even though one of our greatest Theologians of the last century once said, *That he never ventured to go up into the Pulpit to explain the Word of God to the people, without having done Him the honor of studying it well beforehand.* <note>Likely a reference to Jean Calvin or Theodore Beza, emphasizing the Reformed tradition of rigorous study versus Labadie's claimed spontaneous inspiration.</note> However, our Most Illustrious [Labadie] one day performed an admirable feat of his primary trade; having entered the Temple <note>Protestant church.</note> a little before going up into the Pulpit, he noticed that the Reader had, in his opinion, incorrectly marked the Psalm that was to be sung, which the other man nonetheless contested;

contested <note>original: "testa ;" from "contesta"</note>; but finally he was forced to change the number according to the good pleasure of Monsieur de Labadie, who, having subsequently gone up into the Pulpit, took it upon himself to say at the beginning of his Sermon, that although he had prepared himself on his text from the Gospel, it so happened that having just noticed that the marked Psalm was more suited to the circumstances of the present time, he had judged it best to postpone his ordinary text to another occasion, and was resolved to say for the explanation of the Psalm what *the spirit of God would inspire him with on the spot.* But because the Listeners perceived that he performed then with less failure than usual, and that the dispute he had just had with the Reader showed that he had very seriously thought about it in his study, they concluded on the spot, and without much preparation in forming their conclusion, that Labadie, who wanted to pass for a man who produced *impromptus*, was the greatest scoundrel, and the most impudent liar in the world.

In truth, did he not show good grace when he used those fine figures of Rhetoric, and when he recommended himself in such an agreeable manner? What conclusion could one draw to his advantage when one said: Monsieur de Labadie is not a *lazy belly* <note>original: "ventre paresseux"; a biblical allusion to Titus 1:12, often translated as "lazy gluttons."</note>, he is not a *drunkard*, he is not a *lecher*, he is only very slightly *ignorant*, he does not know much.
