Among other things, the History of his conduct in Middelburg teaches us that two years ago, Mr. du Soul <note>Moïse du Soul (also known as Solanus), a Protestant scholar and pastor who served as chaplain to the Count of Schomberg.</note>, then Pastor to His Excellency the Count of Schomberg, having given a very good and very edifying performance <note>original: "action"; here referring to a sermon or liturgical service.</note> to the people there on a weekday—as it is his usual talent to preach very solidly—it happened that people spoke of it with a high reputation in the city. Because he had the misfortune of having had quite few listeners when he preached, this awakened the zeal of many Pious persons who ardently desired to hear the said Gentleman to benefit from his beautiful and excellent Exhortations. And since two Elders <note>The "Anciens" were lay leaders in the Reformed (Protestant) church hierarchy.</note> who had had the privilege of hearing him in 1667 at the Synod of Leiden, urgently represented to Labadie at the Church <note>original: "Temple"; French Protestants (Huguenots) referred to their places of worship as temples rather than churches.</note> at the conclusion of the Prayer service, that to satisfy the desire of the whole Church, he would do very well to offer the Pulpit to Monsieur du Soul for the following Sunday; they could get nothing from him

other than this act of incivility: giving a nudge with his elbow to one, and the same to the other of these two Gentlemen between whom he stood, he abruptly left them. This so greatly shocked these two Elders that, not knowing what to report back to Mr. du Soul, they resolved to follow Labadie to his house. There, they no doubt represented to him what was proper and begged him to repair the fault the next morning and to grant their request, since it concerned the satisfaction of the entire Church. But how do you think he repaired it? It was that the following day, Saturday, at the Church during the morning prayer exercise, he said these fine words to Mr. du Soul: *"Sir, I would indeed offer you my Pulpit for tomorrow morning, if you were not requested by the Church of Flushing <note>original: "Flessingue"; the English name for the Dutch port city Vlissingen.</note>."* To which the said Mr. du Soul replied that he was only engaged at Flushing conditionally and in the event of the Pastor's absence; but knowing him to have returned, he was released from his word; that he thanked him for the honor of his Pulpit which he offered him, and that he accepted with all his heart, and that to better fill it he was going to prepare himself early and would withdraw. But it was here that pride, Jealousy, and Envy burst forth all at once, and that Labadie—who believes himself the Antichrist of Ministers, since he raises himself above everything that is called a Minister in the world <note>A polemical reference to 2 Thessalonians 2:4, which describes the Antichrist as exalting himself above all that is called God.</note>—to prevent a more learned and more eloquent Pastor from edifying...

<meta>This page continues the critique of Jean de Labadie's ministry in Middelburg. Following the previous page's account of how local elders wished to hear the respected minister Moïse du Soul preach, this text describes Labadie’s jealous refusal to yield his pulpit and mocks his pretentious rhetorical style.</meta>

# Curious History
<meta>catchword: concise</meta>

might not edify the Church, feeling himself pressured, took it upon himself to say, *Sir, I am not giving you my pulpit, and you shall not have it.* And then this man, puffed up with Pharisaic pride <note>Referring to the self-righteousness and legalism associated with the Pharisees in the New Testament; a common insult in 17th-century religious polemics.</note>, sought the glory of men rather than that of God, and the preservation of his own reputation rather than the edification of the Church. It seemed to him that he was right to act in such a way, and not to let the Sieur du Soul <note>Moïse du Soul, a noted Protestant scholar and pastor.</note> preach in his pulpit; for, believing himself to his own ears and taste to be the most eloquent Preacher in the world, it was not reasonable that he should take away "the most" from his people, only to give them "the least." But anyone other than he would certainly not have shared his view, and would have judged that this proceeding savored more of the Envy and Jealousy of a Monk than of the Charity and zeal of a truly Reformed Pastor. For by refusing his pulpit to a man gifted with incomparably greater talents, he took away the most from his people, only to give them effectively the least. As he affects all sorts of extraordinary things in the pulpit, solely to appear extraordinary to the people, so too in the exposition of a text, his conscience would forbid him from following the received method. And how could he follow the beaten path, since the Lord’s Prayer <note>original: "la priere Dominicale"</note> is not extensive enough for his taste, and needs to be seasoned with his commentary to be presented to God with success? This, he believes, could not be done in the concise terms

concise <note>original: "r'accourcis"</note> terms that JESUS CHRIST taught to his Apostles. The divisions of his text are often as excellently rhymed as those of that "good" Preacher of the Roman Church, who, dividing his discourse regarding the offering that JESUS CHRIST made of himself to his Father, said that this offering *was very lovable, very suitable, very profitable, and very costly:* <note>original: "moult aimable, moult convenable, moult profitable, & moult coutable." The author is mocking a medieval-style rhyming sermon structure.</note> and often his headings serve so poorly as titles for the things he has to say, just as the titles of the chapters in Montaigne's book of *Essays* <note>The writer Michel de Montaigne (1533–1592) was famous for giving his essays titles that often had little to do with the actual content, a style the author here views as disorganized.</note> relate to the subject treated therein. In a word, we can say that if this good man preaches in the same manner that he writes—that is to say, with as little intelligibility and correctness—his Sermons must resemble the fig tree of the Gospel, which abounded in leaves but bore no fruit.

But perhaps one will reply: "Will it then be said that Monsieur de Labadie is not a man of wit? Do they wish to contest this advantage he has acquired of being so eloquent in the pulpit? For even in all the Synods <term>Synods: representative assemblies of ministers and elders that govern the Reformed Church.</term> he has so thoroughly held the upper hand in this regard, that not one of all the Ministers, old or young, who today act so maliciously against him, dared to open their mouths in his presence, because with a single one of his words he was capable of confounding them and rendering them mute."

This reply, in our view, does not add much value...

wrongly regarding the precious qualities of Monsieur de Labadie, and by this account, this honest man performs miracles in the Synodical Assemblies <note>Meetings of church leaders (ministers and elders) to decide on doctrine and administration.</note> quite opposite to those of the Holy Spirit in the College of Apostles, upon whom it so favorably inspired its fires and lights that they began to speak in foreign tongues and felt themselves become divinely eloquent. But this fearsome man perhaps did not know why silence was kept at the Synod when he was present; it was because *the wolf was in the story* <note>original: "Lupus erat in fabula." A Latin proverb similar to "speak of the devil," used here to suggest his presence was ominous.</note>, and because this *Wolf under sheep’s wool appeared in the assembly*. In a word, we do not contest Monsieur de Labadie’s wit; but we say it is not of the best temper. If he appears to have fire and light, it is that fire and light peculiar to **Comets** <note>In the 17th century, comets were often seen as unpredictable, chaotic, or ill-omened, unlike the steady, divine light of the stars.</note>, and not that fire and light which fills the bodies of the Stars and constitutes the essence of the good Angels. Indeed, one might say of him what is said of the Italians: that they resemble those Slaves of whom the most skilled are always the worst.

# of the Lord Jean de Labadie.
<sig>D 2</sig>

We do not deny, either, that he often affected in our Synods that superiority of speech by virtue of which he wished to pass for the *Rabbi* <note>original: "Rabbi," used here in the sense of "Master" or "great teacher," implying Labadie sought an arrogant level of authority over his peers.</note> or the Master, and that in consideration of his age and the zeal with which he seemed filled, much was often deferred to him. But that he was capable of sowing consternation in the minds of all the Ministers, and rendering them mute at his pleasure by the force of his words—of this there are two examples among others so contrary to his claims, which took place in the presence of two celebrated Synods, that when the People are informed of them, they will soon be disabused of their error.

The first occurred at the **Synod of Flushing** <note>Vlissingen, a city in the Netherlands where a Reformed synod was held.</note> during the first session. While one of the Pastors was offering a prayer to ask God for the assistance of His spirit upon the matters to be treated there, Monsieur de Labadie was insolent and impious enough that, without showing respect either to God or to the Prayer addressed to Him by the mouths of all His servants, he impudently undertook—with a tone of voice as proud and surly as he ever used—to interrupt the prayer. But the spirit of God was stronger than the spirit of the devil: the Pastor continued the thread of his oration, and Labadie was forced to sheathe his insults. The other encounter in which Labadie had need of all the strength of his wit—which nevertheless failed him...

Will it still be said, in praise of his profound capacity, that Monsieur de Labadie is a great man who has composed more books than other Ministers have perhaps read in their entire lives?

That is a bold claim, which is indeed supported by a great number of books; however, Labadie’s spirit often loses its way among them. So many pages are filled with ignorance, and so many other pages are left wanting for common sense. If the good man were capable of taking advice, the best thing for the establishment of his reputation would have been for him never to have taken pen in hand to write, and that he had made the same wish as the most *honest man* <note>Used sarcastically here.</note> of the Caesars of old Rome, namely Nero: *“Oh, would that I did not know how to write!”* <note>original: "O utinam litteras nescivissem." # Curious History

...that he has always been the object of the people's admiration, and of a reputation so high and so celebrated that all his adversaries will never be able to tarnish its great brilliance.

We admit that in all the places he has passed through, he has had the common people as his admirers, who allowed themselves to be dazzled by his deceptions more easily than judicious and enlightened persons would have. Such discerning people are not accustomed to making pronouncements based on mere labels, nor to judging a tree by its leaves and its flowers. If it was observed in the past that at the public combats between men and animals—staged to satisfy the sight of the Roman people—the voices of the Theater were most of the time more favorable to the beasts than to the men (so impertinent is the common people usually), should one find it strange that the people allowed themselves to be charmed by Labadie’s fine outward appearances, and that in their applause they awarded him the Palm and the Crown? Any reputation acquired by such means is a reputation subject to restitution, since it is a very poorly acquired asset; or, at the very least, it cannot be possessed by any better title than that of that *Determined Man* <note>A reference to Herostratus, who set fire to the Temple of Artemis in Ephesus in 356 BC specifically to ensure his name would live on in history.</note> who procured for himself a wretched glory in the world for having dared to set fire to the august Temple of Diana of Ephesus. All this does not prevent people from saying, perhaps, that he has a good number of disciples, and that he is armed with a prodigious quantity of excellent testimonials from the most celebrated Pastors of France, who give him such a profusion of praise that all his envious rivals are confounded by it.

But if he wishes it to be said that he has disciples, it is because he wants people to believe he is a Rabbi or a great Master, the relationship being inevitable between the Master and his disciples: yet if he has some disciples whom he has attached to himself through the interests of the belly <note>A biblical and polemical idiom suggesting they follow him for material gain or physical sustenance rather than spiritual truth.</note>, and whose good sense he has overturned to make them succumb to his daydreams, his glory is not much increased by it; for just as no proposition so ridiculous has ever been put forward that has not had some Philosopher as its defender, so too has there never been a Master so extravagant that he did not have his followers and disciples. He lived in France, under the inspection of so many learned and pious Ministers, that he did not dare to risk making very coarse false steps; likewise, as soon as he arrived in these Provinces <note>The United Provinces of the Netherlands.</note>, he said in confidence to a very venerable Pastor, that at *Montauban*, at *Orange*, and at *Geneva*, *he had lived as a slave, but that now he was going to live in liberty*. Who then does not see that the conclusion is quite unreasonable? Master de Labadie brought so many excellent testimonials from France, therefore it is not possible that he has acted among us as a Turbulent person, an Enthusiast <note>In the 17th century, an "Enthusiast" was a derogatory term for someone claiming direct, often irrational, divine inspiration outside of church authority.</note>, and a Schismatic. Will it finally be said that he is of a temperament far more gentle and submissive than people cry out, since in the time that he has come to settle in the city of Amsterdam, he behaves there as a lamb rather than a Pastor, in that he no longer continues his preaching or his conventicles <note>Secret or unauthorized religious meetings.</note> there? And to show that he does not wish to disobey the superior powers of his city, nor henceforth give any subject for complaint against him, he possesses his soul there in silence and in patience, even praising God in the midst of the most ardent persecution by his enemies?

Saint Cyprian <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Carthage and martyr; the author is mocking Labadie for comparing his own legal troubles to the trials of early Christian martyrs.</note>—ah, what reason would Labadie have here to promise himself a beautiful crown? They obliged him to clear out of their Province because, according to one of the Constitutions of the Emperor Leo, <term>the gates of the city must be entrusted to faithful men</term> <note>Original Greek: τὰς πύλας τῆς πόλεως πιστοῖς ἀνδράσι δεῖ παρακολουθεῖν. Thus, these *Angels of division who had been unable to keep their first home* <note>A mocking reference to Jude 1:6, which describes fallen angels who left their proper dwelling; the author implies Labadie is a demonic force of discord.</note> were constrained to come and seek it elsewhere, and the storm tossed them into the most populous city of Holland <note>Amsterdam.</note> to be better hidden among the crowd. It is not necessary to penetrate into the reasons that led the Lords Magistrates of Amsterdam to permit Labadie to come take refuge in their city; the Mysteries of the Gods are not always revealed to men. Perhaps these prudent Politicians, seeing that it was not necessary to break down a section of the wall to receive this horse among them, charitably believed that it was filled neither with swords nor daggers like that of Troy, and was consequently in no state to sow division in their city.

...to publish both the Reign of his Master and what the spirit was to inspire him to say to the Churches. Ah, it must be said that the spirit he received was not the spirit of adoption and liberty, but the spirit of servitude, of timidity, and of fear; since he acts today as a coward and a timid man. What constancy can the members of a party have when the Leader abandons them so cowardly? What perseverance can they promise themselves in the knowledge of their so-called truths, if their Conductor falls into a most shameful Apostasy <note>The formal abandonment of a religious or political belief.</note>? If he was not sent by God as a Precursor <note>One who precedes and indicates the approach of another; here, Labadie claimed to be a new John the Baptist announcing a new era of the Church.</note>, why did he formerly dispute with such impetuosity against our Synods <note>Regional governing bodies of the Reformed Church.</note> over this Privilege? And if he was sent by God as a Precursor, why is he so shamefully silent today out of consideration for a few Magistrates? *If I yet pleased men*, said St. Paul, *I should no longer be the servant of Jesus Christ*. But if he has so much deference for the threats of a human order against his revealed truths, what has become of *John of Jesus Christ*? What has become of the *Great Herald of King* JESUS? What has become of the *Pastor par Excellence Catholic or Universal Monsieur Jean de Labadie*?

And indeed, he is the most presumptuous man in the world, the proudest, the most ambitious, the haughtiest, the vainest: which he has shown in all his actions and in all his steps. He does this so artlessly that one can apply to him the word of that Ancient: *Good gods, how absurd he is, how much he loves himself without any rival* <note>Original Latin: *Dii boni quam ineptus, quam fese amans sine rivali*. He could never tolerate those of his Colleagues who equaled him in talent, and much less those who surpassed him; and when he saw in our Synods young Pastors who gave hope of becoming, under the blessing of God, greater lights in the Church than his own, he tried to suppress their gifts by treating them as "youth," although this Venerable Old Man had perhaps not known the purity of the Gospel for as long as they had had the honor of preaching it. And so that the Reader may have some samples quite fit to recognize the proud and haughty humor of Monsieur <term>Sieur</term><note>A title of respect, similar to "Sir" or "Mister," often used for gentlemen or professionals in the 17th century.</note> de Labadie, it is only right that, through a short digression, I first treat them to what passed between him and Monsieur des Marets, Pastor at Delft, a few months ago. It happened that the latter, being asked by one of his friends to inquire of Monsieur de Labadie regarding the conduct of a young French boy who had had the honor of playing a part in his illustrious Seminary, and who by necessity was reduced in Delft to accepting the position of a valet. Des Marets, who had never had any particular quarrel with Labadie, and who, outside of the general interest in the affairs of the Synod <note>An assembly of church delegates; here referring to the governing bodies of the Dutch Reformed Church which were then in conflict with Labadie.</note>, had no other motive than to treat him with civility, did not hesitate to write him a letter to this effect dated February 22 of this year, of which he judged it appropriate to keep a Copy; here is how it read:

> *A young boy named Louis de Friche, who brought us an attestation signed by your hand on August 28 of last year, and who claims to have had the honor of staying in your house for four or five months, being*

> *on the point of entering service with Persons of Piety and honor in this city; these same Persons have entreated me to inquire of you regarding his conduct, and to learn from you how he behaved in your household. Furthermore, Sir and Most Honored Brother, I am deeply grieved that matters have been driven to such great extremes. If possible, do what you can on your part to restore peace to the Church, which, at a time when it needs so much Reformation, sees itself on the point of feeling its own entrails torn apart by disputes and counter-disputes. If I alone were capable of stopping the impetuosity of the Torrent with which we are threatened, rest assured that I would heartfully sacrifice both my honor and my life to the glory of such a holy work. As long as I see no more inclination toward this from the side of men, I shall continue to offer my prayers to God, that He might reunite spirits, and that, emerging from the machinery of His Providence, He might strengthen the Peace of His dear Church; it is to His holy grace that I recommend you, and I am with respect,*

> ->*Sir and Most Honored Brother,*<-

The response to this letter was so unworthy, and written with as much contempt as you are about to see, that Mr. <term>Sieur</term> des Marets, after having read it, did nothing but shrug his shoulders, marvelling at the foolish pride of a man who did not even deign to address him as "brother" (a sure proof that from then on his Schism <note>A formal split or division within a church; Labadie was increasingly distancing himself from the Dutch Reformed Church to start his own community.</note> was on the verge of breaking out) and who responded to his civilities only with *et ceteras*. He has spirit and honor, and as I believe, also piety, at least enough of it appeared while he was among us. He paints well, and binds*

We wish that whoever has erred might recognize it; as for us, we know well that we have not done so, nor have we done any wrong. We shall not prevent anyone from repairing all the harm that has been done, and we pray God that He puts this into the hearts of all those who may have had a hand in it. I am,*

Since those who, in regard to your office, ought to be your colleagues, can no longer maintain moderation with you, and since after they show you civility, you treat them with *et ceteras*; I have advised Monsieur des Marets <note>Likely Henri des Marets, a minister in Delft who initially welcomed Labadie but later became a fierce opponent.</note>, whom you treat in this manner, not to reply to you; and as I am his good friend, I have willingly taken on this burden, to have the occasion to tell you in a few words some of the truths about you. # Curious History

<page-num>100</page-num>

<meta>catchword: trait</meta>

Since it is so satisfying to take down a conceited man, we do not know if Jean de Labadie, the Pastor, boasted of having received it <note>Referring to the mocking response mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Another example of his unbearable pride—to which all those in Geneva who have seen his study can testify—is the arrangement of a Portrait, the most impious and profane imaginable. This portrait, which adorns his room, represents the person of Monsieur de Labadie with the criminal detail that, above his head, one sees a Dove in the form of the Holy Spirit, along with these words from the Prophet *Isaiah 61: The Spirit of the Lord God is upon me* <note>Original French: *l'Eſprit du Seigneur l'Eternel eſt ſur moy*</note>. This shows that this Minister of JESUS CHRIST placed himself so much on par with his Master that he believed the prediction of the extraordinary outpouring of the Holy Spirit, which the Prophet promised to the Messiah, was applicable to himself. Such is the extent to which this man believes himself filled with the Spirit of God, though he is inflated only by the spirit of pride, vanity, and lies. However, the encounter he had on this subject with a young Dutch <term>Proposant</term> <note>A "Proposant" was a candidate for the ministry in the Reformed Church who had completed his studies but was not yet ordained.</note>, who was then one of his friends, is too excellent not to be recounted. It happened that while this young man was in Labadie’s study with him, and as the latter showed him his portrait and asked if he recognized the original, the young man—who did not have the best eyesight and who, moreover, spent more time considering the pigeon than examining the face—replied artlessly, "It is," he said, "the por-

<column-break/>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>of Monsieur Jean de Labadie.</header>
<sig>E 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

-trait of Muhammad." <note>The young man mistakes the dove of the Holy Spirit for the dove often associated with Muhammad in Western polemics of the era, which claimed a dove whispered "revelations" into the Prophet's ear.</note> "No," Labadie said to him, "it is mine." And perhaps they were both right, since he might well pass for another Muhammad by wanting to pass for an extraordinary Prophet.

Another great flaw familiar to him is that he rarely brings himself to speak well of anyone, especially any Pastor, however excellent they may be. This is proven by his various treatises in which he makes it his business to blacken their names as much as he can, putting the Churches at risk of having, after JESUS CHRIST, only one single good Pastor in the world: Monsieur Jean de Labadie, Pastor. But to all his slanders, one can respond in general with what Diogenes said to a man who slandered Plato: *"Easy now, my friend; just as it is not reasonable to believe a man when he speaks well of you, neither is it just that you should be believed when you speak ill of him."* <note>Original French: *Tout beau mon amy comme il n'eſt pas raiſonnable qu'on le croye quand il dit du bien de toy, auſſi n'eſt-il pas juſte que tu ſois creu quand tu dis du mal de luy.*</note>

He is so little <term>mortified</term> <note>In 17th-century religious terms, "mortification" refers to the practice of self-denial and subduing physical desires to grow spiritually.</note>, though he wishes to appear very much so, that there is nothing he loves more than his own flesh and himself. The slightest headache he has puts his entire Seminary in an alarm; they run for remedies everywhere for fear that this "new saint" should suffer too much on earth. They do what they can to drive away this cruel enemy <note>The headache.</note> which often only touches the tips of his hair, even when he believes his head is being carried away—without considering that the mere name of this "enemy" has often gotten him out of trouble.

# Curious History

<page-num>102</page-num>

<meta>catchword: is</meta>

...and that a well-timed migraine (for there are, in his view, both good and bad ones) has sometimes preserved the health of his reputation. It is certain that he pampers himself in a hundred ways, and that he takes no small care of the "casing" of his Mind and soul. This is evident whether he is taking a carriage, in which he quite effectively plays the part of the *Most Eminent invalid* <note>A mocking reference to the titles of Cardinals (Eminence), suggesting Labadie acts with the self-importance of a high-ranking Catholic prelate even while pretending to be ill.</note>—and where, while giving blessings to passers-by, he might say as that other Cardinal did when making his entry into Paris: *Since the world wants to be deceived, let it be deceived* <note>Original Latin: *Quandoquidem mundus vult decipi decipiatur*. This phrase is famously attributed to Cardinal Carlo Carafa regarding the people of Paris.</note>—or whether he is using meat essences and broths to repair the exhaustion of his Pastoral physicality. He preaches austerity to others, but reserves delicacy for himself, making himself in this regard like the fruit of the Chestnut trees, which hide a soft down under a rough and prickly shell. For his virtue, entirely Pharisaical <note>Referring to the Pharisees of the New Testament, frequently criticized for outward piety that masks inner hypocrisy.</note>, is austere in public and when he recommends it from the Pulpit, but it is entirely self-indulgent in private; it is all thorns and stings for others, but on the contrary, it is all silk and feathers for himself.

He is the most notorious meddler one could ever speak of, having spent his entire life doing nothing but changing his status, because he was never able to approve of any condition or resolve to live peacefully. He has passed through all the orders of the Roman Church, and has

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>of Master Jean de Labadie.</header>

<meta>catchword: let</meta>

never left one without causing disorder. He served three Churches in France, but upon leaving them, he left them a testament quite contrary to the one JESUS CHRIST left to his disciples, since he left them his division <note>A play on the Gospel of John 14:27, where Jesus says "Peace I leave with you."</note>. It was not only the Church that he disturbed, but he even wished to disturb the State, had it been possible for him. This was seen not only in France, by advising the people to rise up against their King, but even by addressing himself one day to a great Minister of State in this Country during the time our Provinces were engaged in that disastrous war against England <note>Likely referring to the Second Anglo-Dutch War (1665–1667).</note>. He came to announce an important secret, which was *that he held in his hand the means to set fire to the four corners of the Kingdom.* Perhaps this was through his old Masters, the Jesuits, or through his new Brothers, the Quakers <note>The author is attempting to link Labadie to various groups perceived as radical or subversive at the time.</note>. This was a proposition that horrified that wise Minister and obliged him to sharply reject this counselor of fire and blood, who should have been a Herald of grace and peace rather than a Trumpet of Fire and Carnage. In my opinion, that Illustrious politician should have treated him in the same manner that King Henry IV treated his tailor. That man, having taken it into his head that he could regulate all the manufacturing in France, had a treatise printed on the subject, and presenting it to His Majesty, said, *“That he knew neither how to read nor write, but desiring nonetheless to be of service, he had composed this book.”* The King instantly commanded his va—

[his] valet de chambre to fetch Monsieur de Sully <note>Maximilien de Béthune, Duke of Sully (1560–1641), the loyal chief minister to King Henry IV of France, famous for fixing the kingdom's finances.</note>, the Superintendent of his finances. When he arrived, the King said to him, *take my measurement for a suit, since my tailor is meddling in managing my finances*. In the same way, if that excellent Statesman had sent for a Colonel of some Regiment, he might have ordered him to deliver a Sermon the next day, since Labadie was meddling in inventing ruses and stratagems of war. He should have considered that he was called to save souls through the ministry of reconciliation, and not to cause them to perish, and that *the weapons of our warfare are not carnal* <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 10:4: "For the weapons of our warfare are not carnal, but mighty through God to the pulling down of strong holds."</note>; all the war he should have advised was that against vice.

But how could the good man always remember to save the souls of others, when he sometimes takes so little care of his own? He imagines that in the war his soul wages against vice, it is clad in a breastplate so proofed against all temptations of the flesh that even if he were to pollute his hands with sacrilege, his soul would be no more wounded or soiled for it. And so that one may know how much the Theology of this "honest man"—this former Jesuit—is *accommodating*, and how well he understands the *doctrine of probability* <term>Probabilism: a Roman Catholic moral system, often associated with the Jesuits and criticized by their opponents, which suggested that if an opinion was "probable," one could follow it even if the opposite was more certain.</term> of his former Brothers, before we finish the history of his conduct among us, it is right that we recount what happened at Middelburg in the year 1667, according to the au-

It is that two very respectable persons, named *Charles Gilles* and *Catherine Puttenaer*, husband and wife, resolved after the death of their daughter, as an act of Christian charity, to bequeath for the benefit of the poor of the Walloon Church <note>The Walloon Church consisted of French-speaking Protestant congregations in the Netherlands, often formed by refugees from the Spanish Netherlands and France.</note> of Middelburg a sum of twenty silver ducatons <note>A ducaton was a large silver coin used in the 17th century, of significant value.</note>. Now, it so happens that Monsieur de Labadie—who understands *manual retentions* no less than he does *mental retentions* <note>A satirical play on "mental reservation," a Jesuit practice of holding back part of the truth while speaking. Because, even if he did not rank himself alongside *Ananias* and *Sapphira* <note>A Biblical couple in the Book of Acts who were struck dead by God for withholding part of a donation to the early church while pretending to give the full amount.</note>, he nevertheless believed that without fear of being struck down by Heaven, he could in good conscience subtract exactly half of the payment brought to the feet of the poor; and without wounding his soul, maintain the conscience of an ostrich, capable of digesting ten good silver ducatons all at once. Whatever he might say for himself—whether he ranked himself among the poor and thus, in that capacity, faithfully shared the cake with them, or whether he...

[he] held to the devil’s fine maxim, that *well-ordered charity begins with oneself*; he always believed that he could celebrate once more, for the last time in his life, that Feast of his old Calendar, which is that of the *Finding of the Holy Cross* <note>original: "l’Invention de S. Croix." The author is making a biting pun. While "Invention" refers to the liturgical feast celebrating the "finding" of the True Cross by St. Helena, it also implies that Labadie is "finding" (stealing) money or "inventing" falsehoods.</note>.

I could say more if he himself had not just betrayed his sincerity, his piety, and his zeal in the most malicious and impudent piece of writing that could ever come from the hand of a Schismatic <note>A "Schismatic" is someone who promotes a "schism"—a formal split or division within a church, usually considered a grave sin by the religious authorities of the time.</note>. It is phrased in such atrocious terms that it alone is enough to have him deposed from his ministry, even if he were guilty of no other crime. And because all the venom seems to be contained in the tail of this scorpion, we have reserved the modest and necessary censure of this poisoned *Declaration* as the most authentic and strongest piece of evidence. This serves to show all people who love the Truth, Piety, and the Union of the Church of JESUS CHRIST, that Labadie is the most turbulent, the most proud, and the most impious of all men—if it be true that *the wicked man is never worse than when he counterfeits a man of virtue*; and that *the most dangerous wound the Church can receive is that of Schism*.

Let us examine the Document itself which advises this Schism, and in which Labadie speaks for a number of persons of his faction; it bears this title: *Declaration*, etc.

No one can be surprised that Jean de Labadie—possessed for a long time by **Enthusiasm** <note>In the 17th century, "Enthusiasm" was a derogatory term for the belief that one received direct, private revelations from God, bypassing the established Church and Scripture.</note> and the spirit of **Montanus** <note>A 2nd-century leader who claimed to be a prophet; the author is comparing Labadie to ancient heretics to delegitimize him.</note>, as we have just proven—has found in this country certain ***Quintillas*** and ***Priscillas*** <note>In the early church, Quintilla and Priscilla were famous prophetesses and followers of Montanus. The author uses their names here as a pejorative labels for Labadie's female supporters.</note> who have embraced his interests and taken up his cause with fervor; and that, following both his natural temperament and his design, he has finally sounded the trumpet, like ***Sheba*** the son of ***Bichri*** (2 Samuel 20:6) <note>A biblical figure who led a revolt against King David; the author likens Labadie’s departure from the church to a violent rebellion.</note>, and raised the flag of his premeditated Schism. He intends to make himself known to posterity and to force those who compile the list of heretics and Sectarians of these recent times to insert the ***Labadists*** therein. 

We shall not repeat the history here, neither of all his irregularities in all our Ecclesiastical Assemblies since his arrival in this country; nor of his defiant violation of the orders he had accepted, and of the very authority of the Lords of the States of **Zeeland** at the **Synod of Flushing**, where our Churches made an extraordinary effort of charity and condescension to bring him back, if possible, to his duty; nor of the deceptions, disguises, and **Equivocations** <note>The use of ambiguous language to conceal the truth, a tactic often historically attributed by Protestants to the Jesuits, whom Labadie had formerly been a part of.</note>...

Jesuitical tactics, with which he has often tried to deceive us; nor of his just deposition <note>Removal from his position as a pastor.</note> at the last Synod of Dordrecht; founded primarily on the resolution he had taken to separate himself and his followers from our Communion, and to place himself in the ranks of those *who form a separate sect to break the union of the Church*, who excommunicate themselves from our own, according to our Liturgy, every time we take communion in the body and blood of JESUS CHRIST, in the holy Sacrament of the Eucharist. # Modest
<page-num>116</page-num>

...by his holy grace, pure and clear in our Churches. *Lord, to whom shall we go but to thee? thou hast the words of eternal life.* <note>original: "Joan. 6. 68." — A citation of John 6:68, where Peter affirms his loyalty to Jesus.</note>

We shall pass over in silence an expression in this title that reeks more of the vintage of **Socinus** <note>Faustus Socinus was a 16th-century theologian whose followers, Socinians, rejected the Trinity. In this context, calling someone "Socinian" was a serious accusation of heresy.</note> than of the soil of the Scriptures, in which we find indeed that the *Church* is called sometimes the *Church of God*, and sometimes the *Church of JESUS CHRIST*. But it is never called jointly *the Church of God and of JESUS CHRIST*. A poet such as Labadie is permitted to abound in such <term>pleonasms</term> <note>The use of more words than are necessary to convey meaning; redundancy.</note>. However, it must be noted that he is so ashamed of his **Schism**, that he tries to cover its infamy at the beginning of his *Declaration*. For instead of saying that he wishes to present the reasons and motives that oblige him to break with the **Walloon Churches** <note>French-speaking Protestant churches in the Netherlands, distinct from the Dutch-speaking ones.</note> of this country and to abandon their communion—having no further connection with them in Faith, Order, Worship, or Discipline (which is how he should have spoken to call things by their true names)—he contents himself with saying he wishes to produce them *as if obliged thereby to have no communion with the so-called Walloon Synod*. He does this to hide himself behind the ambiguities of his former trade. All the **Flemish Churches** of this country have no communion with the Walloon Synod regarding <term>Ecclesiastical Policy</term> <note>The administrative and legal governance of the church.</note>; they govern themselves by their own Classes and Synods, just as the Walloon Churches

<column-break/>

# Refutation.
<page-num>117</page-num>

do by theirs. Yet they nevertheless share a communion of Faith, Worship, and Charity with ours, insofar as they hold to the same Confession of Faith and govern themselves by the rules of the same Discipline. Indeed, the Flemish recognize the Walloons as their Sisters, and as members of the same National Synod alongside them. There are even some Walloon assemblies or Churches in this country that have no communion with the Walloon Synod regarding discipline and external government—such as those in Wesel, Emden, Groningen, and Bergen op Zoom—yet they share communion in Doctrine with those that compose and depend upon the Walloon Synod. 

In contrast, Labadie wishes to allege reasons that oblige him to no longer have any communion in Doctrine, Service, Liturgy, or Discipline with the Churches that make up the Walloon Synod. These reasons, consequently, cause him to break on all these points with all the **Reformed Churches** of the country; to withdraw himself from the jurisdiction of their National Synod (which is composed only of the Flemish and Walloon Churches); and to no longer be among the number of Reformed Churches publicly maintained and approved in the **United Provinces** <note>The Dutch Republic.</note>. Behold, then, the "advantage" he procures for those who might be so ill-advised as to adhere to him: he makes them pass, at best, for tolerated sectarians, and places them in the rank of a...

### Modest Refutation.
<page-num>118, 119</page-num>

...of a handful of mutineers, who have torn up their banner to follow an *Eletto* <note>An *Eletto* was a leader chosen by mutinous Spanish soldiers in the 16th and 17th centuries; the author uses this to compare Labadie to a rebel military commander.</note>, and submit themselves to him. Furthermore, he cannot reasonably expect, by separating from the communion of the **Walloon Synod**, to be tolerated along with his followers in any city of the country where there is already a Walloon Church, at least under that same name; for this would only cause trouble among the citizens and establish two parties, similar to those of **Paulinus** and **Meletius**, who once troubled the Church of Antioch for so long. <note>Paulinus and Meletius were rival bishops during the 4th-century Schism of Antioch, a famous conflict over who was the legitimate leader of the church.</note> And even assuming that Labadie raised new recruits in Amsterdam for his **Schism**, in order to form them into a body of the Church—just as **Tertullian** said that *heretics also build churches, just as wasps make honeycombs in imitation of bees*—his society could never pass for a Walloon Church, but at most for a gathering of Independents, Fanatics, and **Quakers**.

And above all, one discovers in this schismatic resolution the weakness and lack of judgment of the one who took it. For even if he were to seduce a small number of people from our Churches to attach them to himself, he could not in good conscience retain nor exercise among them the title of Pastor, in which he continues very inappropriately to glory. For as it was noted above from **Saint Cyprian**, he who separates himself from the unity of the Church by Schism loses, *by that very fact* <note>original: "ipso facto"</note>, the status of Bishop, and can no longer perform its functions. 

Besides, Labadie, convicted of Schism before having publicly raised its banner, has been legally deposed from his Ministry and legitimately degraded. According to the oldest Canons of the primitive Church, by this deposition he is *restored to lay communion* <term>lay communion</term> <note>A disciplinary state where a former clergyman is stripped of his office and rank, permitted only to participate in the church as an ordinary layperson.</note>, as Antiquity puts it, so as to be able to commune only among the laity—should the Synod lift the suspension of the Holy Supper which it has forbidden him—without restoring him to his Ministry. 

For if, upon leaving the Roman Church without having been either suspended or degraded, he nevertheless had to receive a new ordination in our Churches according to their discipline—his pretended priestly dignity or "Papistic" priesthood, received at **Bazas**, being no longer of use to him at **Montauban** <note>Labadie was ordained as a Jesuit priest in Bazas but converted to Protestantism in Montauban in 1650.</note>—much less could the privilege of having exercised the holy Ministry at **Geneva** or **Middelburg** serve him for the erection of a new Church, entirely separate from ours, after having been justly stripped of that character in our communion and reduced to the condition of the simple common people, that is to say, of those called *Idiots* or private persons, of whom it is spoken in 1 Corinthians 14:16. <note>In the 17th century, "idiot" (from the Greek *idiotes*) referred to a private individual or a layperson without formal education, rather than its modern derogatory meaning.</note> If he built the undertaking of this new Ministry upon the *indelibility of the character* <note>original: "l'indelebilité du Caractere"</note> of the Priesthood in the judgment of the Papacy, he would be leaning on a ruinous foundation, and one which he has renounced by...

### Modest Refutation
<page-num>120</page-num>
<header>Modeste</header>

...his reordination at Montauban <note>Montauban was a major center for French Protestantism where Labadie was formally received after leaving the Catholic Church.</note>: Besides the fact that, according to the Canons of the Roman Church, if one is suspended from *holy orders* <note>original: "sacris"</note> or defrocked, he would preach and administer the Sacraments *validly* (as they say), but not *lawfully* <note>original: "legitimement"</note> because of his irregularity. If he also wished to set up "altar against altar" <note>A traditional expression for creating a rival, schismatic church.</note> and undertake a new Ministry in a new flock—which could in no way restore to him what he has lost among us—under the pretext that his deposition was done by *an erring key* <note>original: "clave errante"; a theological term referring to a church authority making a mistake in excommunication or discipline</note>, contrary to the Canons and the proper forms; beyond the fact that he would be making himself Judge in his own cause, he could and should have for this purpose presented a petition to the Sovereign Magistrate, according to the practice of the ancient Church, of which we have examples in the case of **Saint Athanasius** and several others. He could have alleged that the **Walloon Synod** <note>The governing body of French-speaking Reformed churches in the Netherlands.</note> had proceeded *abusively* against him in his deposition, and requested that the review of his case be committed to some other Synod of the country to be judged according to the Canons of the Church and the rules of the Word of God. In that case, our Company would have had no difficulty in submitting its procedure to the test and the censure of another Ecclesiastical body, according to the principle that *the spirits of the Prophets are subject to the Prophets* <note>original: "les esprits des Prophetes sont sujets aux Prophetes"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 14:32, implying that religious leaders must submit to the judgment of their peers.</note>. But our **Independent** <note>A term used here pejoratively to describe someone who rejects the hierarchical authority of the Reformed synods.</note>, who wishes to recognize no power, whether Ecclesiastical or civil, above himself, took great care not to take

---

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>Refutation.</header>

take this path. Because by following it, he would have blocked his own path to the **Schism** that he has always been plotting, and would not have been able to give birth to this monster, conceived long ago, with which he was quite "pregnant" upon arrival.

But let us examine the alleged reasons with which he wishes to color his **Schism**, and see how he begins: *He founds it on a strange corruption of the Walloon Synod—that is to say, of all the Churches of that body—in three significant Heads, which he calls, three lines later, in his Monastic jargon, three significant corruptions and breaches made in three remarkable points or parts of the Church and the house of God.* As if in this matter one had to make a distinction between the Church and the house of God; for that would be done by a Theology as bad as one must be an absurd Philosopher to treat "points" and "parts" in a building as the same thing. Now, our man protests that he will remain a Schismatic with his followers, if he finds any, *so long as the Synod does not reform its errors, its abuses, and its excesses in all of them.* The Reader must guess who these *all of them* are, in whom the Synod must reform *its Errors, its Abuses, and its Excesses*, and what difference one should perceive between these three things. This is speaking with little clarity for a man who prides himself on speaking well, and who formerly resided at **Port Royal** <note>A famous center of Jansenist Catholic thought in France; the author uses this to mock Labadie's intellectual pedigree.</note>. He attempts to express them through the corruption of the Synod: 1. *In Faith and in...*

*doctrine. 2. In its societies or assemblies. 3. Now, to better emphasize his *Trident* <note>original: "Trident"; likely referring to a three-pronged argument or a specific work by Labadie referencing the Council of Trent</note>, he wants us to notice that *for these three significant corruptions, our fathers and we have left the Roman communion, and have excommunicated the Pope and his Councils or Synods, somewhat before and at the very least better than they had and have excommunicated us.* A thought that he wants to be taken for a star of the first magnitude in the Heaven of his discourse, since he has marked it with a *Nota Bene* in the margin. For this is one of his usual impertinences in his ridiculous writings, that he fills all the margins without judgment with this mark N. B. <note>Latin: "Nota Bene" or "Note Well"</note> to serve as a leaden weight for his flimsy cloth <note>original: "plomb à sa tirtaine"; a metaphor for trying to give weight or value to something cheap and poorly made</note>, and as a warning to the sellers of pepper & tobacco of the place where they should tear the pages to wrap their merchandise. mark his ignorance, & to condemn his entire proceeding: His ignorance appears in the deduction of the causes of our separation from Rome, and of the manner in which it was done. Our Fathers, who were still living in the Communion of Rome, requested the Reformation of the intolerable errors & abuses that had crept into Doctrine, Worship, & Discipline. Instead of yielding to their complaints, violence was done to their consciences, and there was exercised against them not only very vicious Ecclesiastical censures, but also very cruel persecutions, which made mountains of their ashes and rivers of their blood; So much so that *Babylon not having wanted to be healed*, Jerem. 51. 9. <note>Jeremiah 51:9; here "Babylon" is used as a traditional Protestant metaphor for the Roman Catholic Church</note> but having proscribed, burned, hanged, broken on the wheel, and massacred those who wanted to bind her wounds, good people were constrained to leave, according to the order God gave them in Apoc. 18. 4. <note>Revelation 18:4: "Come out of her, my people, so that you will not share in her sins..."</note> The Excommunications that Rome has thundered, & thunders every year against us, are known to everyone, and the Anathemas <note>Formal curses or excommunications</note> of the Council of Trent can be read in its decrees. that the Protestants had pronounced solemnly and as a body against the Pope and his Councils, before they had been Excommunicated by them: Luther did not burn the body of Canon Law at Wittenberg, until after Pope Leo X had condemned his books to the fire, and fulminated his bull *Exurge* <note>Latin: *Exsurge Domine* ("Arise, O Lord"), the 1520 papal bull condemning Luther's theses</note> against him. Thus, our separation from Rome cannot be accused of Schism on our part, because it is founded first upon the execrable Idolatry practiced in so many Ways <note>original: "Chefs"; here meaning categories or points of practice</note> in the Roman Communion, from which no one who belongs to it can free themselves. ### Modest
<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>Modest</header>
<meta>catchword: a</meta>

\* *The Old Man of the Mountain* <note>The leader of the Order of Assassins, used here as a metaphor for a fanatical or dangerous leader</note>, set against Babylon and the Antichrist, spares the Reformed as little as the Idolater, or the Evangelical Psalter as little as the Roman Missal and Breviary. Either he apparently retains his secret understandings with Rome—whose austere mortifications, ardent devotions, generous renunciations of the world, and solitary institutes he so often praises—or he intends, through this infamous comparison of our Churches with Rome, to suggest something else. By comparing the just and necessary reasons good people have for leaving Rome's communion with the reasons our Fathers had for renouncing the Pope, he would have it understood underhandedly that we were wrong to leave Babylon and to have wanted to form a separate group. He implies that in fleeing the smoke, we have fallen into the fire, and that it has happened to us as it does to the sick who, by changing beds, do not change their disease but carry it with them; and consequently, that it would be better to return from whence we came. And even if Labadie did not have this specific aim here, he at least provides our adversaries with the means to condemn our separation from them as

<margin>
\* The *Old Man of the Mountain*, famous under this name in the History of the Eastern wars for the recovery of the Holy Land. He was the Prince of the Assassins (and from there the name originated), who were so blindly attached to his obedience that, upon the command to go kill such and such a King, in his Palace or in the midst of his troops, they would go determinedly and kill him, or perish there.
</margin>

<sig>F 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

a shameful betrayal of the cause of God. But whatever his design was in this comparison, he brings trial against himself; he resembles a blind man who trips over his own staff instead of using it to support himself. For it is quite strange that, having hardly had the leisure to properly recognize in two and a half years the *three corruptions* of which he accuses our Churches—and upon which he bases his "just causes" for separating from them—he took flight so quickly. He did so without seeking to deliver the Churches from these corruptions or to preserve himself within them, either by communicating on this subject with the other Churches of the country and imploring their charitable mediation (which they would not have refused him), or by addressing the Sovereign Magistrates as the nursing fathers <note>A biblical term (Isaiah 49:23) often used to describe civil rulers who protect and support the Church</note> of the Churches, protectors of their Canons, and guardians of the two tables of the law. On the contrary, he spent forty or fifty years in the Communion of Rome, exercising and practicing the most detestable superstitions. Even then, he was "enlightened" enough to have a clear and distinct knowledge of Rome's *three great corruptions*, yet he did not think in any way of leaving her. Rather, he sought every possible means to remain there, justifying himself and clearing himself as much as he could from the suspicions formed against him in the Civil and Ecclesiastical Courts regarding Syncretism <note>The reconciliation or fusion of different beliefs or parties</note> and collusion with us. His escapades were motivated more by a hatred of the Jesuits than by an affection for the

the truth, doing everything he could to be esteemed a most zealous Papist and a great adversary of the Huguenots. And he would still be within the Roman Communion, making God there with five words <note>original: "cinq paroles." This refers to the Latin formula of consecration in the Mass: "Hoc est enim corpus meum" (For this is my body), which Catholics believe transforms the bread into the body of Christ.</note> by virtue of his Pastoral Priesthood (for in his declaration he claims to have been made a Pastor in the Church of JESUS CHRIST; but without having any flock there, when he was made a Priest of Baal and a Mass-making Priest,) worshipping there on the Altar the God Maozim <note>A biblical reference from Daniel 11:38 to a "god of fortresses," frequently used by Protestant polemicists to describe the consecrated host as an idol.</note>, incensing and kissing the cross, saying the office, and generally performing all the antics of the most devout Papistry, if he had not caught wind that he was about to be flushed out of Toulouse; such that it was not at all the *three corruptions* of Rome that made him throw his frock to the nettles <note>An idiom meaning to abandon the religious life or the priesthood.</note>; he had a stomach strong enough to digest them without troubling his conscience, and he could nourish himself without danger on this poison like Mithridates <note>A king of Pontus in antiquity who supposedly made himself immune to poisons by taking gradually increasing doses.</note>. But the sole fear of Canonical punishments, of degradation and of being thrown into the bottom of a pit prepared for all his extravagances, forced him to seek safety in the arms of the Reformed. It must then be acknowledged that it is either the spirit of the devil that now makes him quit our Communion so precipitously, or it was the spirit of the world that kept him for so long in that of Rome.

This reasoning is confirmed by what Labadie adds: that he does not wish to impute to the *Walloons* <note>The Walloons were French-speaking Protestants in the Netherlands who formed their own Reformed congregations.</note>,

Certainly, it would be very difficult to guess what Labadie believes concerning the Holy Supper, and within it, the presence of JESUS CHRIST, since in his first declaration at Montauban, he made no mention of our free justification before God, nor of Papal Idolatry and Artolatry <note>A polemical term meaning "bread-worship," accusing Catholics of worshipping the physical material of the Eucharist.</note>, nor of the presence of the body of JESUS CHRIST in the Sacrament. He attached himself only to the trivialities <note>original: "la petite oye," literally the giblets or trimmings of a goose, used here to mean minor or peripheral superstitions.</note> of some popular Roman superstitions, just as in this country he has focused only on sins such as pearls, ribbons, furniture, ornaments, and clothes, without attacking the body of the matter. But since he admits that the errors, abuses, and excesses he calumniously imputes to us are well below those of Rome, he is quite wrong to claim that such similar causes should produce similar effects. One should not quit the society and conversation of a man who has a wart on his face, or a boil on the tip of his finger, simply because one avoids conversing with someone who is defiled by leprosy or infected with the plague. # Modest
<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>Modeste</header>
<meta>catchword: de</meta>

to seek among men religious and ecclesiastical societies where there is no error, nor some abuse and excess, is to seek what God himself does not find there. And if Labadie were not infected by the error of the *Cathars*, the *Donatists*, the *Novatians*, the *Pelagians*, and the *Anabaptists* <note>These are various historical groups—some from the early church, others from the Reformation era—that the author identifies as "purists" who sought to separate from the main church because they believed it was not "pure" enough.</note>, he would not wish to desire a Church on earth that is without stain and without blemish, since such a thing will only be found in Heaven. And he ought to apply to himself what Constantine the Great said to Acesius, a Bishop of the Novatians, who was preaching in favor of his schismatics against the errors and abuses found among the Orthodox: *“Prepare for yourself, O Acesius, a ladder to Heaven, to climb up there alone.”* <note>original: "Dresse toy, ô Acesius, une échelle au Ciel, pour y monter seul." This famous anecdote from the Council of Nicaea suggests that those who demand a perfectly pure church end up entirely alone.</note> And as all sorts of errors are not capital, and do not overturn or sap the foundation, so too they do not mandate separation; Saint Paul did not preach schism to the Corinthians, even though there were even *heresies among them*, 1 Cor. 11:19. And Saint Augustine does not want one to separate from the ordinary Pastors even though they err and are mistaken, as long as they maintain the bond of union and charity. It is not reasonable for the good grain to leave the threshing floor because it is found there mixed with straw; the silver of the Church is never so refined in this world that it does not retain some dross, nor the Moon so clear and full of light that it does not keep

It is because Our Lords the **States General** <note>The governing body of the Dutch Republic.</note> having seen fit that the Acts of the **National Synod of Dordrecht** <note>Also known as the Synod of Dort (1618–1619), a major international gathering that settled theological disputes in the Reformed Church.</note> should be translated into Dutch and French, for the use of the Churches of both languages; the one who produced the printed French translation at Leiden in the year 1624, **Sieur Richard Jean de Nerée**, a Minister in the army, did not follow our old manuscript in the Confession—which was restricted to its own specific use—but rather the official Copy of the **Synod of Dort**. Thus, **Labadie** <note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674), a former Jesuit who became a radical Protestant leader and eventually formed his own separatist sect.</note>, who approves of this Copy, is quite wrong to accuse them of error in the Faith, since the French Confession of the National Synod of Dort is also theirs. several respects, and which goes as far as the most subtle Nestorianism <note>A heresy named after Nestorius which suggested that the divine and human natures of Christ were two separate persons rather than being joined in one person.</note>. Thus, in Article 19, through an evident error and a stupid ignorance of true Theology, he objected that the two natures in JESUS CHRIST are described there as being so united in personal unity that they *were not even separated by his death*. For this truth is so evident in Scripture, and is proven so clearly in the same article, that this maxim of John of Damascus <note>An influential 7th-century monk and theologian.</note>—which Labadie should have at least learned from Becan and Suarez <note>Martin Becan and Francisco Suárez were famous Jesuit theologians; the author is reminding Labadie of his former training as a Jesuit.</note>, his masters—cannot be contradicted except by fools: that what the Son of God has once taken to himself, he never leaves: *What the Word once assumed, he never laid down* <note>original: "*Quod Verbum semel assumpsit nunquam deposuit*"</note>. The natural union of the body and the soul in JESUS CHRIST was indeed detached by his death, but not the hypostatic and personal union <term>hypostatic union: the theological doctrine that Christ is one person with two distinct natures, divine and human.</term> of his divine person with his human nature; for it was his human soul that he committed, while dying, into the hands of his Father, although separated from his body, Luke 23:46, and that body which lay in the tomb did not cease to be his body and the *Holy One of God who could not suffer corruption*. Ps. 16:10, Acts 2:31, and 13:35.

eternally in three persons according to their incommunicable properties <note>The unique characteristics that distinguish the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit from one another within the Trinity.</note>, namely the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. And that which is found in the *Body of Confessions* <note>A collection of Protestant statements of faith published in Geneva.</note>, printed in Geneva in the year 1612 in quarto, carries: *We believe in one single God (who is a single essence, truly distinct, in truth <note>original: "revera"</note>, and from all eternity in three persons by incommunicable properties), namely the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit*. But at heart, all these expressions come back to the same thing, and Labadie would be hard-pressed to find any contradiction there; for the article expresses itself clearly enough afterward when it says, *that such a distinction of persons does not mean that God is divided into three, insofar as Scripture teaches us that the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, each has his distinct subsistence <term>subsistence: a term used to describe the individual existence of the persons of the Trinity within the divine essence.</term> by his properties; so that, however, these three persons are but one single God*; it is therefore manifest that the Father is not the Son, and that the Son is not the Father; similarly, that the Holy Spirit is not the Father nor the Son; yet these persons, thus distinct,

<meta>This page continues the defense of the Reformed Confession against the attacks of Jean de Labadie. Having previously discussed Article 19 regarding the two natures of Christ, the author now transitions to Article 8, which concerns the doctrine of the Trinity and the distinction of the three Persons within the single divine Essence.</meta>

# Modest
<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>Modest</header>

are not divided, nor also confounded, nor mixed: for the Father did not take on flesh, nor did the Holy Spirit, but it was only the Son. Could one better or more clearly explain the truth of this mystery, in order to avoid the two pitfalls of *Sabellianism* <note>The belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not distinct persons, but merely different "modes" or masks of a single person.</note> and *Tritheism* <note>The heretical belief that the Trinity consists of three separate gods.</note>? 

But it is the usual artifice of Labadie, which he learned among the Jesuits, to detach a few words from the entire body of a discourse to attribute an offensive meaning to them—a meaning from which they are otherwise sufficiently protected by what precedes or what follows. This procedure is as contrary to good faith, to duty, and to the quality of an honest man—and to the rule of the Jurists <note>Legal scholars or experts in the law.</note>, that one must not pronounce judgment on a Law without having seen it in its entirety—as it is worthy of his character and his malice. 

Thus, he simply produces these words from our 8th article: *That the divine Essence is really and in truth distinct*; from which, expressed in this way, one can understand neither the intention nor the meaning. Instead, the text says that *we believe in one single God, who is a single Essence really, and truly, and eternally distinct into three persons.* Who does not see at once the iniquity of this man? For what our article seems to say about the Essence of God, it says properly of the true God alone in his essence; it does not suppose a real distinction of the divine Essence from the three persons who subsist in it—since there is no such distinction, and the Essence is not 

<meta>catchword: distin-</meta>

---

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>Refutation.</header>

distinguished from the persons except *by reason* <note>original: "*ratione*"; a philosophical term meaning a distinction made by the mind rather than a physical separation.</note>; but rather that this real or true distinction (that is to say, one which is not simply a *distinction of reason* <note>original: "*distinctio rationis*"</note>) exists between the three persons who subsist in this single and simple divine essence. 

Just as the divine Essence is common to the three persons, so too can one say in a certain sense that it is distinct in three persons—not by a distinction of a whole into its parts, nor of a species into its individuals, but by a distinction of the nature of God into his three manners of subsisting. And just as there is nothing so familiar among true Christians as to recognize and adore a single God distinguished into three persons, so too is there no harm in saying that the Essence of God—that is to say, God himself considered essentially—is distinguished into three persons. 

It is beyond controversy both that the three persons are in the divine essence through distinct manners of subsisting that are proper to each of them, and that the divine essence is in these three persons distinctly through their own manners of subsisting. Thus, Labadie has nothing in this first period that does not accord very well both with the Confession revised at Dordrecht <note>Refers to the Synod of Dort (1618–1619), which solidified international Reformed doctrine.</note> and with the Analogy of Faith <note>The principle that individual Bible passages should be interpreted in the light of the whole body of Christian doctrine.</note>. We find nothing more significant here than his extreme malice and his profound ignorance in wanting to impute error and heresy to our orthodox and innocent propositions.

<meta>catchword: Yes</meta>

<summary>The author defends Article 8 of the Reformed Confession against Jean de Labadie, arguing that the distinction of the Trinity is between the three Persons and not a division of the divine Essence itself. He accuses Labadie of using deceptive Jesuit-style tactics to misquote the text and manufacture a heresy where none exists.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the point-by-point defense of the Reformed Confession of Faith against Jean de Labadie. The previous page established that Labadie often takes phrases out of context to manufacture heresy. Here, the author defends the grammar and theology of Article 8, which defines the Trinity.</meta>

-># Modest<-
<page-num>144</page-num>
->### Refutation.<-

"Yes, but," he says, "this same 8th article declares, *'That the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit are distinct by its incommunicable properties (namely of the divine Essence) instead of saying that these are the three who are distinct by their incommunicable properties.'*" <note>Labadie is criticizing the grammar of the Confession, arguing that using "its" instead of "their" implies the properties belong to the Essence itself rather than the three distinct Persons.</note> 

But first, our article, as we have seen, also says the same: *'that the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit each have their own subsistence distinct by its properties;'* that is to say that each divine Person has its own <term>subsistence</term><note>In theology, 'subsistence' refers to an individual instance of a relative existence—how a Person of the Trinity exists as a distinct entity within the one Godhead.</note> and its personal manner of existing incommunicably from all eternity, truly and actually (without this distinction depending simply on our thought and our reasoning, as does the one by which the essence is distinguished from the persons), which is accomplished through the personal and <term>incommunicable properties</term><note>Characteristics unique to one Person of the Trinity that cannot be shared with the others, such as the Father being the 'unbegotten' source.</note> of each.

Moreover, this presumptuous ignorant, who boasts of nothing but <term>infused science</term><note>A claim to direct, supernatural knowledge from God that does not require study or tradition.</note>, did not know that the incommunicable properties, by which the three persons are distinguished in the Divine Essence, can be considered either *collectively* in regard to all the persons together; or *distributively* in regard to each of them taken and considered separately: Our Confession speaks of them differently in this double regard. It speaks of them *distributively* in the second part that we have cited, and which Labadie has maliciously concealed to create an opportunity to slander us; for

<column-break/>

it says in formal terms, that *each of the three persons has its subsistence distinct by its properties,* that is to say, by its own; the Father by not being begotten, and by begetting; the Son by not begetting, but by being begotten from all eternity; the Holy Spirit by being neither begetting nor begotten, but by proceeding from the Father and the Son. 

This is what makes it so, as our article explains, that the Father is not the Son, and that the Son is not the Father, as far as their persons are distinguished by their incommunicable properties, although as far as their singular, unique, and undivided Essence is concerned, the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit are but one. 

But our Confession, in the first part of this article speaking *collectively* of the three Persons who subsist in the Divine Essence, says that they are truly distinguished there by *its incommunicable properties.* Which our Visionary <note>A mocking term for Labadie, suggesting his claims of revelation are mere hallucinations or "visions."</note> not understanding, as "enlightened" as he believes himself to be, has turned into an erroneous and heretical expression without being able to say in what this alleged heresy consists, or by what name it should be called. 

Let him know then, and let him learn from us (though we are younger than he, and though it is his habit to despise the youth of several of our body), that just as the three Persons of the Holy Trinity are and can be called the Persons of the Divine Essence, also the incommunicable properties, 

<sig>G</sig>

He explains that the "incommunicable properties" of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit can be spoken of both collectively and individually without falling into heresy.</summary>

by which they all three subsist therein, actually distinguished from one another, also constitute and may be called the incommunicable properties of the Divine Essence. For these incommunicable properties, which constitute and distinguish the persons, are in God without any doubt, and are not there *accidentally*, but *essentially*, since there is nothing in God which is not God. The incommunicable properties are the personal properties, and why could they not be called the incommunicable and personal properties of the Divine Essence, which mark its various manners of subsisting, and of which each Divine Person has its own for itself, which distinguishes it personally from the other two; to distinguish them from its communicable and essential properties which are common to all three? The essential and communicable properties are those of the divine Essence considered *absolutely*, and according to how it is one and common to all, and in all three persons; But the incommunicable and personal properties are those of the divine Essence considered *relatively*, and as *characterized*, just as <term>Catholic Antiquity</term><note>The author refers here to the "Universal Antiquity" of the early Church Fathers and the consensus of the first few centuries of Christianity, rather than the Roman Catholic Church specifically.</note> speaks of its distinct manners of subsisting. And what is there in all of this that shocks the truth, or that is contrary to the word of God, since it is equally true to say that God, who is a single es-

He moves further to Article 14 of our Confession <note>The Gallican Confession of 1559, the foundational statement of faith for the French Reformed (Huguenot) Church.</note>, and believes himself entitled to reject it, because it rejects *everything said of Free Will*. The \* Sophist Ecebolius was far more constant in his religious sentiments than this man; and Mauduit <note>Probably a contemporary critic or former associate of Labadie.</note> was right to write of him five years ago that he did not yet know himself what religion he belonged to. Having been piqued against the Jesuits <note>A powerful Catholic religious order.</note> after having left them, he threw himself to the side of the Jansenists <note>A reformist movement within the Catholic Church that emphasized predestination and the necessity of divine grace.</note>, and at first did wonders by preaching in various places the necessity and efficacy of grace against the powers of Free Will; he was heard by the people of the Roman Church with considerable attention on this subject, because he treated these matters with a new air, and they had not heard them delivered in such a manner by other preachers.

---

\* Ecebolius, a Sophist (that is, a Rhetorician) of Constantinople, had embraced the Christian religion with a great show of zeal under the Emperors Constans and Constantius. He returned to Paganism under Julian the Apostate <note>The Roman Emperor (reigned 361–363) who attempted to suppress Christianity and revive traditional Roman religion.</note>, who held him in high regard; for among the letters of this Emperor, one is found addressed to Ecebolius. When Julian died, he returned to Christianity in Constantinople, and having placed himself among the ranks of the penitents at the entrance of the Church, he said to the faithful: *Trample me underfoot, I who am salt that has lost its flavor.* <note>original: "foulés moy aux pieds, moy qui suis un sel qui a perdu sa saveur." This refers to the biblical metaphor in Matthew 5:13 regarding salt that is no longer useful.</note>
