Now that, in order to leave us and establish a new Sect in the country, he seeks colors to paint the banner of his Schism <note>A formal split or division within a religious body.</note> with some emblem worthy of his genius, he throws himself back into the party of Pelagius and Molina <note>Pelagius (c. 354–418) and Luis de Molina (1535–1600) were theologians whose views on the high capacity of human free will were rejected by the Reformed tradition in favor of total reliance on divine grace.</note>. He finds fault with what our Churches teach regarding the impotence of our will for all that is good and salutary. For our Confession only rejects all that is said of it by the Doctors of Rome in this one regard: to give to God, and to His grace—which is invincible and efficacious by itself—the glory for all good, and to leave man convicted of the guilt of all evil. This Article 14, which he attacks, having represented at length the profound corruption that man brought upon himself by his sin in all the faculties of his soul, adds the following: *Wherefore we reject all that is taught of the Free Will of man, who is but a slave to sin, because man can do nothing unless it is given to him from Heaven; for who is he that shall boast of being able to do what he wills (that is to say, of being able to be the author of his own salvation)* since Christ says, *no one can come to me, unless my Father who sent me draws him*, or as the Latin translation of the National Synod <note>The governing assembly of the French Reformed Church.</note> followed by the French version carries it—not by correction of some error, as Labadie would like to dream in his Parnassus <note>A reference to Mount Parnassus, home of the Muses, here used to mock Labadie's poetic or self-important imagination.</note>, but by a slightly clearer explanation:

Therefore, we reject everything taught to the contrary regarding the Free Will of man, because it is but a slave to sin, and can do nothing unless it is given from Heaven. For who will boast of being able to do any good as if from himself, since Christ says, *no one can come to me, unless my Father who sent me draws him?* <note>John 6:44</note> Who will plead for his own will, understanding that *the inclination of the flesh is enmity against God!* <note>Romans 8:7</note> Who will speak of his own knowledge, seeing that *the sensual man does not understand the things that are of God* (or of the Spirit of God)? Do you renounce Jansenius <note>Cornelius Jansen (1585–1638), whose followers, the Jansenists, emphasized predestination and the total depravity of man, much like the Protestants.</note> to take up the side of Lessius and Molina <note>Leonardus Lessius and Luis de Molina were Jesuit theologians who defended human free will against the stricter views of grace.</note>, and do you abandon St. Prosper and St. Augustine to attach yourself to Faustus, Vincent, Celestius, Julian, and Pelagius? <note>These are historical figures associated with Pelagianism or Semi-Pelagianism, doctrines that emphasize human effort in salvation, which the author considers heretical.</note> With what conscience can you accuse us of heresy and error in the Faith, because instead of preaching with your Jesuits the liberty, freedom, and absolute independence of human choice, we submit it to the providence of God and His grace, pressing it down under sin, slavery, and servitude before

->Refutation.<-

victorious grace comes to break its chains. We maintain that Luther, in naming it the *bound will* <note>original: *serf arbitre*. A reference to Martin Luther's famous 1525 work *De Servo Arbitrio* (On the Bondage of the Will).</note>, spoke like St. Paul, like St. Augustine, and like all their other true disciples; and we affirm with our other theologians that the name "Free Will" is too proud, and that it is a shadow without a body, and a title without substance, when it comes to its own strength for salvation, for the conversion of sinners, or for the practice of Faith and good works. We heartily reject everything that Rome, through its most faithful agents, teaches and sells on this subject to diminish the corruption of man and his impotence toward good, thereby subjecting the grace of God to him and making the effect and success of grace depend on his own determination. And who does not see, by the mere reading of our article, that it does not speak of Free Will *in the category of being* <note>original: *in genere entis*. This refers to the natural existence of the will as a human faculty.</note>? Regarding the *physical* condition of this natural faculty—by which man wills whatever he wills, and everything he judges and thinks he ought to will, very freely and without any constraint (nothing being more repugnant to the "voluntary" than the "constrained" or "forced")—nothing is denied. Rather, the article speaks only *in the category of morality* <note>original: *in genere moris*</note>, regarding what the will can or cannot do for the good and holy things of salvation and Heaven. Thus, Labadie proves himself here to be a true *Sycophant of Syllables* <note>A "quibbler" or one who manipulates words to deceive.</note>, as St. Basil once called the Eunomians and the Arians <note>Eunomians and Arians were early Christian sects whose complex logical arguments were viewed by the orthodox as deceptive wordplay.</note>, when, detaching these five or six words from the body of our Article: *We reject...*

It is not difficult to judge with what Jesuitical sincerity Labadie brings against Mr. Wolzogen the fragments he has torn from his book, after having seen with how many slanders and how much bad faith he has treated our Confession, to make it hateful and execrable to those who listen to him and believe him. But Mr. Wolzogen has so clearly convicted Labadie of bad faith in the citation of the terms of his book, that he has earned for him the privilege of no longer being believed hereafter, even if by chance he happened to tell the truth. We will be content here to note a single example, in order to say *from one crime, learn them all* <note>original Latin: "Crimine ab uno disce omnes," a line from Virgil’s Aeneid suggesting that one example of villainy reveals the character of the whole.</note>, which is the first bead of his rosary in the list of propositions, which he has again piled up here with as little judgment as sincerity, although he has added his N.B. so that he might be recognized by the *Mark of the Beast* <note>A satirical play on the abbreviation "N.B." (Nota Bene, or "Note Well") and the "Mark of the Beast" from the Book of Revelation.</note>. > *I shall hear,* he says, *and I shall understand how you made heaven and earth in the beginning. Moses wrote this; he wrote it and went away; he has passed from here to you. For he is not now before me; and if he were, I would hold him, and I would pray to him, and I would adjure him by you, that it might please him to reveal these things to me, and I would lend the ears of my body to the sounds that came out of his mouth. But if he spoke to me in Hebrew, he would knock in vain at the door of my senses, and there he would not reach my understanding. If in Latin, I would know what he said; but how would I know if he spoke the truth? And if I did know it, would it be from him that I knew it? Not at all; but there would be a truth, which is neither Hebrew, nor Greek, nor Latin, nor Foreign <note>original: "Barbare"</note>, which would tell me there within, in the interior and the home of thought, without the organs of the mouth and the tongue, and without the noise of syllables. He says what is true; and I, immediately assured, would say to this man of yours, "You speak the truth." Since, then, I cannot question him, I pray to you, O my God—by whom he, being filled, spoke the true truth—I pray you forgive me my sins; and having granted Moses your servant to say these things, grant me also the power to understand them.*

the sense in which he had condemned them. Others protested that they had not read them in that sense at all, and claimed that, being a question of fact, the Pope should not be taken at his word, nor was he infallible in it <note>The author is drawing a parallel to the "Jansenist" controversy, where a distinction was made between whether a doctrine was heretical (a question of faith) and whether that doctrine was actually present in a specific book (a question of fact).</note>. We condemn and detest—and Mr. Wolzogen <note>Ludwig Wolzogen (1633–1690) was a Dutch Reformed theologian whose work *De scripturarum interprete* sparked significant controversy.</note> with us—all the impieties, all the errors of the Socinians, the Pelagians, the Papists, and other heterodox people <note>Socinians denied the Trinity; Pelagians denied original sin; "Papists" refers to Roman Catholics.</note>, which Labadie wants to attribute to Mr. Wolzogen; but we say that the more we flip through and examine his book, the less we find them there. Since it is a question of fact, and no longer of Faith, nor of religion, nor of law, the good man must excuse us if we do not believe him infallible here. Thus, our Synod did not *adopt* the errors of Mr. Wolzogen, nor did it become a guarantor by approving his book. It merely declared that it did not find in this book the errors and impieties of which he was accused, and judged that they could only be found there by taking with the left hand what he gave with the right <note>A phrase meaning to willfully misinterpret someone's good intentions or correct statements as something sinister.</note>. It is well known that very famous Doctors and Professors have sided with Labadie’s sentiment regarding this book, and have written books and given testimonies about it. But it is also known that there are others, who yield in nothing to the former group, who on the contrary approved this book and thus supported the judgment of our Synod. It

happened to the Scholars on this occasion as it did to Homer’s Gods during the Trojan War, who declared themselves some for the city and others against it. A long-held aversion to Cartesianism <note>The philosophical system of René Descartes, which many traditional theologians at the time found threatening to religious orthodoxy.</note>, personal and particular interest against Mr. Wolzogen (who was committed to a party that was not favored), the high opinion held of Labadie’s experience and skill in <term>Ascetic Theology<note>The branch of theology focused on spiritual self-discipline and the pursuit of holiness through specific practices.</note></term>, and the thought that he would be the one to restore the rigor of discipline and *restore the Kingdom to Israel* <note>original: "rétablirait le Royaume à Israël"; a biblical allusion to Acts 1:6, here meaning a total reform or restoration of the church's purity.</note>, all happened to coincide to form an outlook so favorable to his person. And upon this, several men of exemplary piety and integrity were astonished that people were attacking Mr. Wolzogen’s book with such heat for propositions that were at most ambiguous, and which could only be pulled into a bad sense by distant and illegitimate consequences—especially notwithstanding all the orthodox explanations he gave and all the express protestations he made.

At the same time, one saw without emotion, and in various places, very pernicious novelties issuing from the theological chairs themselves and from the pens of those who occupy them. These novelties were directly drawn—beyond the heresy of the Chiliasts <note>Also known as Millenarians, who believe in a literal thousand-year earthly reign of Christ.</note>—from the Dogmas of Pelagius, Socinus, Episcopius, and Bellarmine <note>A list of major theological rivals to orthodox Calvinism: Pelagians denied original sin; Socinians denied the Trinity; Episcopius was a leader of the Remonstrants; Bellarmine was a prominent Catholic Cardinal.</note>, and in express terms and without any detour, they sapped and overturned the foundations of Piety and the Doctrine of the Gospel. in its lung, capable of suffocating it.
But now it is hoped that these Gentlemen who ran with such heat at the alarm bell of Labadie, seeing that he has lifted the mask and raised the flag of a criminal Schism, will be sorry to have had such a good opinion of him, and to have engaged themselves so deeply in his interests. As for the others, considering that Mr. Wolzogen has also obtained Illustrious Approvers—whom he can set against them like "spears threatening spears" <note>original: "pila minantia pilis"; a quote from the Roman poet Lucan's *Pharsalia*, describing a civil war where similar forces face off against one another.</note>—and that he produces theological Judgments and Testimonies for his innocence, capable of serving at least as a counterweight to those whom Labadie managed to engage for himself, they will lean toward the better side. Everything well-considered, they will prefer to absolve the accused innocent than to favor the accuser and the guilty party with their vote. And our Synod, by this means, will remain protected from the calumnies of the Schismatic, and justified against the errors in Faith that, regarding this second point, he dares to impute to it. Certainly, as Mr. Wolzogen had written his book of his own accord, and not at our charge, so we have not canonized it, nor put it in a reliquary. We know that those who "build on the street" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning to perform an act or publish a work in public view, thereby inviting public criticism.</note> expose themselves to the judgment and censure of passers-by; and that in an age as delicate, as weary, and as critical as ours, it is impossible to please everyone, or to publish a work that does not encounter 
de

# Modest

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>Modest</header>

of Momus or of Zoilus, <note>In Greek mythology, Momus was the god of mockery, and Zoilus was a critic notorious for his bitter attacks on Homer; both became symbols for harsh, unfair critics.</note> who line the margins of his work with more scratchings than Labadie marks his own writings with N.B. <note>original: "N. B."; abbreviation for the Latin *Nota Bene*, meaning "Note well."</note>; for there was never an mind so loved and esteemed that it was without excuse, nor a work so finished that the Masters could not find some stroke to criticize, nor a face so accomplished that one could not see some irregular feature. Furthermore, given the weakness and ignorance to which even the most skilled and enlightened are always subject in this world, one cannot expect anything from anyone that is without fault. 

In this profession of writing books, there is no master so small that he does not wish to give the Law to others, who does not believe that his own thoughts should serve as a rule for those of others, and that he has the right to correct the *Magnificat*. <note>A French idiom meaning to find fault with something that is already perfect or beyond reproach, referring to the Virgin Mary's song of praise in the Bible.</note> There is no bird in the cage so small that it does not wish to distinguish itself by the singularity of its song. King Francis I used to say that there was no Gentleman in France whom he could not find guilty of high treason within twenty-four hours. In the same way, there is no sacred book written so innocently and elegantly that a critical and malicious Theologian could not find enough leverage to hand it over to the inquisition and seek to convict it of heresy.

# Refutation.

<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>Refutation.</header>

publishing our own. Our goal was never to adopt this work and make it a Synodal book, as Labadie would like to make the world believe; but since it was a matter of preserving the honor of the Author's Ministry, which this man wanted to unjustly tarnish, we were obliged in conscience to protect his innocence, to clear him of the outrages and crimes imposed upon him, and to employ for his justification, in the fear of God, whatever charity and light He has been pleased to give us.

Let us pass to the third head of our Schismatic’s <note>A person who promotes a "schism" or a formal split within a church.</note> accusations regarding the errors in Doctrine and Faith with which he taxes our Churches. To discharge our duty here, we must take the matter from further back. Our assembly, offended by the license Labadie had taken to publish his books without prior approval or censorship—as he had already done with his *Judgment regarding the present State of the Jews*, his *Pastoral Letters*, his *Herald*, and his *Apostolic Arrival*, contrary to the general Discipline of all the Churches of this country and the particular regulations of our own—had tried to provide a remedy at the Synod of Vlissingen held in April 1668. My Lords the States of Zeeland <note>The governing political body of the Dutch province of Zeeland.</note> supported this just design; for recognizing well that his pen would take flight as often as his spirit, they judged it appropriate to bind him to a greater restraint by the following article, which was the sixth of the agreement—

...proposed by their Noble and Powerful Lordships <note>Referring to the "States of Zeeland," the governing provincial assembly in the Netherlands.</note>, admitted by the Synod, and solemnly received by Labadie and his Consistory <note>A consistory is the local governing body of a Reformed church, made up of the minister and elders.</note>: *When Mr. de Labadie, or any other Walloon <note>Walloon refers to the French-speaking Protestant (Huguenot) congregations in the Netherlands.</note> Minister in the Province of Zeeland, wishes to publish, or bring to light, any theological treatises on any subject whatsoever, they shall henceforth be obliged to request permission for this from the Lords States of the said Province, who will then take the prior advice of the Walloon or another Synod on the said treatises, before granting permission to print them.* He had need of this rather harsh bit <note>The author uses the term "caveson," a heavy noseband used for breaking in horses, as a metaphor for strict discipline.</note> to be kept to his duty, and there was reason to hope, since he had submitted to it voluntarily, and since the Sovereign under whom he lived was involving themselves and wished from then on to take particular notice of his productions even before exposing them to Ecclesiastical Censure, the better to prevent his escapades and stop them for the future. But no sooner had the Deputies of the Synod returned home from Zeeland than Labadie showed that, following the example of that unjust judge of whom it is spoken in Luke 18:2, <note>original: "Luc. 18. 2"; a biblical reference to a judge who "neither feared God nor cared about men."</note> he fears no God and respects no one. For this Treatise should have been written as being *proper for this time* <note>The author is mocking Labadie's grammar: Labadie wrote "propre de ce tems" instead of the more standard "propre pour ce tems."</note>—in which Labadie believed he would stir everything up—and not *proper of this time*, since at the very least, in his opinion, he intended it for the time to come as well. There is even a word that remained at the tip of his pen between *according to the sentiments of Jean de Labadie, Pastor,* and *The Prophetic Exercise*. Perhaps he wanted to plant a cross here for the critics to make them guess what should be filled in, or to give himself the satisfaction of knowing what is desired in his works during his life, which is usually only said for others after their death. The Synod of Naarden <note>A town in the Netherlands where the church assembly met.</note> convened six months after the agreement of Flushing <note>original: "Fleffingue"; a port city in Zeeland.</note>, and was rightly offended by the publication of these new pieces, contrary to the pledge given, contrary to the constant order of discipline, and in manifest contempt of the Noble and Powerful States of the Province of Zeeland, who had pledged their Word and their Authority in such a specific way. Labadie, obliged to answer for this to the Company <note>The "Company of Pastors," the governing body of the church.</note>, excused himself by claiming that some of his books brought to light without following that order—to which he and his Church had so solemnly bound themselves—were only small treatises on Devotion, and that the others were already finished printing, although not yet disclosed nor put on sale at the time of the Synod of Flushing, and that the distribution had been carried out by his servants without his knowledge: The Synod, as appears by the fourth article of its resolutions, was in no way satisfied by this evasion; for books claimed to be of Devotion can also contain maxims perilous to the Church, and must not

any less pass through censorship than the others: And as for the others printed at the expense of his Church, since they had not yet appeared in public at the time of the agreement of Flushing, good Faith then obliged him either to ask for the approval of the States and the Synod, or to make of them a sacrifice to Vulcan <note>A classical idiom meaning to burn the books; Vulcan was the Roman god of fire.</note>, and suppress them: And as for what he alleged, that they had been sold and distributed without his knowledge, he was shamefully convicted of lying by the declaration made by the Booksellers, who were *selling* them publicly, stating they had received them from him for that purpose. This is why the Synod, indignant at a proceeding as consistent with his Jesuit character as it was ill-suited to the status of a Minister of the Word of God, which he then held, declared him censurable and a violator, both of Ecclesiastical discipline and the agreement of Flushing.

Now, wherever one supposes **obligation** and **duty**, it must be admitted that the conscience is bound by some Law—either mediately <note>In this context, "mediatedly" means a human law that derives its ultimate authority from God's general command to obey leaders.</note>, if it is a Law and a human constitution, by that of God; or immediately, if the Law is from God himself and his word. # Refutation.

<page-num>183</page-num>

science, because they are not *of divine obligation and obedience*: This is the fine morality that he proposes in the second part of his *Apologetic Complaints*, page 35, to overthrow with a single kick all the power of Magistrates and Superiors, and to authorize subjects and peoples to trample underfoot all their authority and all their laws, as if there were no sin before God in violating them, and that one only sins "humanly" by neglecting them. 

But from whom did Labadie learn to distinguish between *human* sins and *divine* sins, and to imagine *sins* that could be committed in good conscience? We know well that the Pope plays the part of the **Antichrist** when he attributes to himself the authority to make Laws in the Church that obligate and bind Christian Consciences, directly and immediately, and according to the jargon of Rome **under pain of mortal sin** <note>original: "sous peine de peché mortel"</note>: For besides the fact that he falsely attributes to himself the authority to make Laws in the Church of God, only those of God can bind and obligate consciences in such a way. We know well that Ecclesiastical assemblies have no legislative authority like Civil powers, and we do not wish to equal the Canons <term>Canons: the decrees or rules of a church council</term> of the Church to the Laws of the Magistrate: The former only obligate by the express or tacit, formal or interpretive consent of those who submit to them; but the latter obligate by themselves, and

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of church authority. The previous page established that even human laws can bind the conscience when they derive their authority from God's command to respect order. ### Modeste, Refutation.

<page-num>184</page-num>

...without depending on the consent of the subjects, they leave them only the glory of obeying well: Those <note>referring to purely civil or human laws</note> may be neglected without sin when it can be done without scandal and without contempt; but these <note>laws of divine or natural origin</note> always bind, and whoever violates them is always guilty, even if he violates them so secretly that no one knows anything about it. We also do not attribute to Ecclesiastical assemblies a jurisdiction properly so-called, far from making it collateral to Civil jurisdiction: For they have nothing, as the **Jurists** <term>Jurists: legal scholars or experts in the law</term> observe, of everything without which a true jurisdiction cannot be properly exercised—that is to say, neither a court bar, nor territory, nor bailiffs, nor power that is properly *coercive* <note>original: "Coactive"; the legal power to force compliance through physical or civil penalty</note>, regarding the outward man, nor punishments properly called *afflictive* <note>original: "afflictives"; punishments that cause physical suffering or hardship, such as imprisonment</note> or *destructive* of the body, honor, or the purse. We know that they have properly only the function and administration of a holy Discipline, and that there is cause to correct them when they go astray, either from the rules of the word of God or from legitimate Canons. But this in no way favors the sentiments of our man <note>Jean de Labadie</note>. For although the power of the **Magistrate** <term>Magistrate: here referring to civil government officials or the sovereign</term> is *Architectonic* <term>Architectonic: an overarching or master authority that organizes and directs other forms of power</term>, pre-eminent and primary, and that of the Ecclesiastical assemblies is ministerial alone, and *diaconal* or *hyperetetic* <note>original: "diaconique ou hyperetetique"; derived from Greek terms for service and assistance, meaning the church serves God's will rather than creating its own laws</note>, it does not follow that the regulations of the latter are more *human* than those of the former. 

<column-break/>

<page-num>185</page-num>

Furthermore, there is nothing so human as the orders that a father of a family gives to his children for their occupations and for their tasks in household matters: These children are nonetheless obliged in conscience to follow them, and they sin against God and against the first commandment which is with a promise <note>Exodus 20:12, "Honor thy father and thy mother"</note>, if they refuse to subject themselves to them. What is more, although the political power of Magistrates is much greater for political and temporal things than that of Pastors and Leaders of the Church for Ecclesiastical and spiritual things, it is nevertheless certain that the regulations of the latter ought to be considered much less *human* in regard to their object than those of the former; and thus, by **Labadie’s** <note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674), a former Jesuit turned Reformed pastor who eventually led a separatist communal sect</note> account, there would be much less sin in not submitting to the orders and regulations of Magistrates than to those of Synods. Moreover, Ecclesiastical Regulations are made either regarding things concerning Doctrine in Faith and Morals, or regarding things concerning Discipline and the Policy of the Church. The former bind only insofar as they are formally and specifically founded in Scripture, because we must not be *wise beyond what is written*, **1 Cor. 4:6**. And whatever might be introduced on this subject without the authority of the Scriptures would for that very reason be contrary to the Scriptures, and should, as **Saint Jerome** <note>A 4th-century Church Father known for his translation of the Bible and his commentaries</note> speaks, be...

<summary>The author argues that while the church lacks the state's power to physically punish, its spiritual regulations are more binding on the conscience because they deal with divine matters. ### Modeste

[can be] as easily rejected as alleged. But as for the second kind <note>referring to matters of church discipline and administrative "police" rather than core doctrine</note>, because the Scripture was content to give us only the general rules that we have noted above—leaving the specification and particular determination to the **prudence** <term>prudence: practical wisdom and careful judgment in governing</term> of the Church, which has varied throughout time according to the variety of occasions, times, places, and persons—one must hold as conformable to the Word of God *generically* everything that is not formally contrary to it, neither in its matter nor in its form. Such rules do not clash in any way with Christian liberty and aim only to prevent the disorder of errors and vices, to preserve good order in the house of God—who is a God of order and not of confusion <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 14:33</note>—and to tighten the knot of union and peace among the brothers, so that they may work with one and the same shoulder <note>an idiom meaning to work together in total unity</note>, and without contention, contest, or vainglory, at the work of the Lord and the salvation of souls. Regarding these two kinds of Regulations, which can be made in Synodical assemblies, the ancient Councils used to have two different books placed in an eminent spot before the eyes of all: That of the **Scripture**, for the first kind, to condemn errors and uphold the truth in conformity with the holy letters; and that of the **Canons** <term>Canons: ecclesiastical rules or laws decreed by a council</term> for the second, in order to settle by them the disputes that might arise regarding the **Discipline** and the **Policy** <term>Policy (Police): the internal regulation and administrative order of the church</term> of the Church. ### Refutation.

have had to explain at some length the sentiment of our Churches on this subject, to show that there is no heresy or error in maintaining that the faithful person is obliged to keep the orders and Regulations of Synodical assemblies. But error and heresy are on the side of **Labadie** <note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674), a former Jesuit who became a radical Pietest leader and clashed with the Reformed Church</note>, when he claims in general that *human* regulations, because they are human, do not bind (as he puts it) *under penalty of sin*, and that all Ecclesiastical Regulations—for policy as well as for doctrine—must equally and in the same fashion conform [explicitly] to the word of God.

It is, he says, *opening the door to human traditions in religion and to their observation in conscience and under penalty of sin.* For by this same maxim, one would have to abolish all **Civil laws**, which, for being human constitutions, could not—according to his account—bind the conscience. <meta>This page continues the author's defense of church authority. ### Modeste

<page-num>188</page-num>

[...distin-] guish the manner in which the conscience is bound to the observation and execution of something: either directly and immediately by the Law of God, or indirectly and mediately, whether by the laws of men—whom God commands us to obey, just as subjects are obliged in conscience to obey the ordinances of their Magistrates, and children the orders of their Fathers—or even by our treaties, by our promises, and by our agreements, as no one doubts that an honest man is bound in conscience to keep his Faith and his word. He also ignores in what sense sound Theology disapproves of the Pope claiming for himself the right to prescribe laws to the Church that bind **under pain of sin**. For it is not that there is no sin before God in violating legitimate laws and orders; rather, it is because the Pope has no Legislative power [over the soul], and by claiming the right to bind **under pain of sin**, he pretends to bind consciences to obedience of his orders directly and immediately, which belongs to God alone. He further ignores that the **human traditions**, which all Protestant Churches reject, are: **First**, the Dogmatic traditions, which propose doctrines of faith not found in writing <note>unwritten traditions or extra-biblical oral traditions</note>, besides and against the word of God. **Second**, the Ritual traditions, which some wish to pass off as essential and formal parts [of faith], rather than as simple acts of worship and religious service,

which must be governed by the Scriptures alone; **Third**, the Superstitious traditions, which can be overturned by Scripture and which are contrary to Christian liberty. <meta>This page continues the author's defense of the Reformed Church's authority against Jean de Labadie. The previous pages argued that the Church’s regulations are not tyrannical but are intended to maintain order, unlike Labadie’s own self-imposed rules during his time in Middelburg.</meta>

in the sense that he says it. We admit to him that there is only the Law of God which binds the conscience directly, immediately, and by itself: But this does not at all overthrow the Civil Laws of Sovereigns, nor the Ecclesiastical Canons and Constitutions of the Synods <term>Synods: regional or national assemblies of church ministers and elders who decide on matters of doctrine and discipline</term> in their proper rank. In matters of doctrine and faith, the Church can ordain nothing except according to the word of God and in conformity with it; just as in matters of order and Ecclesiastical policy, which are not defined in Scripture, she can never ordain anything against the Word of God, and she must always do so in conformity with His Word, or at least according to the general rules prescribed for her. 

Ecclesiastical Constitutions—which are merely particular applications of the general rules of the word of God, just as Civil Law contains only particular applications and determinations of natural and perpetual equity to present affairs and circumstances—must undoubtedly be observed, provided they contain nothing vicious or contrary to Scripture or to Christian liberty (two exceptions that we always assume). And Labadie is quite ridiculous to think that there is any constitution that can require observation without there being any sin in despising it or failing to observe it, under the pretext that it could not be established "in the style of Rome" <note>original: "au style de Rome"—referring to the Roman Catholic claim that the Church's laws bind the soul under pain of mortal sin, a power the Reformed Church rejected in favor of "indirect" obligation</note> under pain of sin; that is to say, as

...according to how he touched them with his bow; and living an Angelic life and [belonging] to the order of the watchers <note>French: *veillans*. Likely a reference to the "Watchers" or a specific contemplative lifestyle Labadie claimed to lead, characterized by constant prayer and vigilance.</note>, he woke them from their slumber, and made them feel ecstatic movements in imitation of his own.

It is therefore false that the Synod, in condemning the extravagances of this Enthusiast <note>In the 17th century, "Enthusiast" was a derogatory term for those claiming direct, private revelation from God, often used against radical sects.</note>, has abandoned *the doctrine and the practice of the Apostles and of the primitive Church*, authorized by the book of their *Acts*, and even by the Gospel; since in all of Scripture one does not find a single iota that favors or authorizes his imaginary and fanatic exercise. The conferences that were held in the Jewish Synagogues, after the reading of Moses and the Prophets, were only between the Scholars and the Masters, and were like school exercises, just as the Jews still name their Synagogues today <note>Refers to the term "Shul," meaning school, emphasizing that synagogue speech was academic and regulated, not spontaneous.</note>: no one even had the privilege of preaching or speaking there if they were not formally or *virtually* graduated: That is why the leaders of the Synagogue of Antioch in Pisidia, having noticed that Saint Paul and Saint Barnabas had taken their seats as Doctors <note>In this context, "Doctor" refers to a teacher or one learned in the Law.</note>, had them asked—once the reading was finished—that if they had any word of exhortation for the people, they should speak it (Acts 13: 14, 15, 16). It is false that his Enthusiast propositions—in the way he puts them forward according to the sentiment and manner of the Illuminati <note>French: *Illuminés*; various mystical groups claiming direct divine light.</note> and the Quakers—of speaking

<column-break/>

when the spirit moves, and of remaining silent when it ceases to speak by revelation, etc., can be authorized by Scripture; And it is false that the National Synods of these Provinces held at *Wesel* and at *Emden*, whose statutes were confirmed and received at the Synods of *Leiden*, as is mentioned in the *Harmony of the Confessions of Faith*, intended to establish his Prophetic exercise; here, certainly, he relied on the faith of others <note>A polite way of saying Labadie took someone else's word for it without verification.</note>, or else he was deceived by some false revelation—as he often is—given that all those he has had, or says he has had, have not been true: He speaks in general of the National Synods of these Provinces held at *Wesel* and at *Emden*, without marking the years they were held: In the book containing the Ecclesiastical constitutions of the Reformed Churches of the Netherlands, under the title of *Kerke-Ordening* <note>Dutch: "Church Order." These are the official governing rules of the Dutch Reformed Church.</note>, printed for the fourth time in Delft in the year 1640, there is no Synod of Wesel; however, one does indeed read there at the beginning the Acts of the Synod of Emden convened in the year 1571. But there is absolutely no mention of the Prophetic exercise: And there is no appearance of likelihood that a single Act, conceived in the same terms, would have been drawn up in two different National Synods, one on the Ems and the other on the Rhine. Also, that the Statutes of two National Synods would have needed to be *Confirmed* at the Synod of *Leiden*, without telling us which they are, or when they were held, is to speak like a blind... <note>The catchword *aveu-* indicates the next word is *aveugle* (blind man).</note>

-> ## I 6 <-

...blind man judging colors: Thus, one does not know what Labadie means by the *Harmony of the Confessions of Faith* <note>original: "l’*Harmonie des Confessions de Foy*." This refers to the *Harmonia Confessionum Fidei* (1581), a famous volume compiled to demonstrate the doctrinal unity of various Protestant churches.</note>, to find mention there of either his so-called Synods or the article he produces from it. For the book that bears this name in Latin contains only the uniformity in doctrine of the various Confessions of Faith published by Protestants at different times, in different places, and on different occasions, to show that they all agree on the fundamentals; and therein is found nothing regarding Synodal regulations or discipline. Consequently, the article Labadie produces cannot be drawn from it and is apparently a discarded scrap <note>original: "piece de rebut." A piece of text that was rejected or is of no value.</note>. Furthermore, this *fine article* seems to establish this *Exercise* only to prepare for the holy Ministry those who were devoting themselves to it, since there were to be admitted *and joined not only the Pastors and Ministers, but the Doctors <note>In the 17th century, a "Doctor" was a formal teacher of theology, often a university professor.</note>, and from among the Elders and Deacons, and even from among the simple or common people, those who, for the common good and utility of the Church, wish to employ the talents and gifts that are within them, and this after they have been found fit and approved by the judgment of the Ministers as capable and fit to speak.* Moreover, this could only be conceived or established for large Churches, where there would be not only several Pastors, but also Doctors—apparently in Law and Medicine—who would have enough zeal to

<column-break/>

<page-num>203</page-num>

wish to employ themselves over time in the Ministry of the Gospel, and would wish to prepare themselves for it through these exercises. So that fundamentally, in this article reported by Labadie—by whatever right he obtained it—nothing is contained except what is defined by Article 20 of our Discipline in these words: *The use of the proposition <term>A "proposition" was a formal practice sermon delivered by a candidate for the ministry to demonstrate their theological and oratorical skills before a clerical audience.</term> shall be established in Churches where there are several Ministers fit to lead it, so that by such exercises some may be prepared for the Ministry of the Word, following the order that the present Synod has specially drawn up.* If any National Synod of these United Provinces <note>The Dutch Republic.</note> had prescribed anything regarding this so-called Prophetic Exercise of Labadie, one would find the practice of it in some churches of the country, as it is not likely that they would have collectively made a regulation of such importance without executing it anywhere. But if one takes the trouble to inquire in all the churches of the country, and in all those of the neighborhood, one will find nowhere any trace of this observation. And even if it could be of some use, and did not open the path to Enthusiasm <note>In this period, "Enthusiasm" was a negative term for those claiming direct, private inspiration from the Holy Spirit, which was seen as a threat to church order.</note>, and did not introduce an evident confusion into the house of the God of order: Labadie could not excuse himself for a signal and most proud audacity in having undertaken, from his own head, the establishment of so important a novelty in one of our Churches without having communicated it to all the others.

<summary>The author argues that Labadie’s "Prophetic Exercises" are a misrepresentation of Protestant church history, noting that the "Harmony of Confessions" deals with doctrine, not the spontaneous lay preaching Labadie advocates. He asserts that Labadie’s citations actually refer to the "proposition," a regulated training exercise for aspiring ministers, rather than a general license for all congregants to speak.</summary>

...and without having previously sought the means to maintain uniformity and harmony between the members of the same body.

The fifth point of our heresies and our errors in the Faith consists, according to Labadie, in the claim that our *Synod* <note>An assembly of church leaders and elders who govern the church.</note> believed and still believes it has the power to suspend *Pastors, Elders, Deacons, and Church Leaders from the Holy Supper, from their Ministry, and from their office, and even to depose them without them having committed a crime against the two tables of the Law <note>The "two tables" refer to the Ten Commandments: the first four regarding duties to God, and the remaining six regarding duties to fellow humans.</note> of God, or being justly punishable by the Magistrate, as required by all Ecclesiastical Discipline conforming to the said Law of God*. In all this discourse, he shows that he has lost his bearings <note>original: "perdu sa tramontane." An idiom meaning to be confused or to lose one's way.</note>; he cannot deny that the Synod has the right to suspend and even to depose from Church functions and the Sacraments those who deserve it. The entire question between him and us is therefore a matter of fact: whether we were well-founded in using this right through Church censures against him and his accomplices. We are only blameworthy for having tolerated him for too long in his errors, in his rebellions against the orders of the Church, in his Pharisaical hypocrisy, and in his intolerable pride. And it was finally necessary to employ harsh remedies <note>original: "corrosifs."</note> where milder ones <note>original: "lenitifs."</note> had failed. Merely reading our public Acts is enough to justify them, and the clear knowledge of his crimes against the first and second tables of the Law, without it being ne—

The best and healthiest part of the Church of Middelburg <note>A city in the Netherlands where Labadie was a pastor before his expulsion.</note> has not only acquiesced to it but has also rejoiced in it; and those whom he had led away by his trickery are returning to their senses every day. It is this extravagant book in which he wished to peddle and defend the heresy of the *Chiliasts* <note>Also known as Millenarians; those who believe that Jesus Christ will establish a physical, 1,000-year kingdom on Earth before the final Judgment.</note>, which entirely ruins the nature of the Gospel, destroys the true condition of the Kingdom of JESUS CHRIST, and would have us hope for happiness and glory on earth that is promised to us only in Heaven. This doctrine is as false in religion as it is harmful to the salvation of individuals and pernicious to the State, as all wise men recognize, and as experience has verified through the furies of Münster <note>The Münster Rebellion (1534–1535) was a radical Anabaptist takeover of a German city that ended in a violent siege; it was frequently cited as a "cautionary tale" of religious extremism.</note> and England. After this interlude of his *Herald*, sounding the retreat rather than the parley <note>original: "chamade" — a drum or trumpet signal for a parley or surrender.</note> on this subject, in which he sees all his hopes aborted, he passes from our corruption in Doctrine—because we did not wish to receive the errors and impieties of his own—to that of our *Assemblies*; and as, in laying down the foundations of his Schism, he proves that he is more than a Donatist, more than a Cathar, and more than a Novatian <note>These were historical Christian groups (4th, 12th, and 3rd centuries respectively) labeled as heretical for their extreme rigorism and for separating from the main Church over issues of purity.</note>; so it is almost enough for us to remark that, even if everything he says on this subject were as true as it is false, he could not allege it in good conscience to authorize the insolence of his *Manifesto*, and the fatal design he conceives therein to surprise simple souls, to engage them to follow his banners, and to separate themselves from our communion.

He begins with our *Synodal Assemblies*: We admit to him that they are frequent, because they are convened *twice a year*. But in exchange, they hardly last long: and it has served our poor Churches well that they were convened often enough to stop the gangrene that Labadie produced there, because the slowness of remedies would have rendered the evil incurable. The utility that the public derives from their convocation is well worth the expenses incurred; but it is false that the money of the poor is used for them. These expenses are taken in part from the Collections and contributions...

And the trouble given to us by our Hotheaded friend <note>Referring to Jean de Labadie, whose temperament was famously volatile.</note> for three years proves well enough that we do not always deal with trivial matters; the very care taken regarding gifts and mismanaged funds is proof of the exactness maintained even in the smallest things. And so far from usurping the rights of individual Churches, we aim, on the contrary, only to maintain their rights, good order, and liberty. Labadie will never prove—and we defy him to produce a single example—that any Regulations are made there, or have ever been made, that do not conform to the *Word of God*, and what is more, that are contrary to it. For we see no difference between these two expressions; all Ecclesiastical Regulations must be considered contrary to the *Word of God* when they do not conform to it, either specifically in points of Doctrine, or at least generally in those of Governance <term>Police: Here referring to the "Polity" or the administrative and jurisdictional order of the church.</term> and Discipline. <meta>This page continues the defense of the Reformed Church's organizational structure against the critiques of Jean de Labadie. While the previous page addressed accusations of financial mismanagement and the authority of Synods, this section specifically refutes the charge that regional church bodies (Classes) operate as "oligarchies" and defends the integrity of the local elders and deacons.</meta>

# Modest

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>Modeste</header>
<meta>catchword: God</meta>

of the expenses incurred there, just as Judas complained of the wastefulness of the Lord’s Anointing: as one travels very comfortably in this country, the costs must naturally compensate for that convenience. It is a slander against our *Classes* <note>Regional governing bodies in the Reformed tradition, sitting between the local Consistory and the larger Synod.</note> to accuse them of *Oligarchy*: all members are colleagues and equal in vote and power, and one has no more to say than another. If they exceed their commission, there is reason to complain to the Synod and to censure them; but *Labadie* <note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674), a former Jesuit who became a pietist leader; the author views him as a divisive radical.</note> has no example of this, except in his own little half-cracked brain. And it is a very good and very legitimate order that the Synodal Churches can, either alone or by communication with their neighbors, provisionally provide for incidents that arise which suffer no delay and which the preceding Synod had not foreseen. There are as few *excesses* committed in the Classes as in the Synods, and there are no matters that pass more *quietly or by conspiracy* than those entrusted to Deputies. But our man, still remembering the schemes carried out in convents and among the *Jesuits*, wants to attribute them to us slanderously. If some Classes have lasted a long time and for entire months because of his case, it must be attributed to his haughty and seditious temper.

Classes enter into our *Consistorial Assemblies* <note>The local council of a specific congregation.</note>, which, though composed,

<column-break/>

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>Refutation.</header>

God be thanked, of many people of honor and good men, do not escape the teeth of his satire. The Pastors preside there according to order, but without domination over the *Elders and Deacons*, who each have their free vote and who have as much to say as the Minister. *Labadie* is the only one who had begun to act with a heavy hand in the Consistories, and had tyrannically enslaved—*by conspiracy*—the one in Middelburg; for, like *Paul V*, he *carried the look of empire in his face* <note>original: "Paul V. vultu portendebat imperium." Referring to Pope Paul V, suggesting Labadie possessed a similarly arrogant or papal desire for absolute power.</note>. Our Elders and Deacons are chosen legitimately and by a majority of votes, and when he treats those who are admitted to these holy offices as *vain, worldly, unedifying, and unzealous people—as scandalous, ignorant, lazy, and cowardly*—he reveals what spirit leads him. These speeches are not inspired by truth nor by charity; it is only gall that he pours out. Formerly, *Father Jarrige* <note>Pierre Jarrige (1604–1660), a Jesuit who converted to Protestantism, wrote a scathing critique of his former order, then eventually returned to Catholicism.</note>, who came to us from the Jesuits just as *Labadie* did, but who returned to them, placed the *Jesuits on the Scaffold* here in a specific treatise; but this man, who undergoes the metamorphosis \* of *Hecuba*, deserves at the very least to be put in chains. And because our Consistories are grave, modest, and serious, and one sees nothing there of the fury and of the...

reign Authority, after having recognized their dangerous and harmful spirit, has had them removed from the Province of **Zeeland**: This is what he did not expect when he had prepared and printed his *Manifesto* for our **Synod of Dort** <note>The author refers to a regional session of the Synod in the city of Dordrecht.</note>, imagining that by this he would separate the Church of **Middelburg** from our body, to make it his "New Jerusalem," and to propagate his wretched schism further from there, in order to thus ruin all the churches of the country, based on the maxim of the one who said, *Divide and Rule* <note>Original Latin: "Divide & Impera."</note>. But God, by His righteous judgment, has caused his pernicious designs to fall back upon his own head, and has proven by the event that they were illicit and vicious.

It is true that our Assemblies are less frequent on workdays than on Sunday, because it is Sunday which is particularly consecrated to them by the Law of God: And one should not be surprised that in a country like this one, where the active life is so vibrant, there are more Marthas than Marys <note>A reference to the biblical sisters Martha and Mary (Luke 10:38–42); Martha represents practical work and busy-ness, while Mary represents quiet devotion.</note>. Everyone hastens as best they can to come to the *reading*, to the *praising* (he remembers, in speaking thus of the **Psalmody** or the singing of Psalms, the **Lauds** of the Papacy <note>Morning prayers in the Catholic tradition; the author suggests Labadie is mockingly comparing Reformed singing to Catholic liturgy.</note>) and to the prayers.

And we do not perceive in our flocks the laziness that Labadie imputes to them on this subject; any more than the lack of *respect and attention to the Word of God and all the exercises of Piety*, with which he so impudently accuses them. If Labadie sometimes saw, in our Churches when he preached there, that the listeners laughed or spoke among themselves during his sermon, he should not attribute it to *frivolity or chatter*, much less to the immodesty or insolence of our people; but rather to his "Tabarin-like" <note>Original: "Tabarine." A reference to Tabarin, a famous 17th-century Parisian street performer known for farce and coarse comedy. The author is accusing Labadie of preaching like a buffoon or a clown.</note> and theatrical way of preaching, by which he exposed himself to public mockery. Besides, as our **Temples** <note>The standard term used by French Protestants (Huguenots) for their places of worship to distinguish them from Catholic "Churches."</note> are open to everyone, we cannot prevent **Papists** <note>A derogatory term for Roman Catholics.</note> and profane people from often entering, more with the intent to criticize than to learn, and more out of curiosity than zeal, nor can we prevent their immodest countenances, for which we are not responsible. We do not establish ourselves as defenders of luxury and vanity, working as much as we can to steer our people away from them; but nevertheless, because *vanity* is a vice of the spirit, and not of clothing, and because there is often much more modesty, humility, piety, and devotion under purple and silk than under the cowl of a Monk, or under the homespun cloth and the affected simplicity of an **unfrocked priest** <note>Original: "defroqué." ...of attention, because they do not entertain them with a heap of words, repeated a thousand times and devoid of sense as Labadie used to do; but with solid and very edifying matters. And it is very remarkable that this man—who at first excused himself by claiming a defect in his sight so as not to read our ordinary **Liturgy** <note>The set order of public worship. Labadie preferred spontaneous, "spirit-led" prayer over prescribed texts.</note>—would open his eyes wide as a mite <note>Original: "yeux comme un siron." A "ciron" or "siron" is a tiny mite. The author is likely mocking Labadie’s intense, searching gaze despite his supposed poor vision.</note> while preaching, to search in the corner of a **Temple** <note>The standard term for a French Protestant church building.</note> or in the shadow of a pillar for a drowsy person. He did this just to interrupt his own discourse (so little was he himself attentive to it), crying out to *my brother the sleeper* or to *my sister the sleeper* <note>Original: "mon frere le dormeur," "ma sœur la dormeuse."</note>, and commanding that they be woken up. 

And as for his complaint regarding the profanation of our Temples—as if *out of a house of prayer one were making marketplaces, or the filth of a Comedy, where vanity is displayed and luxury appears, etc.*—one notices a strange confusion. This is an exaggeration devoid of sense, apparently based on a **Papistical** <note>Catholic.</note> assumption that Temples, beyond their sacred use during and for Exercises of Piety, possess an inherent holiness that must be strictly respected. This point affects the **Flemish Churches** <note>Dutch-speaking Reformed churches.</note> no less than the **Walloon** <note>French-speaking Reformed churches in the Low Countries.</note> ones, just as everything he says in general about our Ecclesiastical Assemblies concerns those churches as much as, or even more than, our own.

From these generalities which mean nothing, he moves on to **three principal things** regarding our Assemblies, for which he accuses them of

<column-break/>

*visible corruption.* The first concerns the use of Liturgies, that is to say, the public forms observed for prayers as well as for the administration of the **Sacraments** <note>Specifically Baptism and the Lord's Supper.</note>. This is what he calls in his gibberish, *The literal practice of Liturgies*: as if one could conceive of a "Mystical" practice of them. 

His refusal to adhere to the public forms of our common Liturgies—even to the point of accusing their use of *notable corruption*—confirms well enough that he "plows with the heifer" <note>An idiom based on Judges 14:18, meaning to use someone else’s methods or arguments.</note> of the **Independents** and the **Fanatics** <note>Terms used here for radical Protestant groups, such as the English Congregationalists or Quakers, who rejected central church authority and set liturgies.</note>. It shows that he renounces not only the communion of the Walloon Churches of these Provinces, but also that of all the Reformed and Protestant Churches of Europe. Except for a handful of Fanatics and Independents, these all have their constant and uniform Liturgical Formulas for ecclesiastical prayers, for the blessing of Marriage, for the administration of **Baptism**, for the celebration of the **Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>, for the **Psalmody** <note>The singing of Psalms.</note>, and for **Catechetical instruction** <note>The formal teaching of church doctrine, usually to children or new members.</note>. 

God under the Law had prescribed the formula for the Priestly blessing in Numbers 6:23 <note>Original: "Nomb. 6. 23." The verse where God gives the specific words for the "Aaronic Blessing."</note>. The Psalms composed by **David** and **Asaph** were sung in ordinary service and formed a part of the Liturgy in the Temple of Jerusalem (1 Chronicles 16:8; 2 Chronicles 29:25). If we believe the Jews, **Ezra** <note>Original: "Esdras." The biblical scribe credited with reorganizing Jewish life and liturgy after the Babylonian exile.</note> had drawn up a large part of their prayers for ordinary service;

...just as Saint **John the Baptist** <note>The precursor to Jesus who, according to the Gospels, taught his disciples specific ways to pray.</note> once provided his Disciples with some Form of Prayer, and those of **JESUS CHRIST** received from him that of the **Lord’s Prayer**, Luke 11:1-2, and that of **Holy Baptism**, as it was to be administered and conferred in the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, Matt. 28:19. Since the time of the Apostles, all Christian Churches have had their particular Liturgies, and this is—between those of the same Province or of the same language—the most visible bond of their union, and to speak with **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) from Carthage.</note>, the livery or the mark of their fraternity. This refers to Matthew 6:7, where Jesus warns against the "vain repetitions" of the heathens who think they will be heard for their many words.</note> Matt. 6:7, imitated the one and the other far more than we do in our Liturgies.

He clearly shows himself to be ignorant of what **Christian liberty** is, when he claims that to bind oneself to Liturgical and public formulas—in order to maintain uniformity in worship and public service—is something that is repugnant to him and destroys it; whereas re-

For even though the Prophets and the Apostles, on extraordinary and particular occasions for prayer, did not attach themselves to some Liturgical formula—just as we do not stop at one in a similar case—who told him either that the Prophets rejected the ordinary formulas of public service when they attended to it, or that the Apostles never recited in their ordinary worship the Lord’s Prayer, which they had learned from the Lord? Certainly, if we believe **Gregory I** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604).</note> and **Polydore Virgil** <note>A 15th-century Italian humanist scholar who wrote on the history of Church rites.</note> after him, the Apostles recited it constantly in the administration of the **Eucharist**, and it was the principal or the only piece of their Liturgy for this purpose. Who will doubt that **Paul** and **Silas**, singing and chanting psalms to God in the depths of their prison, employed some of the **Psalms** and sacred Hymns that they had learned from the **Jewish Liturgy**? Does he not claim that those of David, so full of instruction and consolation, should form a principal part of our Liturgy, and that we are thus obliged to attach ourselves to them? Who will cast into doubt that the Hymns the first Christians sang in their assemblies, even before daybreak in honor of JESUS CHRIST, did not constitute a part of their Liturgy? This is especially clear if one recalls what the younger **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Younger (61–113 AD), a Roman lawyer and author who wrote a famous letter to Emperor Trajan about the early Christians.</note> reported in his letter to the **Emperor Trajan** concerning the Christians: that they had the custom, *to chant a hymn to Christ as if to a God, alternately among themselves* <note>original: "Carmen Christo quasi Deo dicere secum invicem"</note>; and that this word *Carmen* (Song/Hymn), as the learned **Vossius** <note>Gerhard Johann Vossius (1577–1649), a Dutch classical scholar and theologian.</note> remarked on this passage of Pliny, signified among the Romans, *whatever they said in prescribed words* <note>original: "quicquid conceptis verbis dicerent"</note>, which is to say, all the formulas to which they were obliged to adhere. If one takes the trouble to look in the work of the diligent **Alexander Ross** <note>A Scottish chaplain and author known for "Pansebeia: or, A View of all Religions in the World" (1653).</note> in his book titled *Religions of the World*, in the third part and the twelfth section, at the description he gives of the **Millenarian Independents** <note>Radical Protestant groups who believed in a literal thousand-year reign of Christ on Earth.</note> in the new Church of Arnhem, one will find there the portrait of most of the extravagances of Labadie. This clearly shows his *temerity*, his *rebellion* against the public orders of the Church, his *Enthusiasm* <note>In the 17th century, "Enthusiasm" was a derogatory term for claiming direct, private inspiration from the Holy Spirit over scripture or church authority.</note>, and his *Libertinage*, in condemning as corruption the general use of Liturgies in the Church;

gies; because in adhering to them, he has no means to exercise his chatter, which goes like the clapper of a mill, and the strange volubility of his tongue, as much as he would like. This has been the route he has followed since his entry into this country, to establish himself as a party leader and to set fire to the Church. If one compares everything he says here against us with what the **Quakers** <note>The Religious Society of Friends; at the time, they were often viewed by established churches as dangerous radicals for their rejection of formal ministry.</note> peddle every day in their booklets against the Ministers of the Word of God to make them odious to the people, one will see that he and they are driven by the same spirit and drink from the same spring. # Modeste

<meta>catchword: nous</meta>

...ment the <term>Pastors</term> <note>The French "Pasteurs" refers to the ordained ministers of the Reformed Church.</note>. The one to whom we must render an account on the last day of our administration, and under whose eyes we frankly employ ourselves—as much as it pleases Him to fulfill His strength in our weakness—knows with what zeal and sincerity we try to acquit ourselves well. The satisfaction that the public takes in us through His blessing, the singular affection our flocks bear for us, the attention with which they listen to us, and the edification they testify to receiving from our work, protect us well enough from the calumnies of this Hothead <note>A reference to Jean de Labadie, the antagonist of this text.</note>. If we sometimes offer our pulpits to *Foreign* Ministers, just as was practiced in the primitive Church, it is to tighten the knot of our holy Union, and to procure through them a new edification for our Listeners. If we sometimes place <term>Proponents</term> <note>original: "Propoſans"; these were divinity students or candidates for the ministry who were permitted to preach to gain experience.</note> there, it is not to unburden ourselves, nor because we do not take a singular pleasure in performing our duties; but it is to satisfy the above-alleged article of our Discipline, and to better prepare them for the functions of the holy Ministry. Far from performing our duties *perfunctorily* <note>original: "perfonctoirement"; meaning done as a matter of routine or without care.</note>, as Labadie accuses us—remembering very little both what the Apostle says, *Who are you who judges the servant of another?*, Romans 14:4 <note>The text cites Rom. 15:4, but the quote provided corresponds to Rom. 14:4.</note>, and that it is he who has performed his own *perfunctorily*, having always shamelessly boasted of having preached without meditation,

---

<page-num>225</page-num>
<header>*Refutation.*</header>
<sig>K 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: ché</meta>

we bring to it all the study, all the meditation, and all the exactness that we can, preferring that our sermons smell of oil <note>A classical metaphor: "smelling of the lamp" means a work shows the effort of long nights of study.</note> rather than wine, and that our listeners leave the temple satisfied with good things, rather than burdened and disgusted by a tedious length and useless repetitions. The best preachings are not measured by the yardstick, but they are weighed on the scales. We do not want our discourses to smell of <term>Enthusiasm</term> <note>In the 17th century, "Enthusiasm" was a derogatory term for claiming direct, private inspiration from the Holy Spirit outside of scripture.</note> and Fanaticism, which this Innovator <note>Labadie</note> calls *the Anointing of the Holy Spirit* <note>original: "l’Onction du S. Esprit"</note>, but according to the rule of Scripture: *we speak as the Oracles of God*, 1 Peter 4:11. And although the *manner* in which we preach does not smell of the monk's cowl, the <term>quack</term> <note>original: "Orvietan"; a reference to a famous medical panacea sold by street performers, used here to mean charlatanism.</note>, or the theater, we nevertheless draw our words, like the Ulysses of **Homer**, from the depths of our chests and with all suitable dedication. We do not pretend to speak to the heart—there is only God who can do that; but we pray that He will be pleased to do so by His grace, while we speak to the ear of the people who listen to us. We do not put into our censers the "strange fire" of the Missionaries and the Jesuits, but we fill them as much as possible with the fire from the Altar of God. The **Calvins**, the **Farels**, the **Bezas**, and to descend lower, the **Du Moulins**, the **Rivets**, the **Spanheims**, nor today the **Daillés**, the **Drelincourts**, the **Turretins**, and the **Claudes** <note>A "who's who" of famous Reformed theologians from the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>, never preached...

# Modest / Refutation.

[con]dition; and the ancient Canons <term>Canon: a decree or law issued by a church council</term> did not allow Christian women to come to Communion without having their *Domino*, or their *Dominical* <note>original: "Dominical"; a white linen cloth or veil that women were historically required to use to cover their heads or hands when receiving the Eucharist.</note>, and, so to speak, without having dressed in their Sunday best. But Labadie’s goal has always been to make the wealthiest and most comfortable members odious to the poorest and most disadvantaged, in order to cultivate followers among the greatest number and prepare them to demand a new redistribution of wealth, and to seize it by force.

Just as imaginary are *the great abuses he fabricates in the administration of the Lord’s Supper*, perhaps because there is no elevation of the host <note>The ritual lifting of the consecrated bread for adoration, a practice central to the Catholic Mass but rejected by the Reformed tradition.</note>, as he had begun to introduce at Middelburg. Far from admitting *many children*—whether in age or in knowledge—on the contrary, we complain that fathers and mothers do not prepare their children earlier; we wish to oblige them, through the consideration that they are members of the Church, to a more modest and restrained youth. Furthermore, we admit no one who has not given an account of their Faith and does not know its foundations according to their capacity. No one is even received unless they promise to live honestly and religiously; we also exclude those who are convicted of public scandal. But we do not believe that the title of *vain* and *Worldly* should be extended so

<column-break/>

far as our Humorist <term>Humorist: in the 17th century, this referred to someone governed by their own peculiar whims, moods, or eccentricities rather than by reason or tradition.</term> does. He has set himself against all those to whom God has given more worldly goods than others, tearing them down with these odious names and inflaming the animosity of the people against them. But he should remember that, as on page 17 of his *Manifesto*, he claimed that Ministers and Elders could not be suspended or deposed from their offices except for crimes justly punishable by a Magistrate and which carry a mark of infamy in the world; likewise, one cannot exclude Christians from the Lord’s table except for similar faults, roughly equivalent to those for which, under the ancient Canons, public penances were imposed, according to the distinction then made between *crimes and sins* <note>referring to the distinction between "crimina" (public, scandalous offenses) and "peccata" (private, daily faults).</note>. But it is false that we admit anyone to the holy Supper *whose Doctrine and morals are unknown to us*. Although the Church does not claim knowledge of secret things, and it is reasonable to presume the best of everyone as long as there is no just reason to believe ill of them, Labadie is nevertheless annoyed that the Apostle St. Paul left it to each person *to examine themselves*, 1 Corinthians 11:28. He would very much like to re-establish the use of Auricular Confession <note>The practice of private, oral confession of sins to a priest.</note> and the Penitentiary Priest <term>Penitentiary Priest: an office in the early church and Roman Catholic tradition responsible for hearing confessions and imposing penance.</term> to subject the consciences of the people to the examination of his Confessors, and to force them to learn from them how to discern the body of the Lord.

It refers to the Catholic belief that a sacrament is effective simply by being performed, regardless of the recipient’s faith—a view rejected by the Reformed author.</note> (as they say that the herring barrel always smells of fish), he is mistaken in believing that the *fruit*, or the effect of the Holy Supper, should be to perfect the faithful in this life, or to lead them to Pelagian impeccability <term>Pelagianism: A 5th-century heresy that argued humans could achieve moral perfection through their own will without divine grace.</term>, which, according to his Pharisaical arrogance, he attributes to himself; for we only participate in it to console ourselves against the feeling of our imperfections and our sins, and to strengthen ourselves in the assurance of their remission through the death of JESUS CHRIST, which we proclaim there until he comes.

The other motive he proposes for banishing nuptial blessings from Ecclesiastical Assemblies—which have been in use at all times and which constitute one of the conditions required in the public courts for the validity and legitimacy of Marriage, as one of its principal solemnities—is because, according to him, a contract so honorable and so important could not be better solemnized than by the prayers and the Blessing of the Church. *It is because, in his account, they are accompanied by a display of vanity, in sumptuous clothes, in finery, and in other ornaments that are entirely worldly and Pagan*, which profane what *Scripture calls holy*. We do not disagree regarding the holiness of marriage in its origin, its institution, and its use; however, Labadie would have done the public a favor by marking the passage of *Scripture* where it is called *holy* <note>The author is likely being pedantic here, noting that while marriage is a divine institution, the specific word "holy" (sacrement) was a point of contention between Catholics and Protestants.</note>, and by showing us what he finds pagan in our wedding solemnities. For elsewhere, Scripture in a thousand places approves the use of both feasts and nuptial ornaments, in sumptuous clothes and other finery, for both the Groom and the Bride.

Furthermore, he does not consider that our Churches, as public places, serve not only a sacred use for exercises of piety, but also a civil use for the burial of the dead; and that the heirs of those who have their own tombs there, and who have been buried there, have the right to have monuments erected to their memory...

## Refutation

...monuments suitable to their condition, to have their coats of arms and the marks of their nobility hung there, and to have the places granted to them by public authority covered in mourning. The *images* and paintings that serve as the crest <note>original: "tymbre"; the helmet or decoration placed above the shield in a coat of arms.</note> of the coats of arms of illustrious and noble families, or which compose the body of them, cannot be stumbling blocks or traps of Idolatry and superstition to anyone, since they have no religious use, neither for history nor for worship. Perhaps Labadie, so occupied with rekindling devotion, would prefer our Churches to be filled with images of male and female Saints in the Roman <note>meaning Roman Catholic</note> fashion, to serve as books of devotion for the unlearned <note>original: "idiots"; in the 17th century, this term referred to the uneducated or laypeople who could not read.</note>. And who ever heard it said that the civil marks of mourning continued for *entire years* are contrary to Christian virtues and the Word of God? What is this new piety that wants to fight nature, stifle humanity, and make Christians more stupid and more insensitive than beasts? We may well weep for and regret our dead as being absent, without however mourning them as being lost. And although according to Saint Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian for both Catholics and Protestants.</note> the care of funerals, the condition of the burial, and the funeral pomp bring no help to the dead, they are nonetheless performed for the consolation of the living. And it is indeed to be ignorant of holy history not to know that the holiest of men have led great mourning over...
