Thus, one will not find that any complaint of *such things* has ever been brought to our Synod, as Labadie feigns, nor that it has refused to provide a *remedy, for fear of either deserting the Churches <note>original: "Temples"; the standard term for French Protestant church buildings.</note>, or irritating vanity and pride, preferring to retain it rather than cast it out, and at the very least to tolerate it rather than embitter it.* For although we admit that there are sometimes public evils that might be called similar to cankers <note>original: "chancres"; referring to persistent, spreading sores or ulcers.</note>, which become irritated and embittered against remedies instead of being cured by them, we nevertheless have reason to praise God that, among the human infirmities to which the Church Militant on earth is always exposed, Labadie has thus far been unable to reproach our Churches with anything of the kind.

Finally, he comes to the alleged *Corruption* of our *Government*, which is the third Head of his *Manifesto*. On this subject, we shall be briefer than on the preceding ones, because he does nothing here but rehash the same points; and even if his accusations in this regard were as true as they are false and ill-founded, they could in no way excuse—far less authorize—his Schism and make it legitimate. First, our Synod does not arrogate any authority to itself over individual Churches and their

<column-break/>

# Refutation.

Consistories <note>The local governing council of a Reformed church, composed of the minister and elders.</note> that he himself did not recognize as legitimate and approve, both in his own name and in that of his Consistory, in the agreement of Flushing <note>original: "Flessingue"; a city in the Netherlands where Labadie was previously established before his break with the church.</note>. The common Ecclesiastical Discipline of all the Churches of this country is express on this subject; there is no Church in our body that complains of our conduct, or that has not strongly approved of it against Labadie. And all his grand words of *Pope* and *Papal*, of *Rome*, *Vatican*, *Inquisition*, *Tyrannical*, *Imperial*, and *Monarchical*, serve as much to mark the symptoms of his fury as he is ridiculous in imagining that the Government of the Church ought to be *Christian Republican, and even in some fashion Ecclesiastically Democratic*. Who that reads him can understand him? To establish Democracy in the government of the Church, in the manner of the *Morelists and the Independents* <note>The "Independents" (Congregationalists) believed each local congregation should be autonomous, a view the Reformed Synods saw as a recipe for "confusion."</note>, is to introduce confusion there and to overturn the order that God has placed there by His word. There would not even be any absurdity in the conduct of the Synod if it were to deviate sometimes from its first resolutions by the establishment of second ones; for the same right that the National Synod has to change the articles of the common Discipline, when the public utility requires it, must be granted to the particular Synods for regulations that do not concern—

---

* In the year 1566, a man named Morel <note>Jean Morel, whose 1562 treatise argued that church authority should rest with the entire congregation rather than a council of elders.</note> wished to introduce the Democracy of the Independents into the Churches of France; he was refuted by a learned treatise printed at the same time by Robert Estienne <note>A famous scholar-printer in Geneva.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the "Modest Refutation" of Jean de Labadie. The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the authority of Synods over individual church consistories, specifically addressing the claim that Synods lack the power to govern.</meta>

# Modest

govern <note>Completing the word "gouvernent" from the previous page.</note> their Churches, no one is ignorant of those words of Saint Augustine: *that even previous plenary Councils are sometimes corrected by subsequent ones.* <note>original: "que les Conciles precedens même pleniers, se corrigent quelque-fois par les suivans." A reference to Augustine's *De Baptismo*, highlighting that church law is not immutable but can be improved.</note> However, Labadie proves nothing of what he asserts, neither regarding any change, nor any contradiction in our regulations and resolutions; much less that they are made without any foundation in the Word of God, or outside and against it. It is true that Synodal Regulations are binding *under penalty of Ecclesiastical Censures*, just as all legitimate bodies <term>Colleges|In this context, formal administrative or governing assemblies</term> that establish statutes have the right to bind those dependent upon them under suitable penalties. A regulation or a law that proposes no penalty is like a bell without a clapper that cannot ring, or a bee without a stinger that will never make honey or wax. And every assembly that possesses any image of jurisdiction has the right to say that it *judges*, that it *decides*, that it *orders*, that it *enjoins*, without any suspicion of Tyranny; the individual must yield to Unity and each member to the whole body. Likewise, Churches that believe themselves aggrieved by some *new Regulation* may provisionally refuse to submit to it, and request redress at the following Synod. *The Synod’s recourse to the secular arm* <note>The "secular arm" refers to the use of civil government power to enforce church discipline.</note> to be maintained in its authority against the turbulent, although our man is greatly angered by it, is nonetheless very legitimate, since the Sovereign Magistrate is the guardian of the

<column-break/>

# Refutation.

the two tables of the Law <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>, the Fosterer of the Church, the Protector of its Canons, and the Conservator of its orders. The practice of this is also very ancient in the Church of Christ; Paul of Samosata, a heresiarch <note>A leader of a heretical sect.</note>, refusing to obey the Synod of Antioch which had condemned him, the Emperor Aurelian—though a Pagan—at the request of the Orthodox, employed his authority to make him leave the Church. Ancient history is full of examples that good Christian Princes have given of this just care; and Labadie here imitates the seditious voice of his good ancestors, the Donatists <note>A 4th-century schismatic group in North Africa known for challenging state involvement in church matters.</note>, who said, according to Optatus: *What has the Emperor to do with the Church?* <note>original: "Quid Imperatori cum Ecclesia?"</note>

Second, Labadie falsely accuses our Synod of *Tyranny over Pastors*; he is the first to make this complaint, and he can give no other proof of it than himself—he who interprets being brought back to obedience to the Canons as oppression, and who pretends he is wronged when he is deprived of the means to tyrannize the whole Church and to bully everyone, like the one of whom it was said: *He who tyrannizes for no reason thinks himself insulted by all.* <note>original Greek: "Εἰκῇ τυραννεῖ πᾶσιν ὑβρίζεσθαι δοκεῖ." A Greek maxim suggesting that those who rule arbitrarily view any opposition as a personal slight.</note> There is nothing so just as to require the Pastors of the Walloon Churches <note>French-speaking Reformed churches in the Netherlands.</note> to *sign the Confession*: Labadie, in accepting the call of a Walloon Church, bound himself by that very act like all the others. And according to what the Acts say,

And we have noted in the proper place that the pretexts he alleged to exempt himself were groundless: furthermore, to accommodate his fastidious <note>The original "scrupules recerchés" suggests overly picky or manufactured excuses.</note> scruples, he was first offered the chance to sign our Latin Confession, according to the revision of the National Synod of Dort <note>The Synod of Dordrecht (1618–1619) established the theological standards for the Reformed Church in the Netherlands.</note>; but he would listen even less to that, because the Fanatic *Serrurier* <note>Pierre Serrarius (1600–1669), a mystic and millenarian who influenced Labadie. It is very strange that he rebels against this, and that having signed both the Confession and the Discipline of the Churches of France—as is the custom there—he refuses to sign anything of the sort in the United Provinces. This is not a Tyrannical oppression, but a respectable and pleasant duty for good and faithful Pastors to adhere to *Liturgies* and *formulas*, and it is only Independent troublemakers and proud Fanatics who refuse to observe them. # Refutation.

-futed above his so-called *Prophetic* Exercise, and shown that there were good grounds to condemn the undertaking and the book he composed about it. We do not forbid the freedom to write to those capable of it; but because a poorly written book containing dangerous doctrines, being distributed everywhere—since *the written word remains* <note>original: "la lettre écrite demeure," a reference to the Latin proverb *Litera scripta manet*.</note>—is much more harmful than several Sermons delivered to a single flock, the Discipline of the country (to which all the Flemish Brothers <term>Freres Flamens|The Dutch-speaking members of the Reformed Church in the Netherlands</term> are subject as well as we) does not permit Pastors to publish any book without it being seen and approved by their colleagues. Thus, Labadie reasons very poorly when he concludes from the freedom to preach, which all Ministers have, to the freedom to publish and print such books as they please without censorship or approval. To him who is permitted the greater, the lesser cannot be refused; but he who is granted the lesser has no right to do the greater. <note>The author is mocking Labadie's logic: Labadie argues that because he is trusted to preach (the "greater" responsibility), he should be trusted to publish without oversight (the "lesser"). The third list he makes of the *alleged abuses of our Government*, regarding the *absolute authority over entire Churches* that *our Synods* <note>A Synod is a regional or national council of church leaders (ministers and elders) who decide on matters of doctrine and administration.</note> arrogate to themselves, is of the same caliber as the previous ones. It is very reasonable that they take up <note>The original French "evoquent" refers to the legal right of a higher court to take a case away from a lower one.</note> the knowledge of important causes that trouble the Churches and that they cannot resolve by themselves. Thus, the Synods are within their rights, in the cases of Churches brought before them, to *nullify* and *reform* the sentences that the individual Consistories <note>A Consistory is the local governing body of a single Reformed congregation, made up of the pastor and elders.</note> had rendered there, as is practiced everywhere there is a subordination of inferior and smaller assemblies to superior and larger ones. And although there were never writings more formally *defamatory* than those which have so often come from the hand of *Labadie* and his small mind, he foolishly supposes that the *Complaints* that have sometimes been brought to the Synods against him and against those he had engaged in his faction should bear no other title than *Defamatory Libels*; and that justice has been denied to him, when in fact people simply refused to practice injustice and violence on his behalf. way of amicable settlement and Christian *amnesty*, do not wish to read or strictly examine the writings, the *Petitions*, and the *Acts* of the parties. Constantine the Great <note>The Roman Emperor who convened the first Council of Nicaea in 325 AD.</note> acted in this manner at the first Council of Nicaea, tearing up and throwing into the fire all the memoirs, all the petitions, and all the insulting writings that the Bishops had presented to him against one another. One may also sometimes condemn some *without hearing them*, when they allow themselves to be condemned by contempt <note>The legal term "contumace" refers to a failure to appear in court when summoned.</note>; but it is far from the case that our Synods have ever refused to hear *Labadie*; on the contrary, he always acted through *declinatory pleas* <note>A legal tactic used to challenge the jurisdiction of a court to avoid a trial.</note>, refusing to appear and absenting himself from the hearing. He and those of his faction were never found in the wrong unless justice required it, and their proceedings against the *Sieur du Moulin* <note>Likely Peter du Moulin the Younger (1601–1684), a prominent Reformed theologian who clashed with Labadie's radicalism.</note> had been so corrosive and so far removed from the rules of justice and charity—solely to satisfy the *Hatred* and extreme *Passion* of this hothead <note>The original "Emporté" suggests someone easily carried away by anger or zeal.</note>, who had been both judge and party in the matter—that it was necessary to annul them. From these general theses, he descends to his specific case, and makes a crime out of his suspension at the Synod of **Naerden**. He does not forget from the very beginning, so full of himself is he, to preach his own praises, finding no better **Apelles** than himself for the portrait of his **Alexander**. <note>Apelles was the famed court painter of Alexander the Great; the author implies Labadie is a narcissist who can only be praised by himself.</note> He alleges his age, which is **nearly sixty years**. name of his *Consistory* <note>The governing body of a local Reformed church, made up of the minister and elders.</note>, in order to more skillfully play the role of his passion; although it is certain that before this denunciation, there was perhaps no one in his Consistory who had seen this book, nor anyone, we dare to assert, who was capable of reading and understanding it. But he was condemned for having *falsely denounced* it, and for having *proven* nothing that could support the atrocity of his accusation. Despite being appointed as the *Examiner* along with others through an excessive indulgence, he failed to communicate with his Co-examiners and did not follow the orders that had been prescribed to him. For this, he was blamed—both **the book and its Author** <note>The author is Louis de Wolzogue, a theologian whose work "Of the August Word of God" was being scrutinized for heresy.</note> (the Synod did not use the word *calumniate*) were cleared of the accusations, both by the other Examiners and by a due and lawful knowledge of the case, through the uniform judgment of the entire Synod. And as he admits that *the terms of his Denunciation were too harsh and could have been milder*, it is of no use to claim that the Denounced party did not want anything altered after the injury was made and pronounced in open Synod; because the more atrocious the injury was, the greater the reparation he should have expected from it. It is quite remarkable that this fine Denouncer was so little prepared, and so poorly informed about what the book he was denouncing at Flushing <note>original: "Flissingue," the city of Vlissingen in the Netherlands.</note> actually contained, that instead of attaching to his denouncia-

<column-break/>

tion the specification of the passages and propositions of the book—which he respectively accused of **Socinianism**, **Pelagianism**, **semi-Pelagianism**, **Arminianism**, **Blasphemy**, profanity, and impiety <note>These terms refer to various theological "heresies" of the time. Socinianism denied the Trinity; Pelagianism argued humans could achieve salvation without divine grace; Arminianism was a controversial view on predestination that opposed strict Calvinism.</note>—to which, according to order and justice, he could have been obliged on the spot (because he who sets himself up as Denouncer and Accuser in a trial must have all his proofs ready), nothing could be drawn from him. Neither by the Denounced party, to whom he had taken it upon himself to send them shortly thereafter, nor by his Co-examiners, who were forced to guess what the Denunciation was aimed at until six months later at the Synod of Naarden. He was waiting, like Cardinal du Perron <note>Jacques Davy du Perron (1556–1618), a French cardinal known for his intellectual debates with Protestants; the author implies the accuser is acting with the same arrogance or delay tactics.</note> once did, for his manuscripts from Rome. Furthermore, all *the printed works in French, as well as in Flemish and Latin*, to which he now refers us, did not appear until after the matter was judged; it was, as they say, **mustard after dinner** <note>original: "de la moutarde apresdiner," a French idiom meaning "too little, too late."</note>. And the refusal he made to satisfy the sentence of the Synod on this point did not proceed from his conscience—he has none—nor from being *well assured and convinced that the Synod, as well as Wolzogue, was in error*; or that he took care not to *betray the truth*, for which he never had any care and which he does not understand; but from his insupportable pride in presuming much more of himself alone than of all the others together; and from what he thought he would find in this encounter,

with which to sustain the Schism <note>A formal split or division within a church body.</note> that he had planned for a long time, having a plausible pretext to break with us by accusing us of being heretics and promoters of heresies.

As for his public books against the Vlissingen agreement <note>Vlissingen (Flushing) was the site of a 1667 agreement intended to reconcile Labadie with the church; he later rejected it.</note>, it is distressing for us to have to follow him step by step through his empty repetitions. One will know the truth of the matter if they take the trouble to read articles 4, 5, and 6 of the Synod of Naarden, and at the same time, what can be said in response to his evasions.

Already, although he pretends that the books disapproved by the Synod were being printed, or perhaps finishing their printing, at *Amsterdam* at the time of the Vlissingen agreement, they were nevertheless neither *published* nor *distributed* by Labadie until after the agreement, by virtue of which he was bound in conscience to suppress them. What is more, neither in the article of his suspension, nor in any of the others from the Synod, is there any mention of *The Reformation of the Pastorate*, nor of the *Manual of Piety*; although he has the effrontery to say that his suspension was based on the printing of the latter, the title of which reeks enough of his time as a monk <note>Labadie was formerly a Jesuit priest before converting to Protestantism; his opponents frequently mocked his "monastic" tendencies.</note>, just as the title of the other is ridiculous—it being possible that Pastors may need to be Reformed, but not the *Pastorate* <note>The office of the ministry itself.</note>, which ought to be, when well-exercised, the instrument of the Reformation. Furthermore, the article of the agreement speaks generally of

---

<page-num>257</page-num>

*treatises on Theology of whatever subject matter*, it being quite unreasonable to want to exempt from this rule books on *Christian Doctrine and piety*; unless it is because Labadie’s theology departs significantly from piety and Christian doctrine. Besides, it would be quite easy to show in his *Manual* many erroneous, monastic, and fanatical propositions, contrary to the dogmatic truth of the Word of God: so much so that, in fact, *one only had to read all the said books to verify that they should not have been printed, and to send them to the saffron.* <note>original: "envoyer au saffran." A 17th-century idiom meaning to discard or treat as waste paper, often used by grocers to wrap spices like saffron.</note> In any case, regarding these books, Labadie yields <note>original: "donne les mains," an idiom meaning to concede or submit.</note>, claiming only *that the two small instances of distribution, carried out by his order after the agreement and shamelessly denied at the Synod by an infamous lie, are nothing but peccadilloes, which were not grounds for Pastoral suspension or matters for excommunication.* For he wants his own faults to be viewed through distancing-glasses, which diminish objects and make a fly out of an Elephant; but he only looks at the faults of others with a microscope to make an Elephant out of a fly. At the very least, he cannot deny that *his two small instances*, which are the least among the other just causes for his suspension and the deposition <note>Removal from office.</note> that followed it, have far more atrocity than all those he imputed to Mr. du Moulin <note>Pierre du Moulin the Younger (1601–1684), a colleague and rival of Labadie.</note>, his colleague.

colleague, when—by abusing the name and authority of his Consistory <note>A local administrative body of a Reformed church, made up of the minister and elders.</note>, of which he was the moving spirit—he acted against him through *pastoral suspension* and *excommunication*. For one cannot suspend a Pastor from the Holy Supper without thereby suspending him from his ministry, since the use of the Sacraments cannot be forbidden to him as long as he retains the right to administer them to others.

What he repeats yet again from his two treatises, *Ecclesiastical Power* and *Prophetic Exercise*, has already been refuted in its proper place. It is quite ridiculous to maintain *that the proposition censured in the first is not there in the terms and in the sense of the Synodal article*, since in this Manifesto he still strongly maintains it, holding it as one of the *theorems* of his Schism <note>A formal split or division within a church body.</note>. He marks the negative of it as one of our heresies that obliges him to leave us, and pretends that every Ecclesiastical order is purely human if it is not read formally in Scripture. Furthermore, he does not consider that Ecclesiastical orders are not all, nor always, matters of simple *propriety*, but often of necessity; and that even what was often only a matter of propriety and liberty *in the process of being formed* <note>original: "in fieri"</note>, requires *here and now* <note>original: "hic & nunc"</note>, as they say in the Schools, a necessary obligation *once established* <note>original: "in facto esse"</note>. Though it is granted to him that no order can be established in the Church that becomes a burden to Christia-

He, instead of asking to be heard, fled in contempt from the Synod for fear that he would be obliged to answer for them. his holding of himself in contempt is of the same quality as the rest: no one of sound judgment will ever believe that he had such an order from the Church of Middelburg, or that this was included in his *Instructions*. It would have been bringing to the Synod the draft of the Sentence it was meant to pronounce, just as the Canons to be established were brought from *Rome* to the *Council of Trent* <note>The Catholic Council (1545–1563) that defined Counter-Reformation dogma; the author is using a famous Protestant jab here.</note>, with the *Holy Spirit* who drafted them arriving in the postman’s mailbag; it would not be coming to the Synod to bring back what was judged most suitable for truth, Justice, and Peace. He could not depart from it quietly and by stealth, as he once did from Toulouse, without making himself a deserter of his commission and *in contempt* <note>original: "contumax," a legal term for one who willfully disobeys a court order or summons.</note> of the summons being made for him to return. He himself knows well that until then, not a single moment of time had been lost, and that he himself had delayed the processing of his Church’s business by the comic interludes of Menuret <note>likely an associate of Labadie known for disruptive behavior</note> and their stubborn legal bickering; that having refused to acquiesce to the report of the Commissioners established in the previous Synod to examine the book of Mr. Wolzogen <note>Ludwig Wolzogen, a theologian Labadie accused of heterodoxy.</note>, but instead demanding that the whole Company judge it, this had taken much time. Entire sessions had been given to hearing the case with his opposing party present. Thus, it cannot serve as an excuse that he had already stayed *eight or nine days* at Naerden, since it was in his own interest—if he had wished to remain in order and in union—to hear the report of the examiners regarding his *Herald* <note>original: "Herault," referring to Labadie's book "L'Hérault du Grand Roi Jésus" (The Herald of the Great King Jesus).</note> and to purge himself of the horrible errors that they offered to demonstrate there, and which were found in his other books.

To say that he only left after a *formal denial of justice to his Church, and a refusal to read its acts, and a written notification that he was obliged by said Church to withdraw for these two just reasons*; this is to remember very little that this written notification was not from him, but from his co-delegate alone. It is a pure fiction, insulting to the honor of the Church of Middelburg, to believe that it would have presumed such a *denial* and *refusal* from the Synod when sending its delegates there, and would have obliged them for that reason and in advance to withdraw. This alleged *denial* and this imaginary *refusal* are chimerical and maliciously invented to authorize his rebellion and excuse his contempt of authority; one need only read the second and third Articles of the Synod of Naerden to recognize their falsehood. 

He further alleges that *he was not notified in time to stay, and that he had already left the city when he was told something about it, and that he had reason to believe that the order to stay (hurried as it was and made by a single individual) was not from the Synod at all, but from that individual, before whom his co-delegate constrained him on behalf of the Church and by virtue of its order to depart.* But this is always to "cover oneself with a wet sack" <note>A French idiom meaning to use a weak, transparent excuse that fails to truly hide one’s fault.</note> and to hide in the water to take shelter from the rain. they had already summoned him to his lodgings to appear the following day at the Synod, in order to hear what had been decided regarding the book of Mr. Wolzogen and to satisfy the requirements thereof. He knew well enough that Mr. Saurin was a member of the Synod and one of the examiners of his *Herald* <note>Referring to Labadie's work *L'Hérault du Grand Roi Jésus* (The Herald of the Great King Jesus), which the Synod was reviewing for errors.</note>, who was to make the report in his presence; thus, he could not doubt that Saurin had the task of bringing him back (if it could be done) like a fugitive slave to his duty. This alleged compulsion by his co-delegate is nothing but a pure farce, unworthy of the gravity of a Pastor and an Elder. Labadie, as Pastor, was as much or more within his rights to compel his Elder to stay, as his Elder was to force the Pastor to leave. 

Even this "Pastor par excellence"—now justly "depastored" <note>Stripped of his ministerial office.</note> and returned to the guidance of the **great Pan of Arcadia** <note>A satirical reference to Pan, the Greek god of the wild and shepherds. The author is mocking Labadie's fall from a prestigious pulpit to being a "shepherd" of a fringe group in the wilderness.</note>—should have stayed out of honor and conscience, had he any, despite the Elder's wishes. He should have stayed to hear what faults were found in his writings and to justify himself, if he could, against the errors and heresies contained therein. He had no reason to ask Mr. Saurin for *remarks on his books*, since the report was to be made within the Synod, and he was obliged to respond to it on the spot, without claiming for that purpose *more time than the Synod could last*. Also, in renouncing all his other excuses, he frankly admits under a "N.B." <note>Latin: *nota bene*, meaning "note well" or "take notice."</note> that he withdrew so as not to be obliged to comply with what had been judged in the matter of the

*book and the Person* of Mr. Wolzogen, to which end he had been summoned to his lodgings on the eve of his departure by two Synodists <note>Delegates or members of the Synod.</note>. He should have begged them to grant him the liberty of his own sentiments regarding the book and the doctrine of the Accused <note>Referring to Wolzogen, whom Labadie had denounced.</note>, and been content to have seen him acquitted by the Synodal judgment without forcing him or his Church to make such a public reparation. 

But he preferred to break rather than bend, and he was careful not to let pass an opportunity so favorable, in his opinion, to authorize and complete his **Schism** <note>A formal split or division within a religious body.</note>, and to involve his Church in it. He could not have been ignorant that a proceeding so irregular and so defiant as his would infallibly draw upon him the censures and **canonical penalties** <note>Punishments or disciplinary actions dictated by church law.</note> that were decreed by the Synods.

Why then, things having passed in this manner, does he accuse the Synod either of *violence*—after it showed such support, patience, and moderation—or of *jealousy*—after he gave such ample cause for pity for his irregularities, rather than for envy? ...part of a true eloquence, and one sees how much he lacks it in everything he puts on paper. We have, and have always had among us, a good number of people who, under the blessing of God and by His grace, far surpass him in speaking well, in knowing well, and in doing well. Our **Company** <note>The "Company of Pastors," the governing body of the Reformed Church.</note> was right to conceive a just *resentment*—not because he urged the reformation of morals, for which each of us works as much as he can, but because he urged it more regarding external appearances and, indeed, regarding indifferent things, or at most the *minor trappings* <term>petite oye: literally "the giblets," a 17th-century idiom for the non-essential trimmings or accessories of a vice rather than its core.</term> of vices, rather than the whole body of their assembly. He focused more on satirical invectives against the Pastors—to maliciously disparage them among their flocks—than on serious censures of evil to oblige his listeners to *abhor it*, according to the exhortation of the Apostle in Romans 12:9, and *cling to what is good*. His aim was more to overturn our orders, to ruin our discipline, and to put everything into confusion (as he declares that his goal was *to reform the Synod through his preachings and his books*) than to recommend piety, justice, and sobriety to the people. Finally, he acted more through secret and malicious designs of *separation* than of correction and amendment. Thus, it was necessary to suspend him in his absence, since he absented himself and fled, just as one hangs a criminal in effigy who becomes a

fugitive to avoid being hanged in person. And besides the fact that he should have foreseen that this would happen to him according to the Canons of the Church—since he sinned out of pride and with a high hand against all its orders, and made it his task to admit none of them—great care was taken to make this known to him, to his **Consistory** <note>The local governing council of a Reformed church, made up of the pastor and elders.</note>, and to his **Church**. Even a "plank in this shipwreck" <note>A traditional metaphor for a means of recovery or a last chance for salvation.</note> was offered to him by means of the **Classis** <term>Classis: A regional governing body in the Reformed tradition, similar to a presbytery.</term>, providing him the means to prevent the effect of this suspension, if he had possessed the slightest inclination to remain within the order and union of our Churches.

And does he not have the gall to complain that the Synod of Naarden blackened him through *seven or eight of its articles, where it tried to wither him and render him infamous to the whole world, making them public and having them subsequently printed in two languages, as well as his Classis*? He, who in his *Apologetic Complaints*—as bloody as they are satirical against our Synod and several of its members, both living and dead—at the very time when he was being spared the most, had equally shown in his **Monastic style** <note>A jab at Labadie's past as a Jesuit priest; his critics often accused him of retaining a "monkish" or Catholic temperament.</note> both the proud vanity of his spirit, by immoderately praising himself on every page and every line (failing to remember the words of his Cato: *Fools do this, whom empty glory torments* <note>original Latin: "Hoc faciunt stulti quos gloria vexat inanis."</note>), and the poisoned malice of his heart against our orders, our persons, and our Synods.

...that he tears apart everywhere in a truly appalling way? Furthermore, My Lords the States of **Zeeland** <note>Zeeland is a maritime province in the southwestern Netherlands where Labadie was active.</note> were so scandalized by it that in the settlement of **Flushing** <note>Original: "Flessingue," a port city in Zeeland.</note>—which he only accepted in order to break it, just as the other **Cato** <note>A reference to Cato the Younger, who famously entered the theater only to walk out as a protest against the indecency of the floral games.</note> entered the Amphitheater only to leave it—they had insisted upon these three articles:

*Since the Synod and several members find themselves offended by various writings of Mr. de Labadie, published both under the name and by order of the Consistory and in his own private capacity, they shall be obliged to hold them as null, and to express their displeasure regarding them to the said Synod and members.*

However, regarding the present complaint about the articles of the **Synod of Naarden** <note>Naarden is a town in the Netherlands; the Walloon Synod met there in 1668 to address the Labadie controversy.</note>, their publication, and those of the **Classis** <term>Classis: A regional assembly of pastors and elders in the Reformed Church, holding authority over several local churches.</term> that followed them, he must learn that it is the crime that stains the man, and not the sentence that condemns him; that canonical punishments carry no mark of infamy; and that nothing has been published regarding our legitimate proceedings against him to inform the public except after a legion of booklets and *blue books* <note>Original: "blaew boexkens," a Dutch term for polemical or scandalous pamphlets, often bound in blue paper.</note>, which, after leaving the Synod of Naarden, he sent flying through the air like so many **Sibylline leaves** <note>In Roman myth, the Sibyl wrote prophecies on leaves that the wind would scatter; the author suggests Labadie's pamphlets are chaotic and destabilizing.</note>, to defame us and to disturb the union and peace of our Churches. He calls his crimes his "innocence," and what was justly opposed to them, "persecution" and "cruelty"; acting thus in the example of his **Jesuit** fathers, who placed in the **Catalogue of their Martyrs**—because the devil also

<column-break/>

has his own—**Garnet** and **Oldecorne** <note>Henry Garnet and Edward Oldcorne were English Jesuits executed for their involvement in the Gunpowder Plot of 1605.</note>, authors and approvers of the **Gunpowder Plot** <note>Original: "Fougade d’Angleterre," literally "the English explosion."</note>.

Now, since he and his **Consistory** <term>Consistory: The governing body of a local Reformed church, composed of the minister, elders, and deacons.</term> had recognized in the settlement of Flushing (Article 2) the legitimate authority of the Synods and Classes over the Consistories, he belatedly decided to declaim against the proceedings held by the **Classis of Middelburg** under the authority of the Synod, toward the Consistory there, which he had driven even to the fury of the \* **Circumcellions** in his detestable schism. This miserable man had so completely hoodwinked most of those who composed that Consistory that there is no conceivable violence or sedition they would not employ to maintain themselves in their schism with him. One only has to read the records of that Classis, and what is related in the *History of his Life* (which is published along with this present refutation of his Manifesto), to give those who love order and piety an extraordinary horror and a just indignation at their proceedings. It was therefore necessary that the Classis, after having endured evident dangers of stoning and massacre from these mutineers, should employ against them the razor and the...

\* The **Circumcellions**, otherwise called "Circuit-goers," were insane **Donatists** <note>A 4th-century schismatic Christian sect in North Africa known for their rigorism and occasional violence.</note> in Africa, so passionate for martyrdom that they forced those they met in the fields to kill them, under threat of being killed themselves.

the branding iron <note>The author uses the medical metaphor of "cautery" (burning tissue) to describe the painful but necessary spiritual punishment of excommunication.</note> of the latest censure, until—having come to their senses, as the wisest have already done—they seriously detested the pernicious schemes of this **firebrand** <note>Original: "Boute-feu," a term for an agitator or someone who literally "sets the fire."</note>, who had engaged them in an action so criminal before God and so hateful at all times in His Church. No doubt they now recognize, by Labadie's own *Manifesto*, the wrong they did in letting themselves be so easily carried away to *another Gospel*, <margin>Galatians 1:6</margin> and in embracing, along with his Schism, a religion other than the one they had always professed, and which had been constantly taught to them since the blessed establishment of the Reformation in this country; for now this standard-bearer of rebellion has lifted the mask and declares himself opposed to it on so many points. of this man against the honor of this **Classis** <term>Classis: A regional governing body in the Reformed Church made up of ministers and elders.</term>. obliged to obey: So true is it that these proceedings had nothing *Papal* about them, nothing of the methods of the *Inquisition*, and nothing that smacked of the era or the tyranny of the *Duke of Alba* <note>The Duke of Alba was a Spanish governor-general notorious for his "Council of Blood" and the brutal suppression of Protestants in the Netherlands during the 16th century.</note>.

One could not depose the current Consistory <note>The Consistory is the local governing body of a Reformed church, composed of the minister, elders, and deacons.</note> without creating a new one; and this was done by the voices and votes of the old Consistory—that is to say, of the Elders and Deacons who were then out of office. And the very fact that these newly chosen individuals from among the members of the Church accepted the call and were confirmed in it, at the agreed-upon time, without opposition from the flock, sufficiently proves that they are legitimate Elders and Deacons. They did not *usurp* the offices of their predecessors, since those predecessors had been discharged; the outrageous discourse that *Labadie* provides here merely highlights, through his passionate outbursts, that neither they, nor those who chose them, nor those who admitted, received, and approved them, belonged to his faction or his party.

As for *Pierre Yvon* <note>Pierre Yvon (1646–1707) was Jean de Labadie's most loyal disciple and eventually his successor as head of the Labadist sect.</note>, it is strange that *Labadie* is so liberal in giving him the title of Pastor, since he never received the imposition of hands, and is not consecrated as a Pastor to any Church. Though he was chosen for the church at *Middelburg* by *Labadie’s* faction, his Election was never approved by the Magistrate; but as the efforts and intrigues of his Master had prevailed in helping him, in the Election for the ordinary Ministry,

It was as if the Classis, representing the Synod and authorized by it, did not have as much power over a Proposant <term>Proposant: A candidate for the ministry who has completed his studies and is licensed to preach, but has not yet been fully ordained.</term> as Consistories have over the individual members of their Churches, whom they suspend from the Lord's Supper when, having caused some scandal, they refuse to come and answer for it before them. The Synod aggravated this first censure by declaring him unworthy and incapable of being employed in the holy Ministry, and he remains suspended from the Holy Supper *until, through a serious repentance, he has repaired the many scandals he has caused.* *Labadie* must have greatly inflated this balloon with his own breath, for despite the fact that Yvon has never been a Pastor and is nothing but a small, degraded Candidate, he was given the title of Pastor at *Veere*, a title he insolently took for himself when writing to My Lords of *Zeeland*. Regarding the other disciple of Labadie named *du Lignon*; of whom he also wished to speak in this *Manifesto*; the same Synod of Dordrecht <note>The Synod of Dordrecht (1618–1619) was a landmark gathering of the Reformed Church that established definitive Calvinist doctrines, though here the author likely refers to a more recent regional synod held in the same city.</note>, in Article 9, approved the proceedings of the Classis <note term="Classis">A regional governing body in the Reformed Church, composed of ministers and elders from a specific district.</note> of Middelburg against him. It may be that he carried honorable testimonies from the *Academies of Die*, *of Geneva*, and *of Heidelberg*. But it has been more than three years since, by his hot-headed, extravagant, factious, and purely Labadist conduct, he was synodically judged and declared incapable of any employment in the Churches of our body. Furthermore, just as the praises Labadie gives to these three disciples of his are excessive enough that they should not be believed without caution, so too are the insults he vomits against the Synod of Naerden and the Classis of Middelburg atrocious enough to deserve no credit. This is because our goal in this **Apology** for our Churches has been principally to treat general matters concerning **Doctrine** and **Discipline** attacked by the Schismatic; and because all the particulars of what happened in the Classis have been sufficiently published in **Flemish** and in **French**, both by the *Minutes* of the Classis itself and by the resolutions of the **Venerable Magistrate of Middelburg** and of **My Lords the States of Zeeland**, who praised the conduct, approved, and confirmed the entire proceeding, as well as by what has happened since.

at the Walloon Synod of Dordrecht, where everything that the Classis <note>A regional governing body in the Reformed Church, composed of ministers and elders.</note> had decreed against Labadie and his followers was not only approved, but strengthened with as much prudence as justice: Labadie having been absolutely deposed there, as accused and convicted of Schism, heresy, perfidy, infractions of Discipline, rebellion against the orders of the Church, and several scandals; his Yvon, his Menuret, and his du Lignon <note>Pierre Yvon, Pierre Menuret, and Theodor du Lignon were Labadie’s most loyal associates who helped lead his separatist community.</note> having been treated there as has already been mentioned, and Monsieur du Moulin <note>Cyrus du Moulin, a rival minister whom Labadie had attempted to discredit.</note> on the contrary restored to the honor of his Ministry at the very request of the Church of Middelburg, and thus recognized and declared its legitimate Pastor.

These wretched Sectarians, after their deposition, had tried to plant their stake at Terveere <note>The town of Veere in Zeeland, Netherlands.</note>, thinking to draw there the remnants of the Walloon Church of Middelburg, which they might dismember through their wicked practices, in order to build there the first Temple to their Idol. The Magistrates of Terveere had even allowed their ordinary prudence to be overtaken by political illusions, with which Labadie lured them, in the imaginary hope of attracting a great number of new citizens—wealthy, skilled, and powerful merchants—who would bring them abundance and trade. The pretext for this was plausible enough because of the proximity of these two cities; one could regain what the other might lose, just

Furthermore, this was nothing but pure imagination; for the "tail" of Labadie <note>A derogatory biblical allusion to Revelation 12:4, suggesting Labadie is like the dragon whose tail sweeps away a third of the stars.</note> was not capable of drawing a great number of stars after it; the anger of the Wallons, after the first outburst had passed, would easily cool; and there would be no lack of counter-politics in Middelburg to frustrate these designs and to blow up this mine. Likewise, our Schismatics, through the mask of their hypocrisy, had deceived the piety and religion of the Leaders of the Church that God gathers in that same city—though not all of them. # Modest

<page-num>280</page-num>
<header>Modeste</header>

...they will forgive us if, with all the respect due to them, we complain a little to them regarding both the ease with which they were brought to receive these people into their communion—considering the <term>Schismatic</term><note>A "Schism" refers to a formal split or division within a church body.</note> design they had to erect altar against altar there—and the honorary praises with which they crowned them during their shameful and infamous departure. The <term>Walloon Churches</term><note>French-speaking Protestant churches in the Netherlands, primarily made up of refugees from the Spanish Netherlands and France.</note> in this country, falling under the same National Synod as the Flemish ones, have the honor of being members alongside them and together compose but a single Ecclesiastical body, being united in Confession, Doctrine, and Discipline. 

Now, as it breaks the union of the Churches and sows the seeds of Schism among them when a particular Church or Synod chooses to receive into its communion someone who has been suspended from the Lord's Table by another Church of the same body, or to offer the hand of association by recognizing them as Pastors when they have been degraded and deposed from the Ministry by the Synod to which they belonged; these gentlemen of Terveere <note>The town of Veere in Zeeland.</note> cannot be excused for having (by the admission of these Sectarians, who were solemnly suspended from the Holy Supper by their Synod, and respectively deposed from the Ministry and declared incapable of exercising it) broken the bond of the <term>Communion of Saints</term> and violated, through this carelessness, not only all the Ancient Canons—which, to prevent such an...

---

<page-num>281</page-num>
<header>Refutation.</header>

...evil, had established among the Churches the use of <term>letters of dismissal</term><note>original: "lettres dimissionales, Canoniques, Systatiques & formées." These were various formal certificates required for a member or minister to move from one congregation to another with their standing intact.</note>, canonical, recommendatory, and formal, to which the common discipline of the Churches of this country conforms in Article 82; but they also violated all the rules of Christian charity. And as these gentlemen must have undoubtedly assumed, uncharitably, that we had acted with injustice and violence against those people, so too by attributing to themselves the authority to absolve those whom we had condemned, and to reinstate those whom we had deposed—even without hearing us and without knowledge of the case—they have arrogated to themselves a jurisdiction that God has not given them, nor has His Church; and they failed to remember Article 84 of the Discipline. 

Thus we can say to them that, just as there was great imprudence in so warmly receiving into their home and into their bosom these foxes, who would have eventually eaten their chickens if they had remained there longer, they have likewise spared justice and truth in the testimony they gave them upon their leaving. For this testimony to be *Veranum* <note>original: "Veranum." A likely play on words or a reference to a specific person or document; the author is punning on the Latin words for "truth."</note>, it is—subject to correction—neither *True* <note>original: "Verum"</note> nor *Truthful* <note>original: "Verax"</note>, since it gives Labadie and Yvon qualities they do not possess and praises they do not deserve. On the contrary, My Lords the States of Zeeland <note>The secular governing body of the province of Zeeland.</note> have banished them forever from their entire Province as turbulent people, seducers, and disturbers of the peace.

<summary>This text criticizes the ministers of Veere for welcoming Jean de Labadie and his followers after they had been officially deposed by the Walloon Synod, arguing that such actions violate church law and unity. The author notes that while the local ministers praised the group, the secular authorities of Zeeland ultimately banished them as dangerous agitators.</summary>

### Extract from the Minute Book of the Classis of Walcheren, November 21, 1669.

*The two Brother <term>Ministers</term><note>Referred to as "Predicanten" in the Dutch text; these were the ordained Reformed clergy of the town of Veere.</note> of Veere, cited according to the resolution of the Honorable Classis dated November 7 by the 4th Article, to give an account to the Honorable Assembly regarding a certain <term>attestation</term><note>A formal written testimony or letter of recommendation. In this context, it refers to a document supporting Jean de Labadie's character and teachings.</note> given to Jean de Labadie and Pierre Yvon; having appeared in the Classis, the Honorable President—after they had been asked to step outside—brought into consultation what should be proposed to them and what should be asked of them; whereupon the Honorable Classis saw fit to request a direct and sincere explanation from them on these four things:*

1. *How that attestation was managed; whether it was in the presence of all those who signed it, or by each one individually.*
2. *Whether the attestation was drafted or written by one of the two of them themselves.*
3. *Whether they signed it with such a heading and title.*
4. *Whether that attestation is the same as the one that is printed,*

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

---

<page-num>285</page-num>
<header>Refutation.</header>

*as the one they signed.*

*To the first, it was answered that it was presented to each of them individually, and thus signed.*

*To the second, that they both had no knowledge of who the person was who had formulated or written it.*

*To the third, that they had not seen the heading, nor did they know it.*

*To the fourth, that they could say nothing about it, as they do not have the original.*

*Whereupon, after a final consultation was held, it was seen fit that the following should be recorded in the Minutes:*

*Firstly, that the Honorable brothers who signed the aforementioned attestation roundly declare that they never saw that heading, and that the document was not given by them as an official Church document.*

*Secondly, that the Honorable Classis judges that attestation to have been given against all good order and with danger, with the request that the Mentioned Brothers be pleased to be more cautious in similar cases in the future.*

*Finally, regarding the content itself, that they do not recognize it as theirs exactly as it is printed.*

The two Brother <term>Ministers</term><note>The ordained Reformed clergy of a specific town.</note> of <term>Veere</term><note>A town in the province of Zeeland, historically known in French as Terveere.</note>, following the resolution of the Honorable <term>Classis</term><note>A regional governing body of the Reformed Church.</note> dated the seventh of November in Article 4, having been summoned to give an account to the Honorable Assembly regarding a certain <term>attestation</term><note>A formal written testimony or letter of recommendation used to verify a person's standing in the church.</note> given to **Jean de Labadie** and **Pierre Yvon**; having appeared before the Classis, the Honorable Moderator—after they had stepped aside to allow the company to deliberate—asked the Assembly what should be proposed to them and what should be asked of them in this matter. Whereupon the Honorable Classis found it appropriate that they should be asked for a clear and sincere declaration regarding these four things:

1. In what manner this attestation was drafted: whether in the presence of all those who signed it, or rather at the home of each one individually.
2. If the attestation was drafted or composed by one of them.
3. If they signed it with the same title under which it now appears.
4. If this attestation, in the way it is printed, is the same as the one they signed.

They replied to the first question, that the attestation had been proposed to each of them indivi-

...and let us examine a fifth article of <term>Walloon Synodal</term><note>The "Walloon Synod" was the governing body of the French-speaking Reformed churches in the Netherlands, primarily composed of refugees from the Spanish Netherlands and France.</note> corruption that he attributes to us in our conduct concerning the laws and orders that the Walloon Synod never ceases to impose every day upon the individual churches, even though it is these individual churches that compose the Walloon Synod, and which impose upon themselves the regulations decided therein, according as they wish to prevent, or fear, some disorder.

And in the specific details that this wretched man provides, it appears either that he is feeding his reader tall tales, or that he is fighting against what equity and reason establish. None of our regulations state that our churches may only choose <term>Walloon</term><note>In this context, "Walloon" refers to French-speaking ministers, often from the southern Netherlands or France.</note> Pastors, even though they ought to prefer—all gifts being equal—those of our own household over foreigners. But even that is very just, and is the constant practice of all the churches of the country, and it destroys the lie of his first proposition: that they cannot call foreigners, not because they have not come to be heard, but because they have not been heard in the calling church or by its deputies. 

The <term>vocation</term><note>The formal process by which a congregation "calls" a minister to serve them.</note> of Ministers is not like the marriage of Princes, to whom one shows only the portrait of those they are to marry. A Church must know the one it wishes to choose for its Pastor, and to know him, it must hear him, according to the words of a Pagan Philosopher: *Speak, and I shall know you.*<note>original: "Parle, & je te connoîtray." A French translation of the Latin "Loquere, ut te videam," a famous maxim attributed to Socrates.</note> And if the Church of Middel-

<sig>N 3</sig>

-burg had followed this rule in the calling of the seditious Labadie, it would have prevented the evils he has caused in the country, and the scandals he has given here. 

We also do not require that no foreigner be called unless he has *signed the Walloon Confession*; but we justly require that no one be admitted as a Pastor in our Churches who has not signed the Confession, which is that of the whole Country. For Labadie acts as a deceiver when he presupposes that the Walloon Confession is different from the Flemish one; and the Confession in the French language that we all sign, and which so many holy Martyrs have signed with their blood, has never been accused of error except by troublemakers and schismatics. 

It is also a matter of prudence and for the interest of the Churches of the country that students coming from abroad should not be preferred over those of our own household and those whom the churches support, unless *they have resided at least an entire year in these Provinces at their own expense*, so that there is the leisure and the means to know them, to see if they are worthy of public support or of being received into the rank of <term>Proposans</term><note>Candidates for the ministry who were permitted to preach "proposals" or trial sermons before being fully ordained.</note>. And in this, one does not simply believe the *Ministers* on *their word*, or on *their simple notes*, as is necessarily required for travel and food expenses—where one does not get receipts from carters, boatmen, nor inns—nonetheless, if any excess were found, the items would be struck from the account rather than passed.

What he adds, *that it appears from our Synodal Registers in how many points we burden the Churches and their members from day to day*, is nothing but a hyperbolic amplification of his previous lies. *The authority to cut off* the heterodox and the scandalous belongs no less to the Synod over the Consistories than to each Consistory over the individual members of the Church. And the Synod should no more wait for *the advice of the Churches* in which these rotten members are found before cutting them off—when they are submitted to it either by their offices or in the cases devolved to it—than the Consistories should ask for the *advice* of the whole body of their Churches to exercise <term>Discipline</term><note>Church discipline refers to the formal process of correcting or excommunicating members who violate moral or doctrinal standards.</note> against individuals who are under their inspection.

yet seen an example; nor can one draw a comparison from what happened in <term>Middelburg</term><note>A city in the Netherlands; the author refers to a specific historical instance of emergency political intervention in church governance there.</note> during an extreme necessity—one similar to, yet greater than, that which once obliged a great Prince to change the <term>Magistrates</term><note>Civil officials who often had a say in local church appointments in the 17th century.</note> of some towns in the country, with the protestation that this should not be taken as a precedent. The authority of the <term>Consistories</term><note>The governing body of a local Reformed church, made up of the minister and elders.</note> cannot waver without that of the <term>Synods</term><note>A regional or national assembly of ministers and elders.</note> falling as well; and the Synod only takes notice of causes referred to it by the Consistories. Thus, the jargon of <term>Labadie</term><note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674), the former Jesuit turned radical Protestant leader whom the author is refuting.</note> is ridiculous when he makes people fear that the Synods *will become the Pope and the Cardinals, or at the very least, Primates and Bishops, by arrogating power to themselves, even if they cannot possess the croziers or the revenues.* One might say to him on this matter, as was once said to <term>Diogenes</term><note>The Greek Cynic philosopher, famous for living in a tub and criticizing social hypocrisy.</note>, that he should begin with himself if he wishes to speak the truth. For, seeing himself rejected by the **Jesuits**, who did not find him to be their kind of man, he threw himself with a full heart into the party of the **Jansenists**<note>A Catholic theological movement emphasized original sin and divine grace, often at odds with the Jesuits.</note>, imagining that at the sound of his barrel he might fish for some <term>crozier</term><note>A hooked staff carried by a bishop as a symbol of pastoral office.</note> or some mitre. Seeing himself frustrated in this, after having passed through several other orders, he became a **Carmelite**, just as "despair makes the monk"; and being unable to remain there because he feared the *in pace*<note>A term for a monastic prison cell where monks were sometimes confined for life ("in peace").</note> for his extravagant behaviors, he fled to join us, hoping that he would obtain there, if not the name and the revenue, at least the credit and authority of a **Metropolitan** or a **Patriar-**

**-ch.** <note>Continuing the word "Patriarch" from the previous page.</note> It has been seen that he moved through this country with full sails; but his wings of wax melted under the rays of the Sun, and the more his ambition was immeasurable, the more remarkable his fall has been, and thus: *Icarus made the Icarian waters with his name.* <note>original: "Icarus Icarias nomine fecit aquas." He could not have finished better than by alleging, under the name of a *modern Author*, the libel of the late **Pierre Serrurier**, titled in Latin *Examen Synodorum* [Examination of Synods], published last year, written in favor of Labadie and addressed to our Synod. It is at the head of this booklet that he qualifies himself as *Minister of the Gospel of the Universal Church*, because he had no specific one, having been deposed from the Ministry for more than forty years for the fanatical errors of **Schwenckfeld**<note>Caspar Schwenckfeld (1489–1561), a radical reformer whose followers emphasized internal spiritual experience over outward church structures.</note> with which he was entirely imbued. And it seems that by his last will and testament—having died recently—he appointed our Schismatic [Labadie] as he was himself: communicating with no Church, and heir to this universal and ecumenical Ministry. In any case, Labadie could not have produced a more suitable witness in favor of **Independence**<note>The "Independent" or Congregationalist movement, which argued that each local church should be autonomous, rejecting the authority of Synods.</note> against the authority of the <term>Classes</term><note>Regional governing bodies in the Reformed church structure.</note> and Synods than a professed **Enthusiast**<note>In this period, "Enthusiast" was a derogatory term for those claiming direct divine inspiration outside of scripture or church order.</note>, a Schwenckfeldian heretic and **Chiliast**<note>A believer in the literal thousand-year earthly reign of Christ (Millenarianism).</note>, and his intimate companion in error—so much so that they may be called *a second self* <note>original: "Alter idem"</note> and *Arcadians both* <note>original: "Arcades ambo"; a literary reference suggesting they are two of a kind in their folly.</note>; all this to provide a suitable lid for his "pot-pourri." # Modest

<page-num>298</page-num>
<header>Modeste</header>
<meta>catchword: prehen-</meta>

linking Ecclesiastical power and its administration among us with the *Beast* and the Prostitute of the <term>Apocalypse</term><note>The Book of Revelation; the author refers to the apocalyptic imagery of the "Whore of Babylon" and the "Beast" often used by radicals to describe the institutional church.</note>, hides a mystery of iniquity deeper than a simple design to calumniate. For these heated <term>Chiliasts</term><note>Millenarians who believed Christ would return to establish a thousand-year kingdom on earth.</note>, waiting for the Kingdom to soon be granted to the saints and for the destructive sword to be given to them for the extirpation of everything in the world that is <term>Babylonianism</term><note>A term used by reformers to describe what they saw as the corrupt, "pagan" elements of the Roman Catholic or institutional church.</note> and Antichristianity, want to maliciously extend the branches and fibers of Antichristianity into all the Reformed Churches. They do this because these churches oppose their <term>Quakerism</term><note>In this 17th-century context, "Quakerism" was often used as a derogatory catch-all for radical, individualistic religious movements that rejected formal church hierarchy.</note>, so that having led their faction to the point of taking up arms against the legitimate powers, they might authorize the furious common people to begin the murder and pillage with us, crying out *against the Beast and the woman mounted upon her*. 

But God in His goodness will dissipate these bloody projects, and will finally overwhelm these <term>Zealots</term><note>Fanatical followers of a cause; here referring to the radical Labadists.</note> under the ruins of their <term>Fifth Monarchy</term><note>A 17th-century millenarian movement that believed the four world empires of Daniel's prophecy had passed and the "Fifth Monarchy" (Christ’s reign) was about to begin, often through violent upheaval.</note>. Meanwhile, we remain most heartily submissive to the Higher Powers and to our legitimate Sovereigns, and are always ready to verify that the Power God has given us—which is annexed to the honor of our offices, which we exercise with such liberty under their favorable protection and under their holy authority—is exercised only for edification. And since, thank God, they have been satisfied with it until now, we are so far from fearing that they would take it from us, that we would

---

<page-num>299</page-num>
<header>Refutation.</header>
<sig>N 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: natiste</meta>

fear to incur their just indignation if we applied ourselves to it more sluggishly and with less vigor than we have done in the affair of Labadie.

The conclusion and the tail of the Labadistic *Manifesto* is of the same cloth as its exordium and its head. We have, by the grace of God, sufficiently verified that our Ecclesiastical Body—that is to say, indeed, that of our Churches—was calumniously accused of *those three great corruptions* that Labadie falsely imputes to them as a pretext for the <term>Schism</term><note>A formal split or division within a church.</note> he had resolved to form in this country. But even if these corruptions were found in our body (which, by the grace of God, they are not), they in no way equal the causes our Ancestors had for protesting against the Pope and <term>Papism</term><note>A derogatory term for the Roman Catholic system and its doctrines.</note>, his Councils, his Inquisition, and his Hierarchy. 

If Labadie had possessed even the slightest tinge of the doctrine of Piety concerning the legitimate and just causes that one may or may not have for separating from the Ecclesiastical communion of others—as Monsieur <term>Gentman</term><note>Cornelis Gentman (1626–1696), a Dutch Reformed theologian.</note>, Pastor of Utrecht, has solidly and Christianly treated this matter in a small Flemish book titled, *Reflections on schism and separation from the Church*<note>Original Dutch title: "Bedenkinge wegens scheuringe en afscheidinge van de Kercke."</note>, published at the beginning of this year, and solemnly approved by Messrs. <term>Voetius</term><note>Gisbertus Voetius (1589–1676), a highly influential Dutch Reformed theologian and professor at Utrecht.</note>, <term>Essenius</term><note>Andreas Essenius (1618–1677), another prominent Utrecht theologian.</note>, and <term>Burmannus</term><note>Frans Burman (1628–1679), a theologian known for his attempts to reconcile Cartesian philosophy with Reformed theology.</note>, Professors of Theology at the Academy of Utrecht—he would have surely abstained from a proceeding so <term>Novatian</term><note>Followers of Novatian (3rd century), who advocated for a "pure" church by refusing to readmit those who had lapsed under persecution; here used to mean a schismatic rigorist.</note>, and so <term>Donatist</term><note>Followers of Donatus (4th century), who argued that the validity of sacraments depended on the moral purity of the minister; a classic label for schismatics who demand absolute perfection from the church.</note>.

The author defends the church's alliance with civil authorities and cites contemporary Dutch theologians to prove that Labadie's separation is illegitimate.</summary>

[...Dona]natist and as Mennonite as his own; he would have avoided not only the establishment of his <term>Parasynagogues</term><note>From the Greek *parasynagoge*; it refers to illegal or unofficial religious assemblies held in opposition to the established church.</note>, as Saint Basil calls them, and which Mr. Gentman names with a very fitting word, <term>*By-Kercken*</term><note>Dutch for "side-churches" or conventicles.</note>, just as concubines are called <term>*By-wijven*</term><note>Dutch for "side-wives" or mistresses.</note> to distinguish them from legitimate wives; but we can also say his conduct was entirely Schismatic, for which he must expect only the terrifying judgment of God, denounced by Saint Irenaeus against those like him more than 1,400 years ago in these terms, *Book 4, Against Heresies, Chapter 62.*

> *He will also judge those who create Schisms and who are cruel, having no love for God, and who consider their own utility rather than the unity of the Church; for very slight causes and upon all kinds of pretexts, they divide and cut into pieces the great and glorious body of Christ, and kill it as far as in them lies; speaking of peace and making war, straining at the gnat and swallowing the camel. For no such correction or reformation can be made by them that could equal the harm of their Schism.*

Instead of wanting to seduce the members of the Church of Middelburg and preach separation to them, he should have imitated the prudent moderation of Saint Chrysostom, who, though deposed from his Bishopric by two Councils and banished from Constantinople by pure violence and injustice, nevertheless exhorted the faithful to persevere in the communion of the Church that was

This is why, against his vain and frivolous protestations, we oppose our own in good conscience before God, being innocent of this premeditated Schism of Labadie, into which he ran through a concerted malice, being like <term>Simon the Sorcerer</term><note>Also known as Simon Magus, a figure from the New Testament (Acts 8) who attempted to buy spiritual power; he became a traditional symbol for heresy and spiritual pride.</note>, *in the gall of bitterness and the bond of iniquity; Acts 8:23.* We declare that he has undertaken this only to the ruin of the pure religion of the saving Faith in **JESUS CHRIST** and of the truly Evangelical Reformed and Christian doctrine that we profess. this Schismatic, who uses as a pretext *the purity, the authority & the clarity, and the other true qualities of Scripture*, which we have always very courageously defended against the <term>Socinians</term><note>A rationalist sect that denied the Trinity and the divinity of Christ, named after Lelio and Fausto Sozzini.</note> & the <term>Papists</term><note>A common, often derogatory, term used by Protestants for Roman Catholics.</note>, only to lead us indirectly back under the yoke of Roman traditions, or to subject us to the reveries of the <term>Enthusiasts</term><note>In this period, "Enthusiasts" referred to radical religious groups who claimed to receive direct, private revelations from the Holy Spirit, bypassing church authority.</note>; who alleges *sound & holy liberty of conscience*; which is nowhere greater than in this country, nor better taught & maintained than in our Churches, only to impose upon us the yoke of his superstitious & tyrannical fantasies; who feigns a desire not to *fall back into the Babylonian captivity*<note>A common Protestant metaphor for the perceived spiritual slavery of the Roman Catholic Church.</note>, from which God has so happily delivered us all, only to throw us, if he could, into *the anarchy & the confusion* of his so-called *Prophetic Exercises*, from which, as if from a Trojan horse, would soon emerge the ruin of the Church & the State, armed with seditions, factions, and fires. We pray with all our hearts that our most honored *Flemish Brothers*<note>original: "Freres Flamens"; referring to the Dutch-speaking Reformed congregations in the Netherlands.</note> consider that this cause is common to them as it is to us, in both Doctrine and Discipline, both of which this man attacks quite openly—just as the cause of predestination and grace was once common to us and them—so that it might please them to employ themselves with the same courage, to maintain the good cause and to extinguish this blaze, which threatens their Churches as much as it does ours. # CHRISTIAN
## DECLARATION
->& sincere, of Several Members of<-
## THE CHURCH
->of God & of JESUS CHRIST, etc.<-

<margin>Romans 1:9. Acts 16:19. Luke 24:45. John 9:24. John 12:43. Romans 1:18.</margin>
God — whom we serve in Spirit according to the Gospel of his Son — having by his grace opened our hearts to believe in his Word, and our Understanding to understand the Scriptures, as much as is necessary for our salvation, obliges us at last to give glory to his name; seeking not the glory of men, but his own, and not withholding his truth in injustice <note>The phrase "withholding truth in injustice" is a reference to Romans 1:18, suggesting that remaining silent about the Church's perceived failings would be a sin.</note>, which we do not take as a cause for shame. This is why we, Members of his Church, having clearly recognized the great decay and the Strange Corruption of the Synod called Walloon <note>The Walloon Synod was the governing body of French-speaking Protestant (Reformed) churches in the Netherlands.</note> in three significant heads <note>The "three heads" or "three parts" refer to specific grievances regarding doctrine, church discipline, and the "holiness" of the congregation.</note>, Declare that we cannot in good conscience maintain Union with it, nor communion with it, so long as it persists in these three significant Corruptions, does not repair the breaches it has made in three remarkable points or parts of the Church and the House of God, and does not reform its Errors.

indiscreet; [we ask you] not to take offense, or take as any kind of insult, that which we are about to reveal, being as much obliged to do so for their good as for our own, and doing so only to fulfill our duties and the Christian Evangelical Profession that we have always made and continue to make; to unburden our consciences before God and before men, and to bear witness to the truth, to justice, and to common Piety, as well as to justify our innocence and show that we do not act out of Passion.

After these Protestations and these Prayers, from which we promise ourselves a good Effect, at least among pious Souls, we shall have no difficulty in revealing these **three significant Corruptions of the so-called Walloon Synod**, beginning with the first, which is its **Corruption in Faith and in Doctrine**, which is visible to the eye.

<meta>This page continues a list of grievances against the Walloon Synod, arguing that the church body has overstepped its bounds by creating human-made laws that claim to bind a believer's conscience—a power the author argues belongs to God alone.</meta>

<page-num>320</page-num>

...union of men, and *not on the word of God*, to which the Author <note>Likely referring to Louis Wolzogen, the subject of this critique.</note> presupposes that they do not conform and have no relationship, as he explains himself clearly in several places and at great length; indeed, they are *without the said word and beyond it*. The said Synod nonetheless pretends that the conscience of the faithful is bound to it, and obliges them *under penalty of sin* to keep such purely human and civil orders and Regulations; *and that they indeed sin* if they do not observe them.

Now, who does not see, in the first place, that this opens the door to human traditions in Religion and to their observation *in conscience and under penalty of sin*? In the second place, that it favors the Roman error and its pretended authority to make Laws and commandments of the Church to be observed in conscience and under penalty of sin. In the third place, it burdens consciences and charges Souls with an unbearable yoke, aggravating the Gospel instead of lightening and softening it. In the fourth place, it multiplies sins and even creates unjust occasions for them. In the fifth place, it arrogates a Divine authority and the Empire of God over Souls, usurping a Tyrannical power over them and visibly oppressing them. In the sixth and last place, it debases Scripture, which alone has the right to bind the conscience of the faithful,

regard to *Doctrine* in *five Remarkable points*, which we could increase by several others if we wished to report them all, and in particular, to have regard for the *condemnation* it made of the book entitled *The Herald of the Great King Jesus, and of His spiritual and temporal Reign on earth in His saints and through His saints in the last times*, in which it claimed to find *errors*, without however marking any of them, and much less proving them; and for which it condemned the *Doctrine* in its *6th Article of Naarden* as *New and unheard of in the Churches, contrary to the holy Scriptures, and pernicious to Religion*; although on the one hand the said *Synod* did not dare to mark any *Proposition or Point* within it; and on the other hand, several *Professors and Pastors* have approved and do approve of this book, which the said *Synod* (as it says in its *Article*) condemns and suppresses based on the report of two or three *Examiners*, of whom Louis Wolzogen <note>Louis Wolzogen (1633–1690) was a prominent and controversial Reformed theologian; his involvement suggests a conflict between traditional orthodoxy and newer, perhaps more mystical or radical, interpretations.</note> is one, and indeed the one most followed.

All of this is evident either through *Entire Printed Treatises*, both old and new, which rightly complain about the *Walloon Synods*.
